Open the PDF guide

This guide explains the order-slot version of the paper by Austin Li. It gives each result in two passes:

  1. Part I gives the intuition in words and the derivation, with every algebra step written out. It uses no benchmark numbers;

  2. Part II puts the benchmark numbers on these results and reads the graphs of the strength path.

Orange rules give intuition. Dashed rules give panel notes. Heavy rules state results. Red rules mark common errors. A tag such as [paper (6)] or [paper Prop. 2] gives the equation or the result in the paper. The scripts in verify/ recompute the numerical checks from the primitives. They use exact fractions or high-precision arithmetic. Run them to reproduce the displayed values. Decimal displays are rounded. Use the formulas and unrounded inputs for strict inequalities. Section 14 prepares you for the panel. Appendix A explains how the numbers were checked, Appendix B lists the parameter vectors, and Appendix C maps the paper to this guide.

Part I

Words, intuition, and symbolic derivations

1The paper in brief

The question and the answer

Question. A takeover target has one bidder that is ready to bid (the incumbent). A second buyer (the challenger) must pay a cost to prepare a bid. Does a stronger incumbent keep the challenger out?

Standard answer. Weakly, yes. A stronger rival lowers what the challenger can expect to earn. Thus entry weakly falls (Fishman 1988; Hirshleifer and Png 1989). This is the deterrence effect.

This paper’s answer. Not always. The challenger watches the target’s stock price before it decides. A stronger incumbent can make that price more informative about the challenger’s quality class. If this information effect is large, a stronger incumbent attracts the challenger. The title says this: competition creates competition.

The mechanism as a chain

Under the class ranking, the auction splits the class value gap between two claims:

  • the acquisition claim: what the challenger keeps if it prepares, bids and wins;

  • the traded claim: what the auction pays to the target’s shareholders. The stock trades on this claim.

A stronger incumbent moves these two claims in opposite directions. It weakly lowers the challenger’s gross profit at every belief. It weakly raises the target-payoff spread \(\Delta_T=t_H-t_L\), the gap in expected target proceeds between a high-class and a low-class challenger. An outside investor knows the class and trades on the spread. The investor shares one order slot with liquidity traders, so no order reveals the class, and the investor keeps a residual advantage after every order. Figure 1 shows the chain.

The main result in words

Proposition 2 compares three economies that differ only in the strength of the incumbent: weak (\(r_0\)), strong (\(r_1\)) and very strong (\(r_2\)). The incumbent is uniform on \([0,r]\), so a larger \(r\) means a stronger incumbent. Table 1 gives the direction of each quantity.

The main result in directions [paper Tables 1 and 3]. Section 8.8 gives the benchmark values.
Weak (\(r_0\)) Strong (\(r_1\)) Very strong (\(r_2\))
Gross profit at the prior, \(B_r(1/2)\) below \(c_H\) lower lower still
Target-payoff spread, \(\Delta_T\) below \(k\) \(\rho\kappa\Delta_T\) above \(k\) larger
Preparation threshold, \(\tau(r)\) above \(1/2\) below \(M(1/2)\) just above \(M(1/2)\)
Top posterior, \(M(1/2)\) same same same
Investor orders, classes \(H\) and \(L\) \((0,0)\) \((1,-1)\) \((1,-1)\)
Price informative? no yes yes
High-cost preparation none after a purchase none
Entry \(\mathsf{E}\) \(\rho\) above \(\rho\) \(\rho\)
High-class ownership \(\mathsf{O}_H\) \(\pi\rho\) above \(\pi\rho\) \(\pi\rho\)

From \(r_0\) to \(r_1\), gross profit falls a little, while the spread rises many times over. The spread crosses the trading cost, so the investor starts to trade. Entry rises well above the floor \(\rho\). At \(r_2\) the price stays informative, but \(\tau(r_2)\) lies just above \(M(1/2)\). So even a purchase cannot recruit a high-cost challenger, and entry falls back to \(\rho\). Entry is non-monotone in incumbent strength.

Intuition: why entry rises while profit falls

Profit falls at every belief, but the belief that matters changes. At \(r_0\) the price says nothing, so the challenger decides at the prior \(1/2\), and \(B_{r_0}(1/2)<c_H\). At \(r_1\) a purchase moves the belief up to \(M(1/2)\), and \(B_{r_1}(M)\ge c_H\). The rise in the belief is larger than the fall in profit. A purchase occurs with probability \(b+\lambda/2\) at the prior \(1/2\), so entry is \(\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda/2)\).

Seven claims that you must be able to defend

Use this list to locate each proof.

  1. Two returns to information [paper Prop. 1]. A stronger incumbent weakly lowers each class’s gross profit. Under the class ranking, it weakly raises the target-payoff spread. The proof uses payment integrals and Tonelli’s theorem. Sections 3.5 and 3.6 state the exact scope.

  2. Posterior bounds [paper Lemma A.1; Cor. A.1]. Every posterior lies in \([m(\pi),M(\pi)]\), under every investor profile, including mixed profiles and wrong-signed orders. The zero order needs \(\beta\le1/3\). With \(g_H\ge g_L\), \(B(M(\pi))<c_H\) excludes high-cost preparation under every profile (Section 4).

  3. Price sufficiency [paper Lemma A.2]. If \(\Delta_T>0\), equal prices imply equal posteriors because entry is at least \(\rho>0\). If also \(g_H>g_L\), prices rise strictly with the posterior (Section 4.7).

  4. Global trading bounds [paper Lemma A.3]. Under every candidate, \(\rho\kappa(\pi)\Delta_T\le K_H,K_L\le\Delta_T\). These are gross returns of correctly signed nonzero orders. Zero earns zero; a wrong-signed order earns at most \(-k\) net. The trading-cost window (H2) therefore forces no trade at \(F_0\) and full orders at \(F_1\) (Sections 5 and 6).

  5. Information is the cause [paper Prop. A.3]. At fixed information, a stronger incumbent never raises entry. The theorem needs \(\mathcal L_i(\theta,R\mid j,S)=G_j\otimes F_i\) and a cost independent of all these variables. With full orders frozen, entry is the same at \(r_0\) and \(r_1\). With a hidden price, entry stays at the floor \(\rho\) at both strengths (Section 7).

  6. Coexistence [paper Prop. 3]. Under rules IM and C1, no informed trade and an informative equilibrium coexist exactly on \([r_A,r_N]\). Entry on the informative branch rises to \(r_F\), then stays constant until the entry ceiling. The proof uses algebra and two exact Sturm counts (Section 9).

  7. Research [paper Prop. 4]. The investor chooses presence at cost \((a/2)\lambda^2\). On the interior research branch, research, entry and high-class ownership rise with incumbent strength. Under C1, this branch coexists with no research (Section 10).

A 60-second version for the panel

Panel note

“A stronger rival lowers the challenger’s expected auction profit. I add a stock market before the challenger commits. Under the class ranking, stronger competition also widens the gap in target proceeds between the two classes. An informed investor trades on that gap. Liquidity orders keep each order from fully revealing the class. When the residual advantage covers the trading cost, informed trade starts. A purchase can then recruit a high-cost challenger that stays out at its prior. On an open set of primitives, entry rises with incumbent strength. The weak economy has one uninformative outcome; the strong economy has one informative outcome with more entry. At fixed information, entry cannot rise. The change in information therefore explains the reversal.”

How to read this guide

The guide has three parts.

  1. Part I, the argument (Sections 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 and 7). These chapters give the intuition and the general derivations. They use no benchmark numbers. Each chapter says only which way a quantity moves and which condition binds. Section 2 sets out the model. Section 3 derives every auction payoff. Section 4 derives what one order can reveal. Section 5 solves the investor’s problem. Section 6 proves the main result step by step. Section 7 shows that the change in information causes the rise in entry.

  2. Part II, the numbers and the graphs (Sections 8, 9, 10, 11, 12 and 13). Section 8 puts numbers on Part I at the benchmark. The graphs of the strength path in Sections 9 and 10 need these numbers. The remaining chapters cover robustness, welfare and sale terms, and empirical implications.

  3. Part III, the panel (Section 14): derivations to do from memory, likely questions with answers, and the points a referee will press.

Section A explains how the numbers were checked, Section B lists the parameter vectors, and Section C maps the paper to this guide.

On a first reading, read Part I without the numbers. Work in this order: the auction algebra first, because every later step uses \(\Delta_T\) and \(B(\mu)\); then the posterior bounds; then the trading bounds. With these three tools, the proof of Proposition 2 is a short sequence of inequalities. Then read Section 8 before the graphs.

Intuition: What changed from the Laplace version

One order slot replaces additive Laplace flow. Orders are \(\{-1,0,1\}\), and the low preparation cost is zero. The auction now allows value classes; the point masses remain the benchmark. The guide adds the full equilibrium path and costly research. Section 11.5 derives the exact comparison of posterior bounds.

Next: The model

2The model

The economic setting

The model fits a listed target in a decision interval [paper Sec. 2.1]. In this interval:

  • the public knows about the sale opportunity (for example, a disclosed approach, an announced strategic review or an open contest);

  • the stock still trades;

  • one buyer is prepared, and a further buyer can still come in.

Preparation means due diligence, financing, approvals and the use of evaluation capacity. The cost is sunk. A buyer that does not prepare cannot bid its expected value; it stays out of the sale. So the cost is an entry cost: payment reveals the buyer’s value and permits a bid. It is not the price of an optional report.

Incumbent strength is a distribution, not a bid. The public record can identify a strategic buyer with clear synergies. It can instead identify a financial buyer with high leverage. It need not show what either buyer would pay. The model varies the public distribution of the incumbent’s value and keeps the realized value private until the bid.

Players, values and information

Table 2 lists the players.

Players, primitives and information [paper Secs. 2.2-2.3].
Player Primitive What it knows when it acts
Incumbent Value \(R\sim F\), continuous on \([0,\bar r]\); benchmark \(F\) uniform on \([0,r]\). Already prepared, no further cost. Its own \(R\) (at the bid stage).
Challenger Class \(j\in\{H,L\}\) with \(\Pr(j=H)=\pi\); value \(\theta\sim G_j\) given the class; benchmark \(\theta=h\) in class \(H\) and \(\theta=\ell\) in class \(L\), \(\pi=1/2\). Cost \(C=0\) with probability \(\rho\), else \(c_H\). The price \(P\); then its own cost \(C\). Not \(j\), \(\theta\), \(X\) or \(R\). It learns \(\theta\) only if it prepares.
Investor Fills the slot with probability \(\lambda\). Order \(q\in\{-1,0,1\}\); cost \(k\) for a nonzero order. No position in the target, no control rights. The class \(j\) (not \(\theta\) in general; in the benchmark the class fixes \(\theta\)). Only the distribution of \(R\).
Liquidity trader Fills the slot with probability \(1-\lambda\). Sells, abstains or buys one unit with probabilities \(\beta\), \(1-2\beta\), \(\beta\); \(0<\beta\le1/3\). Nothing about the sale (it trades for reasons apart from the sale).
Market makers Competitive and risk neutral. Set \(P(x)=\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid X=x]\) and absorb the order. The order \(X\in\{-1,0,1\}\) only, not who placed it.
Seller Commits to a cash second-price auction with reserve \(p>0\). Moves first.

The classes satisfy the class ranking: \[G_H(y)\le G_L(y)\ \text{for every }y,\qquad G_H(p^-)=G_L(p^-)=0. \tag*{\textsf{\small[paper (1)]}}\] The first part is first-order stochastic dominance: class \(H\) has weakly higher values. The second part puts both class supports at or above the reserve, so every value meets it. The difference \(D(y)=G_L(y)-G_H(y)\ge0\) recurs below. In the benchmark, \(G_H\) is a point mass at \(h\) and \(G_L\) is a point mass at \(\ell\), so the class and the value coincide and \(D(y)=\mathbf{1}\{\ell\le y<h\}\).

The benchmark ordering is \[0<p<\ell<r<h. \tag*{\textsf{\small[paper (2)]}}\] Read it as follows:

  • \(p<\ell\): the reserve excludes neither class;

  • \(\ell<r\): the incumbent can beat a low-value challenger, with probability \((r-\ell)/r\);

  • \(r<h\): the incumbent never beats a high-value challenger.

The pair \((j,\theta)\), the incumbent’s value \(R\), the cost \(C\) and the draws of the order slot are mutually independent. So the cost carries no information about the class or the value, and the order carries no information about \(\theta\) beyond the class. The standalone value of the target is zero, and all values and costs are per target share. A common rescale of every value, the reserve and every cost rescales prices and payoffs; it leaves orders, beliefs, entry and ownership unchanged. So the guide can measure money in units of \(\ell\).

Panel note

“Why does the challenger not know its own class?” Value depends on facts about the target: its customers, its technology, the demand for its product. A specialist investor can know these facts better before diligence. A buyer knows its own integration plans, which are different facts about the same match. Diligence reveals \(\theta\). The benchmark gives the investor exact knowledge of the class and the buyer none, so that the mechanism is easy to see. Section 6.3 of the paper (Section 11.4 here) gives both sides private signals about different facts. In an example the buyer’s signal is more accurate than the investor’s, and the rise in entry needs both signals. Section 5 of the paper (Section 10 here) makes the investor’s information the result of costly research.

Timing

Three features matter most:

  1. The price forms before the challenger decides. So the price can change the decision.

  2. The market makers know this. They price the entry that each price will cause. This is the feedback from the financial market to a real decision.

  3. No one sees who filled the slot. A liquidity order can match the investor’s direction, so no order is conclusive about the class.

Payoffs and the trading stage

The investor’s profit.

If the investor fills the slot with order \(q\), its order is the whole order that market makers see. It trades at the price \(P(q)\) and earns \[q\,[V_T-P(q)]-k|q|. \tag*{\textsf{\small[paper (3)]}}\] Here \(V_T\) is the terminal payoff of one target share: what the auction pays per share, or zero if there is no sale. The cost \(k\) is a separate friction (an execution cost, a cost to carry the position, the cost to borrow shares for a short sale). It is not price impact. Price impact comes from the competitive rule \(P(x)=\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid X=x]\).

The slot parameters.

Define \[b=(1-\lambda)\beta,\qquad b_0=(1-\lambda)(1-2\beta),\qquad u=\lambda/b.\] Here \(b\) is the liquidity mass in each direction, and \(b_0\) is the liquidity mass at zero. The ratio \(u\) compares informed presence with liquidity in one direction. Sections 4.2 and 4.3 derive the bounds \[m(\pi)=\frac{\pi}{\pi+(1-\pi)(1+u)},\qquad M(\pi)=\frac{\pi(1+u)}{\pi(1+u)+1-\pi}.\tag*{\textsf{\small[paper (4)]}}\] A larger \(u\) moves the two bounds apart: each order says more about the class. At the prior \(1/2\) the two bounds are symmetric about \(1/2\). Section 8.4 gives the benchmark slot.

Expected payoff given the class and the order.

Let \(e(q)\) be preparation after the price \(P(q)\). Given the class, preparation is independent of both acquisition values. Thus the expected terminal payoff is \(t_0+e(q)(t_j-t_0)\), and the investor maximizes \[U_j(q)=q\,[t_0+e(q)(t_j-t_0)-P(q)]-k|q|.\] The ex ante payoff is \(\lambda U_j(q)\); this positive factor leaves the best order unchanged. Section 4.6 derives the conditional payoff from the two preparation outcomes.

The competitive price.

Average the terminal payoff over the class at posterior \(\mu(x)\): \[P(x)=t_0+e(x)\bigl[t_L-t_0+\Delta_T\mu(x)\bigr].\tag*{\textsf{\small[paper (5)]}}\] Section 4.6 gives each algebra step. Section 5.2 then derives the investor’s residual advantage after this price.

Intuition: Why the model uses these devices

  • Alternative arrival. Either trader fills one slot, as in Dow and Gorton (1997). Every class can reach every order through liquidity trade. Thus no order reveals the class with certainty.

  • The unit menu. Every order has positive probability under every profile. Bayes’ rule therefore applies without off-path beliefs.

  • The restriction \(\beta\le1/3\). It extends the posterior bound to zero and gives the universal ceiling. Propositions 2 and 3 need only \(\beta<1/2\).

  • The cost \(k>0\). A small residual advantage need not cover this cost. This permits no informed trade against a weak incumbent.

  • Zero preparation cost with probability \(\rho\). It supplies entry after every price. With \(\Delta_T>0\), this floor permits price inversion. It also supplies a lower bound for correctly signed nonzero orders. Only the high-cost challenger responds to the price.

Equilibrium

An equilibrium specifies orders, beliefs, prices, preparation, and bids [paper Sec. 2.4]. Each class can mix over the three orders, including wrong-signed orders. The market makers see the order; the challenger sees the price.

Formally [paper Def. A.1], an equilibrium is a tuple \(\mathcal E=(\sigma_H,\sigma_L,\mu,P,\bar\mu,\zeta)\) together with the bids. Three objects recur:

  • the liquidity order law \(\eta(-1)=\eta(1)=\beta\), \(\eta(0)=1-2\beta\);

  • the probability of the order \(x\) in class \(j\), \(f_j(x)=(1-\lambda)\eta(x)+\lambda\sigma_j(x)\);

  • entry after the order \(x\), \(e(x)=\rho+(1-\rho)\zeta(P(x))\), where \(\zeta(\bar P)\) is the probability that a high-cost challenger prepares after the price \(\bar P\).

The tuple satisfies six conditions. Read them one by one.

  1. Bayes consistency. \(\mu(x)=\pi f_H(x)/[\pi f_H(x)+(1-\pi)f_L(x)]\) for every order. The market makers see the order, not who placed it. Since \(f_j(x)\ge(1-\lambda)\eta(x)>0\), the formula is defined at every order.

  2. Challenger beliefs. The challenger sees the price, not the order. If several orders share one price, it pools them: \(\bar\mu(\bar P)\) is the posterior of class \(H\) given the event \(\{x:P(x)=\bar P\}\). Its odds are \(\pi\sum f_H(x)/[(1-\pi)\sum f_L(x)]\), with both sums over that event.

  3. Entry optimality. \(\zeta(\bar P)=1\) if \(B(\bar\mu(\bar P))>c_H\), and \(\zeta(\bar P)=0\) if \(B(\bar\mu(\bar P))<c_H\). At equality, the tie rule applies. The zero-cost challenger prepares after every price; its gross profit is an expected auction rent, which is never negative.

  4. Competitive prices. \(P(x)=\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid X=x]\). Market makers earn zero in expectation after each order. With (E1) and the independence of the cost, this condition gives [paper (5)].

  5. Investor optimality. \(\sigma_j(q)>0\) only if \(q\) maximizes \(U_j(q)\) over \(\{-1,0,1\}\), with \(P(\cdot)\) and \(e(\cdot)\) held at their values in \(\mathcal E\).

  6. Truthful bids. Each prepared bidder whose value meets the reserve bids its value. This is weakly dominant (Section 3.1).

The appendix of the paper uses two words. A candidate is a tuple that satisfies (E1)-(E4) and (E6) for some profile. An equilibrium is a candidate that also satisfies (E5). The uniqueness proofs derive bounds for every candidate before they verify the proposed tuple.

Caution: two different comparisons

  • A unilateral deviation by the investor changes only its own order. The price function \(P\) and the preparation rule \(\zeta\) stay fixed. The deviation payoff is \(U_j(q)\) read at the candidate’s \(P(q)\) and \(e(q)\). Use this to test whether a candidate is an equilibrium.

  • A comparison across economies (for example \(r_0\) against \(r_1\)) solves again for the prices and the preparation rule in each economy. Use this to compare outcomes.

Do not mix the two on the panel. A deviation never moves the price schedule. Because every order is on path, a deviation always reaches a price that the candidate already defines.

The default tie conventions.

Two indifferences can arise.

  • Rule IM (investor): an indifferent investor may mix over its best orders. The alternative rule I0 selects abstention when abstention ties with a nonzero order.

  • Rule C1 (challenger): an indifferent high-cost challenger prepares. The alternative rule CM lets it prepare with any probability.

IM and C1 are the defaults [paper Sec. 2.4]. Proposition 2 does not depend on them: every comparison in its proof is strict, so the unique outcome is the same under every tie rule. The tie rules matter on the coexistence path (Section 9). The threshold branch in the paper’s Figure 2 needs CM, except at its left endpoint \(r_A\), which also satisfies C1.

Notation that recurs

Table 3 collects the symbols that recur in the guide.

Notation.
Symbol Meaning
\(F\), \(G_j\) incumbent value law; value law of class \(j\in\{H,L\}\)
\(D(y)=G_L(y)-G_H(y)\) class difference at the value level \(y\); \(D\ge0\) under the class ranking
\(t(\theta)\), \(g(\theta)\) expected target proceeds and challenger gross profit at a known value \(\theta\)
\(t_0\) expected target proceeds if the challenger does not prepare
\(t_H\), \(t_L\) expected target proceeds if a class-\(H\) / class-\(L\) challenger prepares
\(g_H\), \(g_L\) expected gross profit of a prepared class-\(H\) / class-\(L\) challenger
\(\Delta_T=t_H-t_L\) target-payoff spread
\(w_j=t_j-t_0\), \(w(\mu)\) entry gain in class \(j\); its average \(t_L-t_0+\Delta_T\mu\) at belief \(\mu\)
\(\xi(R)\) expected class difference in proceeds at incumbent value \(R\); \(\Delta_T=\mathbb{E}_F[\xi(R)]\)
\(B(\mu)=g_L+\mu(g_H-g_L)\) gross profit at belief \(\mu\); \(B_F\), \(B_r\) when the incumbent matters
\(\tau=(c_H-g_L)/(g_H-g_L)\) preparation threshold, defined when \(g_H>g_L\): \(B(\mu)\ge c_H\) if and only if \(\mu\ge\tau\)
\(\lambda\), \(\beta\), \(\eta\) presence probability; liquidity probability; liquidity order law
\(b\), \(b_0\), \(u\) \((1-\lambda)\beta\); \((1-\lambda)(1-2\beta)\); \(\lambda/b\)
\(\sigma_j\), \(f_j(x)\) investor order law in class \(j\); probability of order \(x\) in class \(j\)
\(\mu(x)\), \(\bar\mu(\bar P)\) market makers’ posterior after order \(x\); challenger’s belief at price \(\bar P\)
\(m(\pi)\), \(M(\pi)\) bottom and top posterior [paper (4)]
\(\kappa(\pi)=\min\{m,1-M\}\) the factor in the trading bound \(\rho\,\kappa(\pi)\Delta_T\)
\(\zeta(\bar P)\), \(e(x)\) high-cost preparation probability; entry after order \(x\)
\(K_H\), \(K_L\) gross return of a purchase in class \(H\) / a sale in class \(L\)
\(\mathsf{E}\) entry: the probability that the challenger prepares
\(\mathsf{O}_H\) high-class ownership: the probability that a class-\(H\) challenger acquires the target
\(W_H(F)\) probability that a prepared class-\(H\) challenger wins against \(F\)
\(r_0,r_1,r_2\) weak, strong and very strong incumbent
\(r_C\) the entry ceiling: \(\tau(r_C)=M(1/2)\)
Previous: The paper in briefNext: The auction stage: who pays what

3The auction stage: who pays what

This section derives equations [paper (6)]-[paper (8)], Proposition 1, Proposition 1(c) and the surplus split. Everything here holds before trading and entry. It is pure auction algebra, and it does not depend on the order slot.

Rules of a second-price auction with reserve

  • A bid is admissible if it is at least \(p\). A bid equal to \(p\) is admissible.

  • The highest admissible bidder wins. There is no sale without an admissible bid.

  • The winner pays \(\max\{p,\text{highest competing admissible bid}\}\).

  • Truthful bids are weakly dominant. So each prepared bidder whose value meets the reserve bids its value: the incumbent bids \(R\) if \(R\ge p\), and a prepared challenger bids \(\theta\) if \(\theta\ge p\). A bidder whose value is below \(p\) does not bid.

Why truthful bids are weakly dominant

[paper App. A.1]. Fix a bidder with value \(v\). Let \(Y\ge0\) be the highest competing admissible bid, with \(Y=0\) if there is none. Any admissible bid that wins pays \(\max\{p,Y\}\). So a bid yields either \(v-\max\{p,Y\}\) (a win) or zero (a loss).

  1. Let \(v\ge p\), and consider the bid \(v\).

    • If \(v>\max\{p,Y\}\), the bid wins and earns \(v-\max\{p,Y\}>0\).

    • If \(v<\max\{p,Y\}\), then \(Y>v\), so the bid loses and earns zero.

    • If \(v=\max\{p,Y\}\), a win pays \(v\), so the bid earns zero.

    In each case the bid \(v\) earns \((v-\max\{p,Y\})_+\), the larger of the two possible payoffs.

  2. Let \(v<p\). Every bid that wins pays at least \(p>v\). So no bid earns more than zero, which is the payoff of no bid.

The argument holds at every \(Y\). So it needs no belief about the rival. None of the payoffs below depends on a conjecture about bid shading. This verifies condition (E6) for every tuple.

Case-by-case outcomes

Fix the incumbent’s value \(R\). Table 4 lists the winner and the payment in each case of the benchmark. Value ties have probability zero, because \(F\) is continuous.

Realized sale outcomes in the benchmark. A high-value challenger always wins because \(h>r\ge R\).
Challenger Region of \(R\) Winner Target receives
None \(R<p\) no sale \(0\)
None \(R\ge p\) incumbent \(p\)
High (\(\theta=h\)) \(R<p\) challenger \(p\)
High \(p\le R\le r\) challenger \(R\)
Low (\(\theta=\ell\)) \(R<p\) challenger \(p\)
Low \(p\le R<\ell\) challenger \(R\)
Low \(\ell<R\le r\) incumbent \(\ell\)

Compact forms of what the target receives: \[\text{no entry: } p\,\mathbf{1}\{R\ge p\},\qquad \text{high challenger: } \max\{p,R\},\qquad \text{low challenger: } \max\{p,\min(R,\ell)\}.\] For a general value \(\theta\) [paper Sec. 3.1]:

  • if \(\theta\ge p\), the target receives \(\max\{p,\min(R,\theta)\}\), and the challenger keeps \((\theta-\max\{p,R\})_+\);

  • if \(\theta<p\), the challenger cannot bid. The target receives \(p\,\mathbf{1}\{R\ge p\}\), as without entry, and the challenger keeps nothing.

Intuition: the one line that drives the paper

If \(R\le\ell\), both challenger values give the target the same payment, \(\max\{p,R\}\). If \(R>\ell\), a high-value challenger pays \(R\), but a low-value challenger loses, and the incumbent pays \(\ell\). So the gap in target proceeds is \[(\text{proceeds with }h)-(\text{proceeds with }\ell)=(R-\ell)_+ .\] A stronger incumbent puts more mass on large \(R\). This raises the gap. The same shift raises what a challenger must pay to win. This lowers its profit.

Derivation of the six payoff terms

\(R\) is uniform on \([0,r]\), so its density is \(1/r\). We use three facts: \[\int_a^b c\,dy=c(b-a),\qquad \int_a^b y\,dy=\frac{b^2-a^2}{2},\qquad \int_a^b(c-y)\,dy=\frac{(c-a)^2}{2}-\frac{(c-b)^2}{2}.\]

(a) No entry, \(t_0\).

\[t_0=\mathbb{E}\bigl[p\,\mathbf{1}\{R\ge p\}\bigr]=p\Pr(R\ge p)=p\cdot\frac{r-p}{r}=p\Bigl(1-\frac pr\Bigr).\]

(b) High challenger, \(t_H\).

The challenger always wins and pays \(\max\{p,R\}\). Split at \(R=p\): \[t_H=\frac1r\Bigl[\int_0^p p\,dy+\int_p^r y\,dy\Bigr]=\frac1r\Bigl[p^2+\frac{r^2-p^2}{2}\Bigr] =\frac1r\cdot\frac{r^2+p^2}{2}=\frac r2+\frac{p^2}{2r}.\]

(c) High challenger’s profit, \(g_H\).

It wins at every \(R\) and keeps \(h-\max\{p,R\}\). So \[g_H=\frac1r\int_0^r\bigl(h-\max\{p,y\}\bigr)\,dy=h-t_H=h-\frac r2-\frac{p^2}{2r}.\]

(d) Low challenger, \(t_L\).

Split at \(R=p\) and \(R=\ell\): \[\begin{align*} t_L&=\frac1r\Bigl[\int_0^p p\,dy+\int_p^\ell y\,dy+\int_\ell^r\ell\,dy\Bigr] =\frac1r\Bigl[p^2+\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2}+\ell(r-\ell)\Bigr]\\ &=\frac1r\Bigl[\ell r-\frac{\ell^2}{2}+\frac{p^2}{2}\Bigr]=\ell-\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2r}. \end{align*}\] The second line collects terms: \(p^2-p^2/2=p^2/2\), and \(\ell^2/2-\ell^2=-\ell^2/2\).

(e) Low challenger’s profit, \(g_L\).

It wins only if \(R<\ell\), and keeps \(\ell-\max\{p,R\}\): \[\begin{align*} g_L&=\frac1r\Bigl[\int_0^p(\ell-p)\,dy+\int_p^\ell(\ell-y)\,dy\Bigr] =\frac1r\Bigl[(\ell-p)p+\frac{(\ell-p)^2}{2}\Bigr]\\ &=\frac{(\ell-p)(2p+\ell-p)}{2r}=\frac{(\ell-p)(\ell+p)}{2r}=\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2r}. \end{align*}\] The second integral uses the third fact with \(c=\ell\), \(a=p\), \(b=\ell\).

(f) The spread, \(\Delta_T\).

Subtract (d) from (b): \[\Delta_T=t_H-t_L=\frac r2+\frac{p^2}{2r}-\ell+\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2r}=\frac r2-\ell+\frac{\ell^2}{2r} =\frac{r^2-2\ell r+\ell^2}{2r}=\frac{(r-\ell)^2}{2r}.\] The reserve \(p\) cancels. This is no accident: below \(\ell\) both values pay the same, so \(p\) moves both terms equally. A direct check: \(\Delta_T=\mathbb{E}[(R-\ell)_+]=\frac1r\int_\ell^r(y-\ell)\,dy=(r-\ell)^2/(2r)\).

Result: equation (6) of the paper

\[t_0=p\Bigl(1-\frac pr\Bigr),\qquad t_H=\frac r2+\frac{p^2}{2r},\qquad t_L=\ell-\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2r},\] \[g_H=h-\frac r2-\frac{p^2}{2r},\qquad g_L=\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2r},\qquad \Delta_T(r)=\frac{(r-\ell)^2}{2r}.\]

Panel note: three quick sanity checks

  • \(t_H+g_H=h\) and \(t_L+g_L=\ell\). Proof for \(\ell\): if \(R<\ell\), the challenger wins, and the payment plus its profit equals \(\ell\). If \(R>\ell\), the target gets \(\ell\) and the challenger gets \(0\). The sum is \(\ell\) in both cases. The incumbent’s profit \(R-\ell\) is outside this sum.

  • \(w_L=t_L-t_0>0\), because \(\max\{p,\min(R,\ell)\}\ge p\ge p\,\mathbf{1}\{R\ge p\}\), and the second inequality is strict on \(R<p\), an event of probability \(p/r>0\). So entry by either class raises proceeds.

Derivatives: equation (7)

Spread.

Use the quotient rule on \(\Delta_T=(r-\ell)^2/(2r)\): \[\Delta_T'(r)=\frac{2(r-\ell)\cdot2r-(r-\ell)^2\cdot2}{4r^2}=\frac{(r-\ell)\,[2r-(r-\ell)]}{2r^2} =\frac{(r-\ell)(r+\ell)}{2r^2}=\frac12-\frac{\ell^2}{2r^2}>0,\] since \(r>\ell\).

Profits.

\[g_H'(r)=-\frac12+\frac{p^2}{2r^2}<0\quad(\text{since }r>p),\qquad g_L'(r)=-\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2r^2}<0\quad(\text{since }\ell>p). \tag*{\textsf{\small[paper (7)]}}\]

Gross profit at a fixed belief.

\(B_r(\mu)=(1-\mu)g_L+\mu g_H\) is a weighted average with fixed weights. So \[\frac{\partial B_r(\mu)}{\partial r}=(1-\mu)g_L'(r)+\mu g_H'(r)<0\qquad\text{for every }\mu\in[0,1].\] Also \(B_r\) rises in \(\mu\), because \[g_H-g_L=h-\frac r2-\frac{p^2}{2r}-\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2r}=h-\frac r2-\frac{\ell^2}{2r} >r-\frac r2-\frac{\ell^2}{2r}=\frac{r^2-\ell^2}{2r}>0\qquad(\text{use }h>r>\ell).\]

The preparation threshold.

With \(g_H>g_L\), solve \(B_r(\mu)=c_H\): \[\begin{align*} g_L+\mu(g_H-g_L)=c_H &\iff \mu=\frac{c_H-g_L}{g_H-g_L}=:\tau(r),\\ c_H-g_L&=\frac{2rc_H-\ell^2+p^2}{2r},\qquad g_H-g_L=\frac{2rh-r^2-\ell^2}{2r}. \end{align*}\] Cancel the common denominator \(2r\): \[\begin{equation} \tag{3.1}\htmlId{eq:03-tau}{} \tau(r)=\frac{2rc_H-\ell^2+p^2}{2rh-r^2-\ell^2}. \end{equation}\] For the derivative, write \(N_\tau=2rc_H-\ell^2+p^2\) and \(D_\tau=2rh-r^2-\ell^2>0\). The quotient rule gives \[\begin{align*} N_\tau'D_\tau-N_\tau D_\tau' &=2c_H(2rh-r^2-\ell^2)-(2rc_H-\ell^2+p^2)(2h-2r)\\ &=2c_Hr^2-2(\ell^2-p^2)r+2h(\ell^2-p^2)-2c_H\ell^2. \end{align*}\] Regroup the numerator. Then \[\begin{equation} \tag{3.2}\htmlId{eq:03-taup}{} \tau'(r)=\frac{2c_H(r^2-\ell^2)+2(h-r)(\ell^2-p^2)}{(2rh-r^2-\ell^2)^2}>0. \end{equation}\] Both terms of the numerator are positive because \(c_H>0\) and \(0<p<\ell<r<h\). Thus \(\tau\) rises throughout this domain, even if the threshold lies outside \([0,1]\). In the benchmark, the threshold already lies above the prior \(1/2\) at the weakest incumbent of the path. Because \(\tau\) rises, an uninformative price never recruits a high-cost challenger along the benchmark path. Section 8.2 gives the benchmark form of \(\tau\) and its derivative.

Result: The two effects

A stronger uniform incumbent raises \(\Delta_T\) and lowers gross profit at every fixed belief. It also raises \(\tau\) in this benchmark domain. The top posterior must clear this higher threshold before a purchase recruits.

The size of the two effects.

Compare the weak and the strong economies of Proposition 2. The spread rises many times over, but gross profit at the prior falls only a little. The threshold rises, as (3.2) requires. So the information channel gains much more than the direct channel loses. Section 8.2 gives the benchmark values (Table 1 of the paper).

Proposition 1: the result for any incumbent distribution

Result: Proposition 1 (competition and the two returns to information)

Let \(R\) have a continuous distribution \(F\) on \([0,\bar r]\), with \(F(y)=1\) for \(y>\bar r\). For any classes \(G_H\), \(G_L\) with finite means, \[\Delta_T(F)=pF(p)D(p^-)+\int_p^\infty[1-F(y)]\,D(y)\,dy,\qquad g_j(F)=\int_p^\infty[1-G_j(y)]\,F(y)\,dy. \tag*{\textsf{\small[paper (8)]}}\] Let \(\tilde F\le F\) be a continuous first-order stochastic strengthening of \(F\).

  1. For every pair of classes, the strengthening weakly lowers \(g_H\) and \(g_L\), and hence gross profit at every fixed belief.

  2. Under the class ranking [paper (1)], the strengthening weakly raises \(\Delta_T\).

The decrease in \(g_j\) is strict when \(\int_p^\infty[1-G_j(y)][F(y)-\tilde F(y)]\,dy>0\). The increase in (b) is strict when \(\int_p^\infty[F(y)-\tilde F(y)]\,D(y)\,dy>0\).

Step 1: write each payment as an integral of an indicator

[paper (A.2)]. For \(\theta\ge p\), \[\max\{p,\min(R,\theta)\}=p+\int_p^\theta\mathbf{1}\{R>y\}\,dy,\qquad (\theta-\max\{p,R\})_+=\int_p^\theta\mathbf{1}\{R<y\}\,dy.\] Check both identities case by case.

  • \(R\le p\). Every \(y\in(p,\theta)\) exceeds \(R\). The first integral is \(0\), so the right side is \(p\); the left side is \(\max\{p,\min(R,\theta)\}=p\). The second integral is \(\theta-p\); the left side is \(\theta-\max\{p,R\}=\theta-p\).

  • \(p<R<\theta\). The first indicator is \(1\) on \((p,R)\), so the right side is \(p+(R-p)=R\); the left side is \(\min(R,\theta)=R\). The second indicator is \(1\) on \((R,\theta)\), so the integral is \(\theta-R\); the left side is \(\theta-R\).

  • \(R\ge\theta\). The first indicator is \(1\) on all of \((p,\theta)\), so the right side is \(\theta\); the left side is \(\min(R,\theta)=\theta\). The second integral is \(0\); the left side is \((\theta-R)_+=0\).

The two indicators add to one except at \(y=R\). Thus target proceeds plus challenger profit equal \(p+(\theta-p)=\theta\) at every \(R\): \(t(\theta)+g(\theta)=\theta\) for \(\theta\ge p\).

Step 2: take the expectation over \(R\).

Each integrand is nonnegative and jointly measurable in \((R,y)\). So Tonelli’s theorem lets us move the expectation inside the integral. Since \(F\) is continuous, \(\Pr(R=y)=0\), so \(\Pr(R>y)=1-F(y)\) and \(\Pr(R<y)=F(y)\). For \(\theta\ge p\): \[t(\theta)=p+\int_p^\theta[1-F(y)]\,dy,\qquad g(\theta)=\int_p^\theta F(y)\,dy.\] For \(\theta<p\): \(t(\theta)=t_0=p[1-F(p)]\) and \(g(\theta)=0\).

Step 3: one formula for every \(\theta\).

At \(\theta=p\), \(t\) jumps by \(pF(p)\). With probability \(F(p)\), the incumbent fails the reserve. An admissible challenger then buys at \(p\); an inadmissible one leaves the target unsold. Since \(t_0+pF(p)=p[1-F(p)]+pF(p)=p\), \[t(\theta)=t_0+pF(p)\,\mathbf{1}\{\theta\ge p\}+\int_p^\infty\mathbf{1}\{y\le\theta\}[1-F(y)]\,dy,\qquad g(\theta)=\int_p^\infty\mathbf{1}\{y\le\theta\}F(y)\,dy.\]

Step 4: take the expectation over the class.

Apply Tonelli’s theorem again, with \(\mathbb{E}_j\mathbf{1}\{\theta\ge p\}=1-G_j(p^-)\) and \(\Pr_j(\theta\ge y)=1-G_j(y^-)\). The functions \(G_j(y^-)\) and \(G_j(y)\) differ only at the atoms of \(G_j\), which are countably many, a set of Lebesgue measure zero. So \(G_j(y)\) can replace \(G_j(y^-)\) inside the integral: \[t_j=t_0+pF(p)\,[1-G_j(p^-)]+\int_p^\infty[1-G_j(y)][1-F(y)]\,dy,\qquad g_j=\int_p^\infty[1-G_j(y)]\,F(y)\,dy.\] Each integral is finite, since \(0\le\int_p^\infty[1-G_j(y)]\,dy\le\mathbb{E}_j\theta<\infty\) and \(0\le F\le1\).

Step 5: the spread.

Subtract the class-\(L\) formula from the class-\(H\) formula. The term \(t_0\) cancels. Since \([1-G_H]-[1-G_L]=G_L-G_H=D\), \[\begin{align*} \Delta_T=t_H-t_L&=pF(p)\bigl[G_L(p^-)-G_H(p^-)\bigr]+\int_p^\infty D(y)[1-F(y)]\,dy\\ &=pF(p)D(p^-)+\int_p^\infty[1-F(y)]\,D(y)\,dy . \end{align*}\]

Step 6: part (a).

In \(g_j\), the weight \(1-G_j\) on \(F\) is nonnegative. A pointwise fall from \(F\) to \(\tilde F\) lowers \(g_j\) by \[g_j(F)-g_j(\tilde F)=\int_p^\infty[1-G_j(y)]\,[F(y)-\tilde F(y)]\,dy\ge0,\] strictly when this integral is positive. Gross profit at a fixed belief \(\mu\) is \((1-\mu)g_L+\mu g_H\), an average with fixed weights, so it falls as well.

Step 7: part (b).

The difference of the spread formula at the two distributions is \[\Delta_T(\tilde F)-\Delta_T(F)=\int_p^\infty[F(y)-\tilde F(y)]\,D(y)\,dy-p\,[F(p)-\tilde F(p)]\,D(p^-).\] To see this, write \([1-\tilde F]-[1-F]=F-\tilde F\) inside the integral, and \(p\tilde F(p)D(p^-)-pF(p)D(p^-)=-p[F(p)-\tilde F(p)]D(p^-)\). Under the class ranking, \(D(p^-)=0-0=0\), so the second term is zero. Also \(D(y)\ge0\) and \(F(y)-\tilde F(y)\ge0\) for every \(y\), so the integrand is nonnegative. The spread rises, strictly when the integral is positive. \(\square\)

Check with the point masses.

For point masses with \(p<\ell\), \(G_L(y)=\mathbf{1}\{y\ge\ell\}\) and \(G_H(y)=\mathbf{1}\{y\ge h\}\), so \(D(y)=\mathbf{1}\{\ell\le y<h\}\) and \(D(p^-)=0\). With \(F(y)=y/r\) on \([0,r]\) and \(r<h\): \[\begin{align*} \Delta_T&=\int_\ell^h[1-F(y)]\,dy=\int_\ell^r\Bigl(1-\frac yr\Bigr)dy=(r-\ell)-\frac{r^2-\ell^2}{2r} =\frac{(r-\ell)(2r-r-\ell)}{2r}=\frac{(r-\ell)^2}{2r},\\ g_L&=\int_p^\ell\frac yr\,dy=\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2r},\qquad g_H=\int_p^r\frac yr\,dy+\int_r^h1\,dy=\frac{r^2-p^2}{2r}+h-r=h-\frac r2-\frac{p^2}{2r}. \end{align*}\] All three match [paper (6)]. Section 8.2 also checks [paper (8)] for a non-uniform incumbent and classes with an atom.

Intuition: slices of the value gap

Read the integrand of \(\Delta_T\) as slices \(D(y)\,dy\) of the class value gap at each level \(y>p\). The slice at level \(y\) reaches the target when \(R>y\): the incumbent outbids the challenger at that level and pays its bid, or the challenger pays at least \(y\). The slice stays with the challenger when \(R<y\). A stronger incumbent raises \(\Pr(R>y)\) at every \(y\). So it moves slices from the challenger’s profit to target proceeds: the spread rises while gross profit falls.

Caution: scope of Proposition 1

The kernel belongs to the cash second-price rule with reserve: an incumbent that wins pays the loser’s bid. Section 7.2 of the paper (Section 12 here) changes the payment rule to Nash bargaining with seller weight \(\omega\). The effect of competition on the spread then has the sign of \(1-2\omega\). The zero-reserve second-price auction is the case \(\omega=0\).

Proposition 1(c): the weakest restriction

Result: Proposition 1(c)

Let \(G_H\) and \(G_L\) have finite means. The spread weakly rises under every first-order stochastic strengthening of every continuous \(F\) if and only if \(D(y)\ge0\) for every \(y>p\) and \(D(p^-)\le0\). When neither class has an atom at \(p\), this condition is exactly first-order dominance above the reserve together with equal admissibility, \(G_H(p^-)=G_L(p^-)\).

Step 1: the kernel.

Fix \(R\) and compute the expected class difference in proceeds.

  • \(R<p\). The incumbent cannot bid. An admissible challenger buys at \(p\); an inadmissible one leaves the target unsold. The target receives \(p\,\mathbf{1}\{\theta\ge p\}\), with mean \(p[1-G_j(p^-)]\) in class \(j\). The class difference is \(p[G_L(p^-)-G_H(p^-)]=pD(p^-)\).

  • \(R\ge p\). The target receives \(\min\{R,\max\{\theta,p\}\}\). If \(\theta\ge p\), both bidders bid at least \(p\), and the target gets \(\min(R,\theta)\). If \(\theta<p\), the incumbent buys alone at \(p=\min\{R,p\}\). With \(Z=\max\{\theta,p\}\ge p\), the indicator form gives \(\min\{R,Z\}=p+\int_p^R\mathbf{1}\{Z>y\}\,dy\). For \(y>p\), \(Z>y\) exactly when \(\theta>y\). So \(\mathbb{E}_j[\min\{R,Z\}]=p+\int_p^R[1-G_j(y)]\,dy\), and the class difference is \(\int_p^RD(y)\,dy\).

So \[\xi(R)=\begin{cases} pD(p^-), & R<p,\\[2pt] \int_p^R D(y)\,dy, & R\ge p,\end{cases} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \Delta_T(F)=\mathbb{E}_F[\xi(R)].\]

Step 2: when is \(\xi\) nondecreasing?

The kernel is bounded, since \(\int_p^\infty|D(y)|\,dy\le\mathbb{E}_H\theta+\mathbb{E}_L\theta<\infty\). It is constant below \(p\). On \([p,\infty)\) it is continuous, and its derivative is \(D\) almost everywhere. At \(p\) it moves from \(pD(p^-)\) to \(\xi(p)=0\). So \(\xi\) is nondecreasing if and only if

  • \(D\ge0\) almost everywhere above \(p\) (no fall on \([p,\infty)\)), and

  • \(pD(p^-)\le0\), that is \(D(p^-)\le0\) (no fall at the step).

Step 3: “if”.

If \(\xi\) is nondecreasing, every first-order strengthening raises \(\mathbb{E}_F[\xi(R)]\). One line: with the quantile coupling, \(\tilde R=\tilde F^{-1}(U)\ge F^{-1}(U)=R\) for a uniform \(U\), so \(\xi(\tilde R)\ge\xi(R)\) at every \(U\).

Step 4: “only if”.

Suppose \(\xi\) is not nondecreasing. Then there are values \(0<R_1<R_2\) with \(\xi(R_1)>\xi(R_2)\). Continuity on each side of \(p\) lets us pick both different from \(p\). Let \(F\) be uniform on \([R_1-\delta,R_1+\delta]\), and let \(\tilde F\) be the same law shifted to \(R_2\). For \(0<\delta<\min\{R_1,(R_2-R_1)/2,|R_1-p|,|R_2-p|\}\), \(\tilde F\le F\) at every point: \(\tilde F>0\) only above \(R_2-\delta\), where \(F=1\). As \(\delta\to0\), \(\Delta_T(\tilde F)-\Delta_T(F)\to\xi(R_2)-\xi(R_1)<0\). So some strengthening lowers the spread. If the support must start at zero, mix the same small uniform component on \([0,\bar r]\) into both laws. The mixtures stay continuous and ordered, and the difference keeps its sign, scaled by a positive factor.

Step 5: the case without an atom at \(p\).

Distribution functions are right-continuous, so \(D\) is right-continuous. If \(D(y_0)<0\) at some \(y_0>p\), then \(D<0\) on an interval \([y_0,y_0+\varepsilon)\), a set of positive measure. So “\(D\ge0\) almost everywhere above \(p\)” is the same as “\(D(y)\ge0\) for every \(y>p\)”, and it gives \(D(p)=\lim_{y\downarrow p}D(y)\ge0\). Without an atom at \(p\), \(D(p^-)=D(p)\ge0\). With \(D(p^-)\le0\), this forces \(D(p^-)=0\), which is equal admissibility. \(\square\)

A worked example where the spread falls.

Keep the point-mass classes, but set an exclusion reserve \(p\) with \(\ell<p<h\). The low class never meets the reserve, so \(G_L(p^-)=1\), \(G_H(p^-)=0\) and \(D(p^-)=1\). The condition \(D(p^-)\le0\) fails. Then \(\xi(R)=p\) for \(R<p\): only class \(H\) buys, at \(p\). And \(\xi(R)=R-p\) for \(p\le R<h\): class \(H\) wins and pays \(R\), while a class-\(L\) challenger cannot bid, so the incumbent buys alone at \(p\). For the uniform incumbent on \([0,r]\) with \(p<r<h\), \[\Delta_T(r)=\frac1r\Bigl[\int_0^p p\,dy+\int_p^r(y-p)\,dy\Bigr]=\frac{p^2+(r-p)^2/2}{r}.\] Its derivative is \(\bigl[(r-p)r-p^2-(r-p)^2/2\bigr]/r^2=(r^2-3p^2)/(2r^2)\). This is negative for \(p<r<\sqrt3\,p\) and positive above. So the spread first falls with incumbent strength, then rises. The cause is the reserve term: a stronger incumbent fails the reserve less often, so the slice \(p\) that only class \(H\) delivers below the reserve shrinks. Section 8.2 evaluates this example. Section 7.3 of the paper (Section 12 here) studies this reserve in full.

The surplus split

Add the two lines of [paper (8)]. First, \(g_H-g_L=\int_p^\infty\{[1-G_H]-[1-G_L]\}F\,dy =\int_p^\infty D(y)F(y)\,dy\). So \[\begin{align*} \Delta_T+g_H-g_L&=pF(p)D(p^-)+\int_p^\infty D(y)[1-F(y)]\,dy+\int_p^\infty D(y)F(y)\,dy\\ &=pF(p)D(p^-)+\int_p^\infty D(y)\,dy. \end{align*}\] Next, \(\max\{\theta,p\}=p+(\theta-p)_+\) and \(\mathbb{E}_j(\theta-p)_+=\int_p^\infty\Pr_j(\theta>y)\,dy =\int_p^\infty[1-G_j(y)]\,dy\). So \(\int_p^\infty D(y)\,dy=\mathbb{E}_H[\max\{\theta,p\}]-\mathbb{E}_L[\max\{\theta,p\}]\), and \[\Delta_T(F)+g_H(F)-g_L(F)=pF(p)D(p^-)+\mathbb{E}_H[\max\{\theta,p\}]-\mathbb{E}_L[\max\{\theta,p\}].\] The reserve term vanishes under equal admissibility, and also when the incumbent always meets the reserve (\(F(p)=0\)). Under the class ranking every value is at least \(p\), so \(\max\{\theta,p\}=\theta\) and \[\Delta_T(F)+g_H(F)-g_L(F)=\mathbb{E}_H\theta-\mathbb{E}_L\theta\qquad\text{for every }F.\] In the benchmark the right side is \(h-\ell\) at every \(r\). A stronger incumbent changes how the gap is split, but not its size. Section 8.2 checks the sum at the strong economy.

Panel note: how to say it

“A stronger incumbent does not change the value gap between the classes. It moves the gap from the challenger’s profit to target proceeds. The stock trades on the target’s share of the gap, and the challenger decides on its own share.”

Link to the old guide

The auction stage does not depend on the financial market. Thus the six benchmark payoffs remain those of the old guide. The new material is the formula for general classes, the reserve term, and Proposition 1(c). Section 11.5 covers the change in the financial market.

Previous: The modelNext: What the order slot reveals

4What the order slot reveals

The order slot gives the market one of three observations: a sale, zero, or a purchase. This chapter derives the beliefs after these orders. It then shows why the challenger can recover those beliefs from the price. The proofs cover mixed profiles and wrong-signed orders. [paper Sec. 3.2; Appendix A.2]

The order law under every profile

Write \(\sigma_j(x)\) for the probability that the investor chooses \(x\) in class \(j\). The three probabilities in each \(\sigma_j\) sum to one. At this stage, allow a sale in class \(H\) and a purchase in class \(L\). The liquidity law is \[\eta(-1)=\beta,\qquad \eta(0)=1-2\beta,\qquad \eta(1)=\beta.\] An investor fills the slot with probability \(\lambda\). A liquidity trader fills it with probability \(1-\lambda\). These are alternative arrivals, so the conditional order probability is a sum: \[\begin{align*} f_j(x)&:=\Pr(X=x\mid j)\\ &=(1-\lambda)\eta(x)+\lambda\sigma_j(x). \end{align*}\] Define the two liquidity masses \[b=(1-\lambda)\beta,\qquad b_0=(1-\lambda)(1-2\beta),\qquad u=\frac{\lambda}{b}.\] Thus \(f_j(\pm1)=b+\lambda\sigma_j(\pm1)\) and \(f_j(0)=b_0+\lambda\sigma_j(0)\). The number \(u\) compares informed presence with the liquidity mass in one direction. It is not the posterior odds that an observed order is informed.

Because \(0<\lambda<1\) and \(0<\beta<1/2\), every liquidity mass is positive. Hence \(f_j(x)\ge (1-\lambda)\eta(x)>0\) for every order and class: every order occurs under every profile. Bayes’ rule therefore specifies every order posterior without an off-path belief.

The formulas do not ask whether the orders are optimal. They apply to a profile with wrong-signed orders in both classes, and to a mixed profile. Section 8.4 works one such profile with exact fractions.

Likelihood ratios and the posterior bounds

A bound on the likelihood ratio gives a bound on the posterior.

Intuition: Why one order cannot reveal the class

A liquidity trader can place any order in either class. The informed investor adds at most \(\lambda\) to the probability of that order. This limits the likelihood ratio, even under an extreme or mixed profile.

For a nonzero order, \(0\le\sigma_j(x)\le1\) gives \[b\le f_j(x)\le b+\lambda.\] The largest ratio uses the largest numerator and the smallest denominator. The smallest ratio reverses them: \[\frac{b}{b+\lambda}\le\frac{f_H(x)}{f_L(x)}\le\frac{b+\lambda}{b}.\] Since \(\lambda/b=u\), these bounds are \(1/(1+u)\) and \(1+u\). Bayes’ rule gives \(\mu(x)=\pi f_H(x)/[\pi f_H(x)+(1-\pi)f_L(x)]\) and \(1-\mu(x)=(1-\pi)f_L(x)/[\pi f_H(x)+(1-\pi)f_L(x)]\). Divide the first by the second; the denominators cancel: \[\frac{\mu(x)}{1-\mu(x)}=\frac{\pi}{1-\pi}\cdot\frac{f_H(x)}{f_L(x)}.\tag*{\textsf{\small[paper (A.3)]}}\] This is the odds form of Bayes’ rule. The posterior rises strictly with these odds. For a likelihood ratio \(L\), its value is \(\pi L/[\pi L+1-\pi]\). Substitution of the two extreme ratios gives \[\begin{align*} m(\pi)&=\frac{\pi/(1+u)}{\pi/(1+u)+1-\pi} =\frac{\pi}{\pi+(1-\pi)(1+u)},\\ M(\pi)&=\frac{\pi(1+u)}{\pi(1+u)+1-\pi} =\frac{\pi(1+u)}{1+\pi u}. \end{align*}\] Thus \(m(\pi)\le\mu(\pm1)\le M(\pi)\) under every profile. [paper (4); Lemma A.1(b)] Both bounds lie strictly between zero and one. Also \(m(\pi)<\pi<M(\pi)\) because \(u>0\).

The zero order and the restriction \(\beta\le1/3\)

At zero the relevant liquidity mass is \(b_0\), rather than \(b\). The same argument gives \[b_0\le f_j(0)\le b_0+\lambda,\qquad \frac{1}{1+\lambda/b_0}\le\frac{f_H(0)}{f_L(0)}\le1+\frac{\lambda}{b_0}.\] To use the previous bounds, we need \[\begin{align*} b_0\ge b &\iff (1-\lambda)(1-2\beta)\ge(1-\lambda)\beta\\ &\iff 1-2\beta\ge\beta \iff \beta\le\frac13. \end{align*}\] Then \(\lambda/b_0\le\lambda/b=u\). The zero-order likelihood ratio also lies between \(1/(1+u)\) and \(1+u\). Hence \(m(\pi)\le\mu(0)\le M(\pi)\). [paper Lemma A.1(c)]

Caution: A zero order can be the most informative order

Do not treat zero as uninformative under an arbitrary profile. It is uninformative under full orders because neither class chooses zero. A one-sided profile can make zero good news.

To see the failure when \(\beta>1/3\), let class \(H\) always abstain and class \(L\) always sell. Then \(f_H(0)=b_0+\lambda\) and \(f_L(0)=b_0\). Since \(b_0<b\), \[\frac{f_H(0)}{f_L(0)}=1+\frac{\lambda}{b_0}>1+\frac{\lambda}{b}=1+u.\] The zero-order likelihood ratio exceeds the top ratio, so \(\mu(0)\) lies above \(M(\pi)\). Section 8.4 gives an exact example. This is a failure of the bound under a profile, regardless of investor optimality. The nonzero-order bounds still hold for every \(\beta<1/2\).

Full orders and the slot beliefs

Under full orders, class \(H\) buys and class \(L\) sells with probability one. The conditional laws and their ratios are \[\begin{array}{c|ccc} x&-1&0&1\\ \hline f_H(x)&b&b_0&b+\lambda\\ f_L(x)&b+\lambda&b_0&b\\ f_H(x)/f_L(x)&1/(1+u)&1&1+u\\ \mu(x)&m(\pi)&\pi&M(\pi) \end{array}\] This establishes the three posteriors directly. [paper Lemma A.1(d)] Full orders reach both bounds. The zero order leaves the prior unchanged.

The slot is easier to describe by \(u\) and \(\beta\) than by \(\lambda\) and \(\beta\). The presence probability then follows from them: \[\begin{align*} u&=\frac{\lambda}{(1-\lambda)\beta} \iff u\beta(1-\lambda)=\lambda\\ &\iff \lambda=\frac{u\beta}{1+u\beta}. \end{align*}\] A larger \(u\) moves both bounds away from the prior. At the prior one half, \(m=1/(2+u)\) and \(M=(1+u)/(2+u)\), and the two bounds are symmetric about one half. Section 8.4 gives the benchmark slot and its beliefs.

A price belief is an average, and the ceiling follows

The challenger sees \(P(X)\), rather than \(X\). For a price \(\bar P\), let \(A=\{x:P(x)=\bar P\}\). Write \(z_x=\pi f_H(x)+(1-\pi)f_L(x)\) for the unconditional probability of \(x\). Then \[\begin{align*} \bar\mu(\bar P) &=\frac{\sum_{x\in A}\pi f_H(x)}{\sum_{x\in A}z_x}\\ &=\sum_{x\in A}\frac{z_x}{\sum_{y\in A}z_y}\,\mu(x). \end{align*}\] Every weight is positive, and the weights sum to one. Therefore a price belief lies between the smallest and largest posteriors in its pool. In particular, \(\bar\mu(\bar P)\in[m(\pi),M(\pi)]\) when \(\beta\le1/3\). This argument needs no assumption that the price reveals the order.

Result: The ceiling under every profile

Let \(\beta\le1/3\), \(g_H\ge g_L\) and \(B(M(\pi))<c_H\). Then no price recruits a high-cost challenger under any profile, in or out of equilibrium, that has Bayes beliefs and optimal preparation. [paper Corollary A.1]

The proof has one inequality chain: \[B(\bar\mu(\bar P))=g_L+\bar\mu(\bar P)(g_H-g_L) \le g_L+M(\pi)(g_H-g_L)<c_H.\] The first inequality uses \(\bar\mu(\bar P)\le M(\pi)\) and \(g_H-g_L\ge0\). Thus high-cost preparation has a strictly negative net return at every price, also at a price that pools orders. The comparison is strict, so the result holds under both challenger tie rules. The universal ceiling uses the zero-order bound, so it needs \(\beta\le1/3\). Proposition 2 instead forces full orders first. Its proof therefore needs only \(\beta<1/2\).

The entry floor and the competitive price

A zero-cost challenger prepares after every price. Its gross profit is a nonnegative auction rent, so this choice is optimal. The model fixes that choice even when its rent is zero. If \(\zeta(P)\) denotes high-cost preparation, entry after order \(x\) is \[e(x)=\rho+(1-\rho)\zeta(P(x))\ge\rho>0.\] This is the entry floor. [paper Lemma A.2(a)]

Let \(w_j=t_j-t_0\) and \(w(\mu)=w_L+\Delta_T\mu\). The auction gives \(w_H\ge w_L\ge0\). Conditional on the class and the order, the cost draw alone determines preparation. The cost is independent of both acquisition values and the class. Also the order gives no further value information once the class is known. Hence \[\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid j,X=x]=(1-e(x))t_0+e(x)t_j=t_0+e(x)w_j.\] Average this identity over the two classes: \[\begin{align*} P(x)=\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid X=x] &=\mu(x)[t_0+e(x)w_H]+[1-\mu(x)][t_0+e(x)w_L]\\ &=t_0+e(x)\{w_L+\mu(x)(w_H-w_L)\}\\ &=t_0+e(x)[t_L-t_0+\Delta_T\mu(x)]. \end{align*}\] This is the competitive price. [paper (5); (A.4); Lemma A.2(b)] It includes the expected proceeds from the entry that the price itself causes.

Price sufficiency, the threshold, and strict price order

Assume \(\Delta_T>0\) and \(g_H>g_L\).

Intuition: Why the circularity is harmless

The price depends on entry, and entry depends on the price. You do not need to solve this circle to invert the price. Two orders with one price have one entry probability, and that entry is at least \(\rho\). The price formula then leaves only the posterior to differ, and it cannot.

First prove price sufficiency without an assumption about entry as a function of the order posterior. If \(P(x)=P(x')=\bar P\), the challenger faces the same price and cost law. Thus \(e(x)=e(x')=:\bar e\). The price formula gives \[0=P(x)-P(x')=\bar e\Delta_T[\mu(x)-\mu(x')].\] Since \(\bar e\Delta_T\ge\rho\Delta_T>0\), the two posteriors are equal. The price belief averages equal posteriors, so \[\bar\mu(P(x))=\mu(x).\] Equivalently, the challenger can recover the posterior from \[\mu(x)=\frac{P(x)-t_0-e(P(x))(t_L-t_0)}{e(P(x))\Delta_T}.\] Here \(e(P)\) denotes the entry probability associated with price \(P\). [paper Lemma A.2(c)] The denominator is positive because of the entry floor.

Since \(g_H>g_L\), \(B(\mu)\ge c_H\) holds exactly when \(\mu\ge\tau=(c_H-g_L)/(g_H-g_L)\), the threshold of Section 3.4. By sufficiency, the challenger’s belief at \(P(x)\) is \(\mu(x)\). Under rule C1, an indifferent high-cost challenger prepares. Thus entry is \(\rho\) below \(\tau\) and one at or above \(\tau\). Under rule CM, entry at \(\tau\) can be any value in \([\rho,1]\). The threshold can lie outside \([0,1]\); the same inequalities still apply.

Take \(\mu_2>\mu_1\), and write \(e_i\) for the associated entry. Optimal preparation gives \(e_2\ge e_1\) under either rule. Add and subtract \(e_2w(\mu_1)\): \[\begin{align*} P_2-P_1 &=e_2w(\mu_2)-e_1w(\mu_1)\\ &=e_2[w(\mu_2)-w(\mu_1)]+(e_2-e_1)w(\mu_1)\\ &=e_2\Delta_T(\mu_2-\mu_1)+(e_2-e_1)[w_L+\Delta_T\mu_1]\\ &\ge\rho\Delta_T(\mu_2-\mu_1)>0. \end{align*}\] The second term is nonnegative because \(e_2\ge e_1\) and \(w_L+\Delta_T\mu_1\ge0\). Thus prices rise strictly with the posterior. [paper (A.5); Lemma A.2(e)] Equal prices can pool equal posteriors, as in the weak economy. They cannot pool distinct posteriors under these assumptions.

Under full orders in the strong economy, only the purchase posterior \(M(\pi)\) exceeds \(\tau\). A purchase therefore recruits the high-cost challenger, and a sale or zero does not. Entry is one after a purchase and \(\rho\) after the other two orders. The three prices are distinct, and the inverse formula recovers each posterior. Section 8.4 gives the three prices at the benchmark.

Panel note: What changed from the Laplace version

The Laplace version bounded the likelihood ratio through the noise density; the slot bounds it through the liquidity mass in each order. The benchmark slot is chosen so that its top posterior equals the Laplace value. Section 11.5 derives this match and explains why it concerns the bound only. The zero order is new: it needs \(\beta\le1/3\).

Previous: The auction stage: who pays whatNext: The investor’s problem

5The investor’s problem

The investor chooses among a sale, zero, and a purchase. Each class compares one correctly signed order with zero once we exclude the wrong sign. The key bounds apply under every candidate profile, before investor optimality is imposed. [paper Sec. 3.3; Lemma A.3]

Hold the price and entry rules fixed

A candidate satisfies (E1)-(E4) and (E6); it need not satisfy investor optimality (E5). An investor deviation changes only its own order. It faces the candidate’s price function \(P(\cdot)\) and preparation rule \(\zeta(\cdot)\), as the caution in Section 2.5 explains. Since its order fills the whole slot, a deviation to \(q\) reaches \(P(q)\) and \(e(q)\). Do not recompute Bayes beliefs from the deviating order profile: those beliefs belong to a different candidate.

Conditional on investor arrival, the expected payoff in class \(j\) is \[U_j(q)=q\{t_0+e(q)(t_j-t_0)-P(q)\}-k|q|, \qquad q\in\{-1,0,1\}.\] The factor \(\lambda>0\) of the ex ante payoff changes no comparison between orders.

Derive the residual advantage and the six payoffs

Use \(w_j=t_j-t_0\), \(w_H-w_L=\Delta_T\), the conditional value \(\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid j,X=q]=t_0+e(q)w_j\) and the price \(P(q)=t_0+e(q)[w_L+\Delta_T\mu(q)]\) of Section 4.6. In class \(H\), subtract the price from the conditional value: \[\begin{align*} \mathbb{E}[V_T\mid H,X=q]-P(q) &=t_0+e(q)w_H-t_0-e(q)[w_L+\Delta_T\mu(q)]\\ &=e(q)[w_H-w_L-\Delta_T\mu(q)]\\ &=e(q)\Delta_T[1-\mu(q)]. \end{align*}\] In class \(L\), a sale earns the opposite difference: \[\begin{align*} P(q)-\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid L,X=q] &=t_0+e(q)[w_L+\Delta_T\mu(q)]-t_0-e(q)w_L\\ &=e(q)\Delta_T\mu(q). \end{align*}\] The proceeds without entry, \(t_0\), cancel in both expressions. The common entry gain \(e(q)w_L\) also cancels.

Multiply the residual by the order sign and subtract the cost. The six payoffs are \[\begin{align*} U_H(1)&=e(1)\Delta_T[1-\mu(1)]-k,& U_H(0)&=0,\\ U_H(-1)&=-e(-1)\Delta_T[1-\mu(-1)]-k,\\[2pt] U_L(-1)&=e(-1)\Delta_T\mu(-1)-k,& U_L(0)&=0,\\ U_L(1)&=-e(1)\Delta_T\mu(1)-k. \end{align*}\] Define the gross returns of the two correctly signed nonzero orders: \[K_H=e(1)\Delta_T[1-\mu(1)],\qquad K_L=e(-1)\Delta_T\mu(-1).\] Then a correct order has net return \(K_j-k\).

Intuition: What rational prices remove and what they leave

Market makers anticipate preparation and include its expected effect in the price. The investor therefore earns no extra return from that common expected effect alone. It still knows the class while market makers retain some uncertainty. Its residual advantage is entry times the target-payoff spread times that residual uncertainty. A liquidity order prevents either nonzero order from revealing the class with certainty.

This argument also applies to general class distributions. The investor knows the class, rather than the realized acquisition value. The relevant spread is \(t_H-t_L\), the difference between expected target proceeds in the two classes.

Wrong-signed orders are dominated

Under the class ranking, \(\Delta_T\ge0\). Also \(e(x)\ge0\) and \(0\le\mu(x)\le1\). Thus \[U_H(-1)\le-k<0=U_H(0),\qquad U_L(1)\le-k<0=U_L(0).\] Zero strictly beats a sale in class \(H\) and a purchase in class \(L\). No equilibrium puts positive probability on either wrong-signed order. This conclusion comes from payoffs; it is not a restriction on the candidate profiles.

Class \(H\) therefore compares \(K_H\) with \(k\). It buys if \(K_H>k\) and abstains if \(K_H<k\). Class \(L\) sells if \(K_L>k\) and abstains if \(K_L<k\). At equality, the correct order and zero are both best responses. Rule IM permits any mixture of these best responses; rule I0 selects zero.

The gross-return bounds under every candidate

For a correct purchase, the posterior bound gives \(\mu(1)\le M(\pi)\). Subtract from one, multiply by \(\Delta_T\ge0\), and then use the entry floor: \[\begin{align*} 1-\mu(1)&\ge1-M(\pi),\\ \Delta_T[1-\mu(1)]&\ge\Delta_T[1-M(\pi)],\\ K_H=e(1)\Delta_T[1-\mu(1)]&\ge\rho\Delta_T[1-\mu(1)] \ge\rho\Delta_T[1-M(\pi)]. \end{align*}\] For a correct sale, \(\mu(-1)\ge m(\pi)\) gives \[K_L=e(-1)\Delta_T\mu(-1)\ge\rho\Delta_T\mu(-1)\ge\rho\Delta_Tm(\pi).\] For the upper bounds, \(e\le1\), \(\mu\le1\), and \(1-\mu\le1\) give \(K_H,K_L\le\Delta_T\). Set \(\kappa(\pi)=\min\{m(\pi),1-M(\pi)\}\). We obtain

Result: The bound that fixes orders before a candidate is known

Under every candidate profile, \[\rho\kappa(\pi)\Delta_T\le K_H\le\Delta_T,\qquad \rho\kappa(\pi)\Delta_T\le K_L\le\Delta_T.\] These are bounds on the gross returns of correctly signed nonzero orders. [paper (A.6)] They hold for every \(\beta<1/2\).

Caution: Scope of the lower bound

Do not apply the positive lower bound to zero or to a wrong-signed order. Zero earns zero, and a wrong-signed order has a negative net return. Do not replace \(\kappa\) by \(m\) at an arbitrary prior. The two coincide at \(\pi=1/2\).

A mixed candidate that is not an equilibrium still obeys these bounds. The bounds use only Bayes’ rule and the entry floor, not the optimality of the orders. Section 8.5 gives a worked candidate.

Why the prior one half gives the largest floor

Calculate the residual at the top posterior: \[\begin{align*} 1-M(\pi) &=\frac{1-\pi}{1+\pi u}\\ &=\frac{1-\pi}{(1-\pi)+\pi(1+u)}=m(1-\pi). \end{align*}\] The function \(m\) rises strictly with its argument. Indeed, \[m(\pi)=\frac{\pi}{1+u-\pi u},\qquad m'(\pi)=\frac{1+u}{(1+u-\pi u)^2}>0.\] It follows that \[\kappa(\pi)=m(\min\{\pi,1-\pi\}),\qquad \kappa(1-\pi)=\kappa(\pi).\] The minimum of \(\pi\) and \(1-\pi\) is largest at \(1/2\). Therefore \(\kappa\) has its unique maximum there: \[\kappa(1/2)=\frac1{2+u}.\] When \(\pi>1/2\), the bound for class \(H\) binds because the market already favors \(H\). When \(\pi<1/2\), the bound for class \(L\) binds for the same reason. Away from the prior one half, the floor falls on both sides by the same amount.

Why the weak economy forces zero and the strong economy forces full orders

If \(\Delta_T<k\), every correct nonzero order satisfies \[U_j(q)=K_j-k\le\Delta_T-k<0=U_j(0).\] Wrong-signed orders are also strictly worse than zero. Thus zero is the unique best response of both classes under every candidate, and (E5) excludes every mixture.

If \(k<\rho\kappa\Delta_T\), every correct order instead satisfies \[U_j(q)=K_j-k\ge\rho\kappa\Delta_T-k>0=U_j(0).\] The wrong sign is strictly worse than zero. Thus each class has one unique best response: a purchase in \(H\), a sale in \(L\). Every equilibrium uses full orders; the argument excludes all other pure and mixed profiles at once.

The weak economy of Proposition 2 has a spread below the cost, so it falls in the first case. The strong economy has a floor above the cost, so it falls in the second case. Section 8.5 gives the benchmark margins.

Caution: Uniqueness and verification are two tests

The bound \(\rho\kappa\Delta_T\) holds against every candidate, so it proves uniqueness. The returns \(K_H\) and \(K_L\) at the full-order tuple verify that one candidate only. Keep the two tests apart on the panel, as in the old guide.

Panel note: What changed from the Laplace version

The slot has only unit orders and zero, so the proof compares three payoffs and needs no derivative in the order size. The Laplace factor \(1-1/s\) (the old guide’s \(1-1/b\)) therefore drops out of the trading-cost window.

Previous: What the order slot revealsNext: The main result: Proposition 2

6The main result: Proposition 2

Proposition 2 compares three economies with the same classes, costs, prior, and order slot. Only the incumbent distribution changes. Entry equals its floor against the weak incumbent. It exceeds its floor against the strong incumbent. It returns to its floor against the very strong incumbent.

The statement and the role of each hypothesis

Let \(F_1\) strengthen \(F_0\) by first-order stochastic dominance, and let \(F_2\) strengthen \(F_1\). The distributions are continuous. The classes satisfy the class ranking: \(G_H\) dominates \(G_L\), and every class value meets the reserve. Write \[B_i=B_{F_i},\quad \Delta_i=\Delta_T(F_i),\quad M=M(\pi),\quad m=m(\pi),\quad \kappa=\min\{m,1-M\}.\] Keep \(0<\pi,\rho,\lambda<1\), \(k>0\), and the slot restriction \(0<\beta\le1/3\).

Result: Proposition 2: competition creates competition

Assume the following three strict hypotheses: \[\begin{align*} \text{(H1)}&\qquad B_0(\pi)<c_H<B_1(M),\\ \text{(H2)}&\qquad \Delta_0<k<\rho\kappa\Delta_1,\\ \text{(H3)}&\qquad B_2(M)<c_H. \end{align*}\] Each economy has a unique equilibrium outcome under truthful bids. [paper Prop. 2]

  1. At \(F_0\), both classes choose zero. Every posterior is \(\pi\), every price is the same, and entry is \(\rho\).

  2. At \(F_1\), class \(H\) buys and class \(L\) sells with probability one. The posteriors are \((m,\pi,M)\), and only a purchase recruits the high-cost challenger. Entry is \(\mathsf{E}_1=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\pi\lambda)>\rho\). High-class ownership rises from \(\pi\rho W_H(F_0)\) to \(\pi[\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda)]W_H(F_1)\) if and only if \([\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda)]W_H(F_1)>\rho W_H(F_0)\).

  3. At \(F_2\), the orders remain full and the posteriors remain \((m,\pi,M)\). The three prices differ, but entry after every order equals \(\rho\).

The high-cost window (H1) sets the two preparation incentives. At the weak prior, gross profit falls below the high cost. At the strong top posterior, gross profit exceeds it. The trading-cost window (H2) fixes orders against every candidate. The ceiling (H3) stops high-cost preparation in the third economy.

The trading-cost window exists exactly when \[\Delta_0<\rho\kappa\Delta_1 \iff \frac{\Delta_0}{\Delta_1}<\rho\kappa.\] The ratio shows how much the target-payoff spread must rise. Parts (i) and (ii) need only (H1) and (H2). The proof below also works for every \(\beta<1/2\).

Step 0: derive the inequalities that the proof needs

Proposition 1 gives \(B_1(\pi)\le B_0(\pi)\). Thus (H1) implies \[B_1(\pi)<c_H<B_1(M).\] Subtract the two gross profits: \[B_1(M)-B_1(\pi)=(M-\pi)(g_{H,1}-g_{L,1})>0.\] Since \(M>\pi\), we obtain \(g_{H,1}>g_{L,1}\). Therefore \(\tau_1\) is defined, and \[\pi<\tau_1=\frac{c_H-g_{L,1}}{g_{H,1}-g_{L,1}}<M.\] Also \(m<\pi\), so neither a sale nor zero can recruit at \(F_1\) once full orders are established.

Hypothesis (H2) gives \(\Delta_1>k/(\rho\kappa)>0\). Proposition 1 gives \(\Delta_2\ge\Delta_1\), so \[\rho\kappa\Delta_2\ge\rho\kappa\Delta_1>k.\] The class ranking also gives \(g_{H,2}\ge g_{L,2}\). Hence \(B_2(\mu)\le B_2(M)<c_H\) whenever \(\mu\le M\). The zero-cost challenger gives \(e(x)\ge\rho\) in every candidate of all three economies.

Step 1: force and verify the weak tuple

Fix any candidate at \(F_0\). By Section 5.6, \(\Delta_0<k\) makes zero the unique best response in each class. Thus every equilibrium has \(\sigma_H=\sigma_L=\delta_0\), where \(\delta_q\) is a point mass at \(q\).

These orders give \(f_H=f_L\) at every order. Every order posterior, and every price belief, equals \(\pi\). Since \(B_0(\pi)<c_H\), high-cost preparation is zero. The only possible tuple is \[\mathcal E_0=(\delta_0,\delta_0,\mu_0,P_0,\bar\mu_0,\zeta_0),\] with truthful bids and \[\mu_0(x)=\pi,\quad e_0(x)=\rho,\quad P_0(x)=\bar P_0:=t_{0,0}+\rho w_0(\pi),\quad \bar\mu_0(\bar P_0)=\pi,\quad \zeta_0(\bar P_0)=0.\] Here \(w_i(\mu)=t_{L,i}-t_{0,i}+\Delta_i\mu\). Now check the six conditions against this fixed tuple. [paper Definition A.1; Appendix A.3]

  1. Since \(f_H=f_L\), Bayes’ rule gives \(\mu_0(x)=\pi\).

  2. The single price pools all orders. Both conditional order laws sum to one, so its posterior is \(\pi\).

  3. The strict inequality \(B_0(\pi)<c_H\) makes \(\zeta_0=0\) optimal. The zero-cost challenger prepares by the model’s convention.

  4. Conditional terminal value is \(t_{0,0}+\rho(t_{j,0}-t_{0,0})\) in class \(j\). Its average at \(\pi\) is exactly \(\bar P_0\).

  5. At this tuple, \(K_H=\rho\Delta_0(1-\pi)<k\) and \(K_L=\rho\Delta_0\pi<k\). Thus zero uniquely beats both alternatives in either class.

  6. Truthful bids are weakly dominant in the cash second-price auction with its reserve.

This proves existence after the earlier bounds prove uniqueness. Entry is \(\rho\), and high-class ownership is \(\pi\rho W_H(F_0)\).

Steps 2 and 3: force and verify the strong tuple

Fix any candidate at \(F_1\). By Section 5.6, \(k<\rho\kappa\Delta_1\) makes the correct order the unique best response in each class. Hence every equilibrium has \(\sigma_H=\delta_1\) and \(\sigma_L=\delta_{-1}\). The order laws now force the posteriors \((m,\pi,M)\). Price sufficiency (Section 4.7) and the inequalities of Step 0 force entry \((\rho,\rho,1)\), with no tie.

The unique possible tuple is \[\mathcal E_1=(\delta_1,\delta_{-1},\mu_1,P_1,\bar\mu_1,\zeta_1),\] with truthful bids and, on \(x=(-1,0,1)\), \[\begin{gather*} \mu_1=(m,\pi,M),\qquad e_1=(\rho,\rho,1),\\ P_1(x)=t_{0,1}+e_1(x)w_1(\mu_1(x)),\qquad \bar\mu_1(P_1(x))=\mu_1(x),\qquad \zeta_1(P_1(x))=\mathbf{1}\{x=1\}. \end{gather*}\] The verification must establish the price beliefs directly. It must not assume entry optimality to prove that same condition.

  1. The likelihoods are \(f_H=(b,b_0,b+\lambda)\) and \(f_L=(b+\lambda,b_0,b)\). Bayes’ rule gives \((m,\pi,M)\).

  2. The stated entry is nondecreasing in the posterior and is at least \(\rho\). The price-difference algebra in Section 4.7 gives \(P_1(-1)<P_1(0)<P_1(1)\) directly. Each price identifies one order, so its belief equals that order’s posterior.

  3. We have \(B_1(m)\le B_1(\pi)<c_H<B_1(M)\). Thus precisely a purchase recruits the high-cost challenger.

  4. At each order, average \(t_{0,1}+e_1(x)(t_{j,1}-t_{0,1})\) with its Bayes weights. This equals the stated \(P_1(x)\).

  5. Direct substitution gives \(K_H=\Delta_1(1-M)>k\) and \(K_L=\rho\Delta_1m>k\). The candidate-wide lower bound implies both strict inequalities. The unique best responses are therefore the stated full orders.

  6. Truthful bids remain weakly dominant.

Step 4: entry and ownership

Only a purchase recruits a high-cost challenger. In class \(H\), a purchase occurs through informed presence or a liquidity purchase: \[\Pr(X=1\mid H)=\lambda+(1-\lambda)\beta=b+\lambda.\] In class \(L\), only the liquidity purchase reaches it, so \(\Pr(X=1\mid L)=b\). Therefore \[\begin{align*} e_H&=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda),\\ e_L&=\rho+(1-\rho)b,\\ \mathsf{E}_1=\pi e_H+(1-\pi)e_L &=\rho+(1-\rho)[\pi(b+\lambda)+(1-\pi)b]\\ &=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\pi\lambda)>\rho. \end{align*}\] The difference \(e_H-e_L=(1-\rho)\lambda>0\) shows the class selection. Prices recruit both classes, but recruit class \(H\) more often.

Let \(W_H(F)=\mathbb{E}_H[F(\theta)]\) denote the probability that a prepared class-\(H\) challenger wins. The class supports lie above the reserve. Preparation is independent of the realized values conditional on the class. Thus the exact ownership comparison is \[\begin{align*} \mathsf{O}_{H,1}>\mathsf{O}_{H,0} &\iff \pi e_HW_H(F_1)>\pi\rho W_H(F_0)\\ &\iff [\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda)]W_H(F_1)>\rho W_H(F_0). \end{align*}\] More entry alone does not guarantee more high-class ownership under general classes. A stronger incumbent can reduce \(W_H\).

A useful sufficient condition is \[(1+\rho)W_H(F_1)\ge2\rho W_H(F_0)>0, \qquad b+\lambda>\frac12.\] Indeed, \(e_H>\rho+(1-\rho)/2=(1+\rho)/2\). Multiplication by \(W_H(F_1)>0\) gives \[e_HW_H(F_1)>\frac{1+\rho}{2}W_H(F_1)\ge\rho W_H(F_0).\] In the point-mass benchmark, \(h>r_i\) gives \(W_H(F_i)=1\). The exact condition then reduces to \(e_H>\rho\), which always holds here. So in the benchmark, entry and high-class ownership both rise from the weak to the strong economy. Class \(H\) gains more entry than class \(L\), because only class \(H\) adds informed purchases. Section 8.6.1 gives the benchmark values.

Step 5: the very strong tuple and the entry ceiling

At \(F_2\), \(\Delta_2\ge\Delta_1\) preserves the strict incentive for both full orders under every candidate. Hence the posteriors are again \((m,\pi,M)\). Every price belief averages these three posteriors and is at most \(M\). Hypothesis (H3) therefore makes high-cost preparation strictly unprofitable after every price. The only possible tuple is \(\mathcal E_2=(\delta_1,\delta_{-1},\mu_2,P_2,\bar\mu_2,\zeta_2)\) with truthful bids, \[\mu_2=(m,\pi,M),\quad e_2\equiv\rho,\quad P_2(x)=t_{0,2}+\rho w_2(\mu_2(x)),\quad \bar\mu_2(P_2(x))=\mu_2(x),\quad \zeta_2\equiv0.\] The six checks repeat those of \(\mathcal E_1\) with \(e\equiv\rho\). (E1) holds by the full-order likelihoods. (E2): two prices differ by \(\rho\Delta_2[\mu_2(x)-\mu_2(x')]\neq0\), so each price reveals its posterior. (E3): \(B_2(\mu_2(x))\le B_2(M)<c_H\) at every price. (E4): the conditional terminal values average to \(P_2(x)\). (E5): \(K_H=\rho\Delta_2(1-M)>k\) and \(K_L=\rho\Delta_2m>k\). (E6): truthful bids remain weakly dominant.

In the third economy, entry is \(\rho\), yet the three prices still differ. Information survives even though high-cost preparation stops.

The ceiling (H3) holds for every strength above an entry ceiling \(r_C\). For a uniform incumbent with point-mass classes, each class profit falls strictly with \(r\). So gross profit at the top posterior, a weighted average of the two, also falls strictly. The entry ceiling \(r_C\) solves \(B_{r_C}(M)=c_H\), and it is unique. Below \(r_C\), a purchase recruits the high-cost challenger. Above \(r_C\), even the top posterior fails to justify the high cost. Section 8.6.2 derives the quadratic for \(r_C\) and its root at the benchmark. At \(r_C\), a purchase leaves the high-cost challenger indifferent. Rule C1 gives total entry \(\mathsf{E}_1\) there; rule CM permits any value in \([\rho,\mathsf{E}_1]\).

Derive the exact admissible band

Fix the auction, costs, and strengths, and let \(\pi=1/2\). Two parts of the hypotheses do not depend on the slot: the left side of (H1), \(B_0(1/2)<c_H\), and the left side of (H2), \(\Delta_0<k\). The slot enters the other three conditions only through \(u\).

At this prior, \(M=(1+u)/(2+u)\) and \(\kappa=1/(2+u)\). For any \(0<\tau<1\), a top-posterior condition becomes a bound on \(u\): \[\begin{align*} M>\tau &\iff (1+u)>\tau(2+u)\\ &\iff (1-\tau)u>2\tau-1 \iff u>\frac{2\tau-1}{1-\tau}. \end{align*}\] The right side of (H1), \(M>\tau_1\), therefore gives a lower bound on \(u\). The ceiling (H3), \(M<\tau_2\), gives an upper bound on \(u\). The right side of (H2) becomes \[\frac1{2+u}>\frac{k}{\rho\Delta_1} \iff u<\frac{\rho\Delta_1}{k}-2,\] a second upper bound on \(u\). At the benchmark, the ceiling bound is the tighter of the two upper bounds. So the trading bound is redundant, and the five inequalities reduce to one open interval of \(u\).

Result: The admissible band at the prior one half

At the benchmark auction, costs and strengths, (H1)-(H3) hold if and only if \[\frac{2\tau_1-1}{1-\tau_1}<u<\frac{2\tau_2-1}{1-\tau_2} \iff \tau_1<M<\tau_2.\] This is Proposition A.1(a). [paper Prop. A.1] Section 8.6.3 gives the exact endpoints.

At fixed \(\beta\), \(\lambda=u\beta/(1+u\beta)\) has derivative \(\beta/(1+u\beta)^2>0\). So the band in \(u\) maps to an open band in the presence probability \(\lambda\). The benchmark \(\lambda\) lies strictly inside it.

The prior window and the boundary cases

Now fix the slot and vary the prior. For any \(0<\tau<1\), convert the top-posterior condition into prior odds: \[\begin{align*} M(\pi)>\tau &\iff \frac{\pi}{1-\pi}(1+u)>\frac{\tau}{1-\tau}\\ &\iff \pi(1+u)(1-\tau)>\tau(1-\pi)\\ &\iff \pi>\frac{\tau}{\tau+(1-\tau)(1+u)}. \end{align*}\] The right side of (H1) gives a lower bound on \(\pi\) with \(\tau=\tau_1\). The ceiling (H3) gives an upper bound on \(\pi\) with \(\tau=\tau_2\). We must also check the other hypotheses. When \(g_{H,0}>g_{L,0}\), the left side of (H1) requires \(\pi<\tau_0\). The right side of (H2) requires \(\kappa(\pi)>\kappa^*:=k/(\rho\Delta_1)\). Both terms in the minimum must exceed \(\kappa^*\): \[\begin{align*} m(\pi)>\kappa^* &\iff \pi(1-\kappa^*)>\kappa^*(1-\pi)(1+u)\\ &\iff\pi>\frac{\kappa^*(1+u)}{(1-\kappa^*)+\kappa^*(1+u)},\\ 1-M(\pi)>\kappa^* &\iff(1-\pi)(1-\kappa^*)>\kappa^*\pi(1+u)\\ &\iff\pi<\frac{1-\kappa^*}{(1-\kappa^*)+\kappa^*(1+u)}. \end{align*}\] At the benchmark, this trading interval contains both the full prior window and the window for parts (i) and (ii). So the full prior window is the interval from the (H1) bound to the (H3) bound. Without (H3), parts (i) and (ii) need \(\pi\) between the (H1) bound and \(\tau_0\). Section 8.6.4 gives the endpoints.

The slot band describes strict hypotheses, rather than every boundary outcome. Read the cases from the bounds on \(u\) at the prior one half.

  • Below the band, (H2) still forces full orders at \(r_1\), but \(M<\tau_1\). Entry at \(r_1\) is then \(\rho\).

  • Above the band, while the trading bound still holds, full orders persist at both stronger strengths. A purchase at \(r_2\) recruits under C1, so the return to the floor fails.

  • Where the right side of (H2) fails, this fact alone specifies no complete equilibrium outcome.

At the lower boundary, \(M=\tau_1\). Orders at \(r_0\) remain uniquely zero, because \(\Delta_0<k\) does not depend on the slot. At the benchmark, the trading margin \(\rho\kappa\Delta_1-k\) stays positive at this boundary. So orders at \(r_1\) and \(r_2\) remain uniquely full. Hypothesis (H3) remains strict. A purchase at \(r_1\) alone now creates a challenger tie. C1 gives total entry \(\mathsf{E}_1\) at that boundary slot. CM allows \(e(1)\in[\rho,1]\), so total entry is \[\mathsf{E}=\rho+[e(1)-\rho](b+\pi\lambda)\in[\rho,\mathsf{E}_1].\] This boundary value of \(\mathsf{E}_1\) differs from the benchmark value, because the boundary slot has a different \(u\) and \(\lambda\).

Why H1 needs the weak prior, and why the region is open

The weaker condition \(B_1(\pi)<c_H\) cannot replace \(B_0(\pi)<c_H\). Keep the benchmark slot and other primitives, but lower the high cost and move the very strong incumbent. A suitable choice puts \(c_H\) between \(B_1(\pi)\) and \(B_0(\pi)\). Then every inequality of the weaker list holds strictly. But the omitted weak-prior comparison has the opposite sign: \(B_0(\pi)>c_H\). Orders at \(r_0\) are still uniquely zero by (H2). The posterior is therefore \(\pi\), where both cost types prepare. Entry at \(r_0\) is one, and entry at \(r_1\) is below one. Entry falls, so the intended comparison fails. [paper Appendix A.3] Section 8.6.5 gives the counterexample in numbers.

For uniform incumbents, all five inequalities in (H1)-(H3) concern continuous functions of the primitives. Their intersection is open in the domain \(0<\beta<1/2\). The structural restriction \(\beta\le1/3\) makes that intersection relatively open within the model domain. The benchmark uses \(\beta<1/3\), so it is also an interior point of that restriction. Its five margins in (H1)-(H3) are all strictly positive. These margins prove that the set is nonempty and contains a neighborhood of the benchmark.
