The exact slot band also gives a nonempty interval at each admissible \(\beta\). The margin at the ceiling is the smallest of the five. It measures sensitivity, rather than an equality. Section 8.6.5 lists the five margins.

Panel note: Why no tie rule matters for Proposition 2

The weak investor comparisons are strict because \(\Delta_0<k\). The two stronger investor comparisons are strict because \(k<\rho\kappa\Delta_1\le\rho\kappa\Delta_2\). All high-cost comparisons at the three equilibrium beliefs are also strict. Thus neither the investor rule nor the high-cost challenger rule changes the three outcomes. The fixed zero-cost preparation convention still supplies the entry floor. Boundary cases outside the strict hypotheses can depend on tie rules, as the previous subsection shows.

Previous: The investor’s problemNext: Controls: information is the cause

7Controls: information is the cause

A panel will ask: “How do you know that the rise in entry comes from information, and not from something else?” The paper answers with one theorem and two controls.

  • The theorem [paper Prop. A.3]: if what the challenger learns is held fixed, a stronger incumbent cannot raise entry.

  • Control 1 [paper Table 3, Panel B]: hold the full orders fixed at both strengths. Entry does not rise.

  • Control 2 [paper Table 3, Panel C]: hide the price from the challenger. Entry does not rise.

In equilibrium, entry rises from the weak economy \(r_0\) to the strong economy \(r_1\). So the rise needs the change in information that trade produces.

Deterrence at a fixed information experiment

An information experiment is the joint law of the class \(j\) and a signal \(S\) that the challenger observes. With fixed full orders, the price reveals \(S=X\). The theorem allows a general signal. Economy 1 has the stronger incumbent: \(F_1\le F_0\), a first-order stochastic strengthening.

Result: Fixed information [paper Prop. A.3]

Fix the joint law of the class \(j\) and the signal \(S\). Fix the class value laws \(G_H,G_L\) and the cost law. In each economy \(i\in\{0,1\}\), require \[\begin{equation} \tag{7.1}\htmlId{eq:07-product}{} \mathcal L_i(\theta,R\mid j,S)=G_j\otimes F_i , \end{equation}\] and let the cost \(C\) be independent of the class, both values and \(S\). If the incumbent strengthens from \(F_0\) to \(F_1\), entry does not rise under rule C1 (an indifferent high-cost challenger prepares). Its decrease is exactly \[\mathsf{E}_0-\mathsf{E}_1=\Pr\bigl\{B_{F_1}(\mu(S))<C\le B_{F_0}(\mu(S))\bigr\},\qquad \mu(S)=\Pr(j=H\mid S),\] and it is strict exactly when this probability is positive.

Read the product condition (7.1) as three parts. Each part matters.

  1. Given \((j,S)\), the challenger’s value \(\theta\) has the class law \(G_j\): the signal tells nothing about \(\theta\) beyond the class.

  2. Given \((j,S)\), the incumbent’s value \(R\) has the law \(F_i\): the signal and the class tell nothing about the incumbent.

  3. Given \((j,S)\), the two values are independent.

The independent cost makes \(\mu(S)\) the whole class belief at the moment of decision.

Caution: Fix the full conditional law

A fixed class posterior alone does not fix the information about either acquisition value. Use the product law (7.1). The two examples below show why both conditional marginal laws matter.

The proof, step by step

Step 1: the belief. The challenger decides on \((S,C)\). The cost is independent of \((j,\theta,R,S)\), so \(\Pr(j=H\mid S,C)=\Pr(j=H\mid S)=\mu(S)\). The joint law of \((j,S)\) is the same in both economies, so \(\mu(\cdot)\) is the same function in both.

Step 2: gross profit after the signal. A prepared challenger keeps \((\theta-\max\{p,R\})_+\); this is zero when \(\theta<p\) (Section 3). By (P1)-(P3), \((\theta,R)\) has the law \(G_j\otimes F_i\) given \((j,S)\). So \[\mathbb{E}_i\bigl[(\theta-\max\{p,R\})_+\mid j,S\bigr]=\int\!\!\int(y-\max\{p,x\})_+\,dF_i(x)\,dG_j(y)=g_j(F_i),\] which does not depend on \(S\). Average over the class with the weights of Step 1: \[\mathbb{E}_i\bigl[(\theta-\max\{p,R\})_+\mid S\bigr]=\mu(S)\,g_H(F_i)+[1-\mu(S)]\,g_L(F_i)=B_{F_i}(\mu(S)).\]

Step 3: the preparation rule. Under rule C1 the challenger prepares exactly when \(C\le B_{F_i}(\mu(S))\).

Step 4: the pointwise indicator difference. Proposition 1(a) gives \(B_{F_1}(\mu)\le B_{F_0}(\mu)\) at every belief. Fix one realization, and write \(b_1=B_{F_1}(\mu(S))\le b_0=B_{F_0}(\mu(S))\). If \(C\le b_1\), both indicators are one. If \(b_1<C\le b_0\), only the first is one. If \(C>b_0\), both are zero. So \[\begin{equation*} \mathbf{1}\{C\le B_{F_0}(\mu(S))\}-\mathbf{1}\{C\le B_{F_1}(\mu(S))\}=\mathbf{1}\{B_{F_1}(\mu(S))<C\le B_{F_0}(\mu(S))\}\ge0 . \tag*{\textsf{\small[paper Prop. A.3, proof]}} \end{equation*}\]

Step 5: the exact decrease. The law of \((S,C)\) is the same in both economies: the law of \(S\) is fixed, and \(C\) is independent of \(S\) with a fixed law. Take expectations of Step 4 under this one law. The left side becomes \(\mathsf{E}_0-\mathsf{E}_1\); the right side becomes the probability in the proposition.

Step 6: a common tie probability. Let an indifferent high-cost challenger prepare with a probability \(q\) that is the same in both economies. Each indicator becomes \(\mathbf{1}\{C<B\}+q\,\mathbf{1}\{C=B\}\). With \(b_1\le b_0\), the difference is \(0\) for \(C<b_1\) and for \(C>b_0\); \(1-q\) at \(C=b_1<b_0\); \(1\) for \(b_1<C<b_0\); \(q\) at \(C=b_0>b_1\); and \(0\) when \(b_0=b_1\). Each value is nonnegative, so entry does not rise here either.

Intuition: Why fixed information gives deterrence

Hold fixed what the challenger can learn. Then its belief after each signal is the same in both economies. A stronger rival lowers gross profit at that belief. It can only remove a reason to prepare; it never adds one. So any rise in entry must come through a change in what the challenger learns.

Why each part of the condition matters: two examples

Each example removes one restriction, (P1) or (P2). In both, the joint law of the class and the signal is fixed, the class value laws are fixed, the cost is independent and the incumbent strengthens. Entry still rises, because the signal carries information about a value. Section 8.7 gives the numbers of both examples.

Example A: the signal tells the challenger about the incumbent (breaks P2).

The incumbent value is a half-half mixture of a low component and a high component. The stronger incumbent moves the high component up, so \(F_1\le F_0\). Both point-mass classes lie above every incumbent value, so the challenger always wins and pays \(\max\{p,R\}\). In economy 0 a fair binary signal reports the component of \(R\). In economy 1 the signal is independent of \(R\). In both, the signal is independent of the class, so \(\mu(S)\) stays at the prior.

  • Economy 0: after the low component, gross profit is high and a high-cost challenger prepares. After the high component, gross profit falls below \(c_H\), and it stays out.

  • Economy 1: the signal does not reveal the high component. The average gross profit clears \(c_H\) after every signal, so a high-cost challenger always prepares.

Unconditional gross profit falls, as Proposition 1 requires. But entry rises: the signal in economy 0 told the challenger when the rival was strong, and economy 1 removes that warning.

Example B: the signal tells the challenger about its value within a class (breaks P1).

Each class value law has two point masses, a lower value and an upper value. The class \(j\) and the signal \(S\) are independent fair binary variables, so \(\mu(S)\) stays at the prior. The incumbent is uniform and independent of every other draw, so (P2) and (P3) hold. Every value exceeds every incumbent value.

  • Economy 0: \(\theta\) is independent of \(S\) within each class. Gross profit after each signal lies just below \(c_H\), so only a zero-cost challenger prepares.

  • Economy 1: the incumbent is a little stronger, and \(S\) selects the lower or the upper value of the class. After the upper signal, gross profit clears \(c_H\), and a high-cost challenger prepares.

The class gross profits fall, but entry rises. The class belief never moves; the value information does the work.

The third part and the cost.

Part (P3) lets Step 2 integrate \(R\) against \(F_i\) at each \(\theta\). If the values were correlated given the signal, the rent after the signal would not be \(g_j(F_i)\). A cost that is informative about the class or the values would act as a second signal; the condition would then have to hold for \((S,C)\) in place of \(S\).

Control 1: full orders held fixed

Proposition A.3 compares economies with the same experiment. On the equilibrium path the experiment changes with \(r\) (no trade at \(r_0\), full orders at \(r_1\)), so the theorem says nothing about that comparison. The two controls build the comparison that the theorem covers.

The control fixes \(\lambda\), \(\beta\), \(\pi=1/2\), the cost law and the full orders \((\delta_1,\delta_{-1})\) at every strength, and lets the challenger and the market makers respond [paper App. A.6].

Step 1: posteriors. Under full orders, \(f_H=(b,b_0,b+\lambda)\) and \(f_L=(b+\lambda,b_0,b)\) on \((-1,0,1)\), with \(b=(1-\lambda)\beta\) and \(b_0=(1-\lambda)(1-2\beta)\). Bayes’ rule gives \((m,\tfrac12,M)\) (Section 4).

Step 2: the price reveals the order. Entry is \(e_r(\mu)=\rho+(1-\rho)\mathbf{1}\{B_r(\mu)\ge c_H\}\) and the price is \(P(x)=t_0+e_r(\mu(x))\,w(\mu(x))\), with \(w(\mu)=t_L-t_0+\Delta_T\mu\). Entry is nondecreasing in the posterior, so the three prices differ (Section 4). So the signal is \(S=X\), and its law given the class, \(f_j\), does not depend on \(r\).

Step 3: the product condition holds. The order depends only on the class and the slot draws, and the slot draws are independent of \((\theta,R)\). Given \(j\), \(\theta\sim G_j\) and \(R\sim F_r\) are independent. So \(\mathcal L(\theta,R\mid j,X)=G_j\otimes F_r\), and Proposition A.3 applies to every pair of strengths.

Step 4: which posterior recruits. Section 3.4 derives \(\tau\) and proves that it rises. It lies above the prior already at the start of the benchmark path. Thus neither a sale nor zero recruits on the benchmark path. A purchase recruits exactly when \(\tau(r)\le M\), or \(r\le r_C\), the entry ceiling. The equality uses rule C1; Section 9.8 gives the alternative at \(r_C\).

Step 5: entry. A purchase has probability \(\tfrac12(b+\lambda)+\tfrac12b=b+\lambda/2\). So frozen entry is \(\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda/2)\) on the benchmark path up to \(r_C\), and \(\rho\) for \(r>r_C\).

Step 6: the crossing probabilities. Check \(\{B_{r_b}(\mu(X))<C\le B_{r_a}(\mu(X))\}\) order by order.

  • \(r_0\to r_1\). A zero cost prepares in both economies. After a purchase, a high cost prepares in both, because \(B_{r_1}(M)\ge c_H\) and \(B_{r_0}(M)\ge B_{r_1}(M)\). After a sale or the zero order it prepares in neither, because \(B_{r_0}(1/2)<c_H\). The event is empty: its probability is zero.

  • \(r_1\to r_2\). The event is \(\{X=1,\ C=c_H\}\), because \(B_{r_2}(M)<c_H\le B_{r_1}(M)\). Its probability is \((1-\rho)(b+\lambda/2)\), exactly the fall in entry.

Step 7: at \(r_0\) the frozen profile is a control, not an equilibrium. There \(e(1)=1\), because \(B_{r_0}(M)\ge B_{r_1}(M)\ge c_H\). The informed orders earn \[\begin{align*} \text{purchase: }&\Delta_T(r_0)(1-M)-k,\\ \text{sale: }&\rho\,\Delta_T(r_0)\,m-k. \end{align*}\] Each gross return is below \(\Delta_T(r_0)\), and (H2) gives \(\Delta_T(r_0)<k\). Both net returns are negative, so the investor would abstain. At \(r_1\) the frozen tuple is the unique equilibrium, because the trading bound forces full orders there (Section 6).

Panel note: Flat entry under fixed information

The frozen experiment gives only three posteriors. Only the top posterior can recruit on this path. A higher threshold leaves entry unchanged until it exceeds that posterior. Thus frozen entry stays at one level through \(r_C\), then falls to the floor \(\rho\). The old Laplace experiment had a continuum of intermediate posteriors. Its frozen entry could fall as the threshold moved within that continuum.

Control 2: the hidden price

Now the challenger cannot see the price; the investor and the market makers act as before.

  1. Entry. The challenger’s belief stays at \(1/2\). The high-cost window (H1) gives \(B_{r_0}(1/2)<c_H\), and Proposition 1(a) gives \(B_{r_1}(1/2)\le B_{r_0}(1/2)\). So entry is \(\rho\) after every order at both strengths.

  2. Orders at \(r_0\). The gross return of a correctly signed nonzero order is at most \(\Delta_T(r_0)\), and (H2) gives \(\Delta_T(r_0)<k\). A wrong-signed order earns at most \(-k\). Both classes abstain.

  3. Orders at \(r_1\). The trading bound holds for any entry in \([\rho,1]\), so with \(e\equiv\rho\). The gross returns of the two correctly signed nonzero orders satisfy \(K_H=\rho\Delta_T(r_1)[1-\mu(1)]\ge\rho\Delta_T(r_1)m\) and \(K_L=\rho\Delta_T(r_1)\mu(-1)\ge\rho\Delta_T(r_1)m\). At the prior one half, \(m=\kappa(1/2)\), so (H2) gives \(\rho m\Delta_T(r_1)>k\). Both classes trade fully. The sale margin equals that of the equilibrium at \(r_1\), because a sale meets entry \(\rho\) in both games.

  4. Outcome. At \(r_1\) the three prices differ: the price is informative, but the challenger does not see it. Entry is \(\rho\), high-class ownership is \(\rho/2\), and proceeds are \(t_0+\rho\,w(1/2)\) at both strengths.

Each tuple is the unique equilibrium of the game in which the challenger decides on its prior. Section 8.7 gives the benchmark values of both controls.

Result: The identification logic

At fixed information, entry cannot rise with incumbent strength. The equilibrium rise therefore needs the change in information that trade produces. The two controls isolate this change: frozen full orders keep entry flat; a hidden price keeps entry at its floor.

Previous: The main result: Proposition 2Next: The benchmark in numbers
Part II

The benchmark and interactive evidence

8The benchmark in numbers

Part I gives each result in words and in general symbols. This chapter puts numbers on those results at the benchmark. The graphs of the strength path in Sections 9 and 10 use these numbers. The subsections follow the order of Part I. Each one points back to the chapter that it checks.

The benchmark primitives

The benchmark has \(h=10\), \(\ell=1\), \(\pi=1/2\), \(p=1/2\), \(\rho=1/4\), \(c_H=6\) and \(k=1/50\). Money is in units of \(\ell\), so \(\ell=1\). The class values are point masses at \(h\) and \(\ell\). The incumbent is uniform on \([0,r]\). The three economies of Proposition 2 have \(r_0=6/5\), \(r_1=3\) and \(r_2=18/5\). The slot has \(1+u=\mathrm{e}\) and \(\beta=0.294655\) (Section 8.4). Section B lists every parameter vector of the guide.

The auction payoffs

This subsection puts the benchmark values into the auction algebra of Section 3.

The threshold and its derivative.

At the benchmark primitives, the general threshold (3.1) and its derivative (3.2) become \[\begin{equation} \tag{8.1}\htmlId{eq:benchmark-tau}{} \tau(r)=\frac{48r-3}{4(20r-r^2-1)},\qquad \tau'(r)=\frac{3(8r^2-r+2)}{2(20r-r^2-1)^2}>0\qquad(1<r<10). \end{equation}\] At \(r=1\), \(\tau=5/8>1/2\). So the threshold lies above the prior along the whole benchmark path.

Derivatives at the strong economy.

At \(r_1=3\), \(\Delta_T'=4/9\), \(g_H'=-35/72\), \(g_L'=-1/24\), and \(\partial B_r(1/2)/\partial r=-19/72\).

Benchmark numbers (Table 1 of the paper)

At the benchmark primitives, the formulas give Table 5. Each entry is an exact fraction; the decimal is the paper’s.

\(\tau(r)\) (8.1) \(195/308=0.633117\) \(141/200=0.705000\) \(4245/5804=0.731392\)

The row for \(B_r(1/2)\) uses \((g_H+g_L)/2=\tfrac12\bigl(10-\tfrac r2-\tfrac1{8r}+\tfrac3{8r}\bigr) =5-\tfrac r4+\tfrac1{8r}\).

Note the size of the change from \(r_0\) to \(r_1\). The spread rises \(40\) times (\((2/3)/(1/60)=40\)). Gross profit at the prior falls by \(41/80\), about \(10.7\) percent.

The threshold rises from 0.633117 to 0.705000, as (3.2) requires.

At \(r_1=3\), entry adds \(w_L=t_L-t_0=11/24\) in class \(L\) and \(w_H=t_H-t_0=9/8\) in class \(H\). The surplus split holds: \(\Delta_T+g_H-g_L=2/3+203/24-1/8=16/24+203/24-3/24=216/24=9=h-\ell\).

Proposition 1 off the benchmark.

The script verify/03_auction.py checks [paper (8)] for a non-uniform \(F(y)=(y/\bar r)^2\) and classes with an atom: the formula and a direct double expectation agree to twelve digits.

The exclusion-reserve example.

Set \(p=101/100\) in the example of Section 3.6. The spread falls for \(r<\sqrt3\,p\approx1.749\). So from \(r=6/5\) to \(r=3/2\) the spread falls, from \(6921/8000=0.865125\) to \(7601/10000=0.7601\). It rises again later and reaches \(20001/20000\) at \(r=3\).

The slot and the beliefs

This subsection gives the numbers for the slot algebra of Section 4.

An order law with wrong-signed orders.

Take \(\lambda=1/3\), \(\beta=1/4\), and \(\pi=2/5\). Let the profiles on \((-1,0,1)\) be \[\sigma_H=(1/5,3/10,1/2),\qquad \sigma_L=(3/5,3/10,1/10).\] Both profiles include a wrong-signed order. Direct substitution gives \[f_H=(7/30,13/30,1/3),\qquad f_L=(11/30,13/30,1/5).\] The posteriors are \((14/47,2/5,10/19)\). The same formulas apply before we ask whether these orders are optimal.

The zero-order bound fails when \(\beta>1/3\).

Let class \(H\) always abstain and class \(L\) always sell. Take the exact example \(\lambda=6/17\), \(\beta=2/5\), and \(\pi=1/2\). The masses and ratios are \[b=\frac{22}{85},\quad b_0=\frac{11}{85},\quad \lambda=\frac{30}{85},\quad 1+u=\frac{26}{11},\quad \frac{f_H(0)}{f_L(0)}=\frac{41}{11}.\] Consequently, \[M=\frac{26}{37},\qquad \mu(0)=\frac{41}{52},\qquad \mu(0)-M=\frac{165}{1924}>0.\]

The benchmark slot.

In the benchmark, \(\beta=<span class="q" data-q="beta_B" data-value="294655/1000000" title="0.294655 | registry beta_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.294655\) and \(1+u=\mathrm{e}\). With this choice, the top posterior equals the Laplace value at scale \(2\) (Section 11.5). The presence probability is derived, rather than separately rounded: \[\lambda=\frac{u\beta}{1+u\beta}=0.336122.\] Thus \(b=<span class="q" data-q="b_B" data-value="0.1956150401803150" title="0.195615 | registry b_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.195615\) and \(b+\lambda=<span class="q" data-q="b_plus_lambda_B" data-value="0.5317368090954362" title="0.531737 | registry b_plus_lambda_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.531737\). At \(\pi=<span class="q" data-q="prim_pi" data-value="1/2" title="1/2 | registry prim_pi | scripts/W1/w1_registry.py">1/2\), \[m=\frac1{1+\mathrm{e}}=0.268941,\qquad M=\frac{\mathrm{e}}{1+\mathrm{e}}=0.731059.\] The script keeps the full value of \(\lambda\) throughout the calculation.

Prices at the strong benchmark.

At \(r_1=<span class="q" data-q="prim_r1" data-value="3" title="3 | registry prim_r1 | scripts/W1/w1_registry.py">3\), \(t_0=5/12\), \(t_L-t_0=11/24\), and \(\Delta_T=2/3\). The threshold is \(\tau=141/200=<span class="q" data-q="tau_r1" data-value="141/200" title="0.705000 | registry tau_r1 | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.705000\). Under full orders, the benchmark gives \[\begin{array}{c|ccc} x&-1&0&1\\ \hline \mu(x)&0.268941&1/2&0.731059\\ e(x)&1/4&1/4&1\\ P(x)&0.576074&0.614583&1.362372 \end{array}\] Only the purchase posterior exceeds \(\tau\). The script substitutes these prices into the inverse formula and recovers all three posteriors.

Run python3 slot/study_guide/verify/04_slot_beliefs.py from the repository root. It prints the order laws, the zero-order counterexample, the posterior bounds, and the price inversion.

The investor’s orders at the benchmark

These values check the trading bounds of Section 5.

A mixed candidate inside the bounds.

For a concrete candidate that is not an equilibrium, use the mixed profile of Section 8.4 at \(r=3\), where \(\Delta_T=2/3\). Every posterior is below \(\tau=141/200\), so set \(e\equiv\rho=1/4\). The three posteriors differ, so the three prices differ and the price beliefs equal the order posteriors. Thus (E1)-(E4) and (E6) hold, although the mixed orders are not optimal. Here \(u=2\), \(m(2/5)=2/11\), and \(1-M(2/5)=1/3\), so \(\kappa=2/11\). The exact gross returns are \[\rho\kappa\Delta_T=\frac14\cdot\frac2{11}\cdot\frac23=\frac1{33},\qquad K_H=\frac14\cdot\frac23\left(1-\frac{10}{19}\right)=\frac3{38},\qquad K_L=\frac14\cdot\frac23\cdot\frac{14}{47}=\frac7{141}.\] Each lies between \(1/33\) and \(2/3\), as the bound requires without investor optimality.

The floor factor at other priors.

At \(1+u=\mathrm{e}\), \(\kappa(1/2)=<span class="q" data-q="m_half_B" data-value="0.2689414213699951" title="0.268941 | registry m_half_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.268941\). At \(\pi=1/4\) and \(3/4\), it equals approximately \(0.109232\).

The trading-cost window at the benchmark.

At the weak benchmark, \(\Delta_T(r_0)=1/60\) and \(k=1/50\). Hence the largest possible net return is at most \[\frac1{60}-\frac1{50}=-\frac1{300}<0.\] At the strong benchmark, \[\rho\kappa\Delta_T(r_1)-k =\frac1{6(1+\mathrm{e})}-\frac1{50} =0.024824>0.\] In the full-order candidate, \(e(1)=1\) and \(e(-1)=1/4\). Thus \(K_H=2/[3(1+\mathrm{e})]\) and \(K_L=1/[6(1+\mathrm{e})]\). Their net returns are \(<span class="q" data-q="p2_base_r1_choice_h" data-value="0.1592942809133300804992271721187758170899035732233" title="0.159294 | registry p2_base_r1_choice_h | audit/slot_manuscript_2026_10_23/scripts/P2_auction/derive.py">0.159294\) and \(<span class="q" data-q="p2_base_r1_choice_l" data-value="0.024823570228332520124806793029693954272475893305824" title="0.024824 | registry p2_base_r1_choice_l | audit/slot_manuscript_2026_10_23/scripts/P2_auction/derive.py">0.024824\).

Run python3 slot/study_guide/verify/05_investor.py from the repository root. It prints the six payoffs in each benchmark economy, the return bounds, and the prior symmetry. It also prints the mixed candidate’s exact returns.

The main result at the benchmark

This subsection evaluates the steps of Section 6 at the benchmark.

Entry and ownership

At \(r_1=<span class="q" data-q="prim_r1" data-value="3" title="3 | registry prim_r1 | scripts/W1/w1_registry.py">3\), Table 5 gives \(g_L=1/8\) and \(g_H=203/24\), so \(\tau_1=(6-1/8)/(25/3)=141/200\). Since \(\pi<\tau_1<M\), only the purchase recruits. With \(\beta=<span class="q" data-q="beta_B" data-value="294655/1000000" title="0.294655 | registry beta_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.294655\) and \(u=\mathrm{e}-1\), the script gives \[\begin{align*} \lambda&=0.336122,& b&=0.195615,& b+\lambda&=0.531737,\\ e_H&=0.648803,& e_L&=0.396711,& \mathsf{E}_1&=0.522757. \end{align*}\] Finally \(\mathsf{O}_{H,1}=\pi e_H=<span class="q" data-q="own_r1_B" data-value="0.3244013034107886" title="0.324401 | registry own_r1_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.324401\). The weak values are \(\mathsf{E}_0=<span class="q" data-q="entry_r0" data-value="1/4" title="0.250000 | registry entry_r0 | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.250000\) and \(\mathsf{O}_{H,0}=<span class="q" data-q="own_r0" data-value="1/8" title="0.125000 | registry own_r0 | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.125000\).

The very strong economy and the entry ceiling

At \(r_2=<span class="q" data-q="prim_r2" data-value="18/5" title="18/5 | registry prim_r2 | scripts/W1/w1_registry.py">18/5\), gross profit at the top posterior is \[B_{r_2}(M)=\frac5{48}+\frac{1451}{180}M =5.997311<6.\] The cost margin is \(<span class="q" data-q="margin_return_B" data-value="0.002688902265905112" title="0.002689 | registry margin_return_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.002689\). Entry is \(\rho\), yet the three prices still differ (Table 6).

For the uniform benchmark, expand gross profit at the top posterior: \[\begin{align*} B_r(M) &=(1-M)\frac{\ell^2-p^2}{2r} +M\left(h-\frac r2-\frac{p^2}{2r}\right)\\ &=Mh-\frac{Mr}{2}+\frac{(1-M)\ell^2-p^2}{2r}. \end{align*}\] Each class profit falls strictly with \(r\), so this weighted average also falls strictly. The entry ceiling \(r_C\) solves \(B_{r_C}(M)=c_H\). Multiplication by \(2r_C\) gives \[Mr_C^2-2(Mh-c_H)r_C-[(1-M)\ell^2-p^2]=0.\] In the benchmark, \((1-M)\ell^2-p^2=1/(1+\mathrm{e})-1/4>0\), so the product of the two roots, \(-[(1-M)\ell^2-p^2]/M\), is negative. The positive root is \[r_C=\frac{Mh-c_H+\sqrt{(Mh-c_H)^2+M[(1-M)\ell^2-p^2]}}{M} =3.592659.\]

The admissible band at the prior one half

Fix the benchmark auction, costs, and strengths, and let \(\pi=1/2\). The benchmark threshold (8.1) gives \[\tau(r_0)=\frac{195}{308},\qquad \tau(r_1)=\frac{141}{200},\qquad \tau(r_2)=\frac{4245}{5804},\] and Table 5 gives \(\Delta_0=1/60\), \(\Delta_1=2/3\), and \(\Delta_2=169/180\). The weak-economy margins do not depend on the slot: \[c_H-B_0(1/2)=6-\frac{1153}{240}=\frac{287}{240}>0,\qquad k-\Delta_0=\frac1{50}-\frac1{60}=\frac1{300}>0.\] The right side of (H1) becomes \[\begin{align*} M>\frac{141}{200} &\iff 200(1+u)>141(2+u)\\ &\iff 59u>82 \iff u>\frac{82}{59}. \end{align*}\] The ceiling (H3) becomes \[\begin{align*} M<\frac{4245}{5804} &\iff5804(1+u)<4245(2+u)\\ &\iff1559u<2686 \iff u<\frac{2686}{1559}. \end{align*}\] The right side of (H2) becomes \[\begin{align*} \frac1{50}<\frac1{6(2+u)} &\iff6(2+u)<50 \iff u<\frac{19}{3}. \end{align*}\] Equivalently, it requires \(M<22/25\). Since \(2686/1559<19/3\), the trading bound is redundant within the narrower band. We therefore obtain the exact equivalence \[\text{(H1)-(H3)} \iff \frac{82}{59}<u<\frac{2686}{1559} \iff \frac{141}{200}<M<\frac{4245}{5804}.\] The equivalent presence interval is \[\frac{82\beta}{59+82\beta}<\lambda<\frac{2686\beta}{1559+2686\beta}.\] At \(\beta=<span class="q" data-q="beta_B" data-value="294655/1000000" title="0.294655 | registry beta_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.294655\), its endpoints are \(<span class="q" data-q="lambda_lo_B" data-value="2416171/8316171" title="0.290539 | registry lambda_lo_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.290539\) and \(<span class="q" data-q="lambda_hi_B" data-value="79144333/235044333" title="0.336721 | registry lambda_hi_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.336721\). The benchmark \(\lambda=<span class="q" data-q="lambda_B" data-value="0.3361217689151212" title="0.336122 | registry lambda_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.336122\) lies strictly between them.

The prior window and the boundary cases

Now fix \(1+u=\mathrm{e}\) and vary the prior. The right side of (H1) and (H3) give \[\frac{141}{141+59\mathrm{e}}<\pi<\frac{4245}{4245+1559\mathrm{e}}.\] The endpoints are approximately \(<span class="q" data-q="prior_lo_B" data-value="0.4678500296861485" title="0.467850 | registry prior_lo_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.467850\) and \(<span class="q" data-q="prior_hi_B" data-value="0.5004243073200632" title="0.500424 | registry prior_hi_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.500424\). The left side of (H1) requires \(\pi<\tau(r_0)=195/308\). The right side of (H2) requires \[\kappa(\pi)>\frac{k}{\rho\Delta_1}=\frac{1/50}{(1/4)(2/3)}=\frac3{25}.\] Both terms in the minimum must exceed \(3/25\): \[\begin{align*} m(\pi)>\frac3{25} &\iff 25\pi>3[\pi+(1-\pi)\mathrm{e}]\\ &\iff\pi>\frac{3\mathrm{e}}{22+3\mathrm{e}},\\ 1-M(\pi)>\frac3{25} &\iff25(1-\pi)>3[1-\pi+\pi\mathrm{e}]\\ &\iff\pi<\frac{22}{22+3\mathrm{e}}. \end{align*}\] This interval has endpoints \(<span class="q" data-q="prior_kappa_lo_B" data-value="0.2704323419376044" title="0.270432 | registry prior_kappa_lo_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.270432\) and \(<span class="q" data-q="prior_kappa_hi_B" data-value="0.7295676580623956" title="0.729568 | registry prior_kappa_hi_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.729568\). It contains both the full prior window and the window for parts (i) and (ii). The full intersection therefore equals the first prior interval above. Without (H3), parts (i) and (ii) require exactly \[\frac{141}{141+59\mathrm{e}}<\pi<\frac{195}{308}.\]

The boundary cases at the prior one half read as follows. For \(u<82/59\), (H2) still forces full orders at \(r_1\), but \(M<\tau(r_1)\). For \(2686/1559\le u<19/3\), full orders persist at both stronger strengths, and a purchase at \(r_2\) recruits under C1. For \(u\ge19/3\), the right side of (H2) fails.

At the lower boundary \(u=82/59\), the posteriors satisfy \(M=141/200\) and \(m=59/200\). Orders at \(r_0\) remain uniquely zero because \(\Delta_0-k=-1/300\). Orders at \(r_1\) and \(r_2\) remain uniquely full because \[\rho\kappa\Delta_1-k =\frac14\frac{59}{200}\frac23-\frac1{50}=\frac7{240}>0.\] At \(\beta=<span class="q" data-q="beta_B" data-value="294655/1000000" title="0.294655 | registry beta_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.294655\), the boundary value of \(\mathsf{E}_1\) under C1 is approximately \(0.515737\). It differs from the benchmark value because the boundary slot has a different \(u\) and \(\lambda\).

Why H1 needs the weak prior, and the benchmark margins

Keep the benchmark slot and other primitives, but set \(c_H=<span class="q" data-q="nec_cH" data-value="47/10" title="47/10 | registry nec_cH | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">47/10\) and \(r_2=<span class="q" data-q="nec_r2" data-value="8/1" title="8 | registry nec_r2 | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">8\). The proposed weaker list has positive margins: \[\begin{align*} c_H-B_1(1/2)&=\frac{49}{120}>0,& B_1(M)-c_H&=1.517155>0,\\ k-\Delta_0&=\frac1{300}>0,& \rho\kappa\Delta_1-k&=0.024824>0,\\ c_H-B_2(M)&=0.312465>0. \end{align*}\] But the omitted weak-prior comparison has the opposite sign: \[c_H-B_0(1/2)=\frac{47}{10}-\frac{1153}{240}=-\frac5{48}<0.\] Entry at \(r_0\) is one, and entry at \(r_1\) is \(<span class="q" data-q="entry_r1_B" data-value="0.5227569434784067" title="0.522757 | registry entry_r1_B | scripts/P1_core/p1_numbers.py">0.522757\). Entry falls, so the intended comparison fails.

At the benchmark itself, the five positive margins of (H1)-(H3) are \[\begin{array}{c|c} c_H-B_0(\pi)&1.195833\\ B_1(M)-c_H&0.217155\\ k-\Delta_0&0.003333\\ \rho\kappa\Delta_1-k&0.024824\\ c_H-B_2(M)&0.002689 \end{array}\] These margins prove that the set is nonempty and contains a neighborhood of the benchmark. The small margin at the ceiling measures sensitivity, rather than an equality.

Run python3 slot/study_guide/verify/06_main_result.py from the repository root. It prints the three tuples, auction quantities, entry, ownership, exact band, prior window, ceiling, and H1 counterexample.

The controls at the benchmark

This subsection puts the benchmark numbers into the theorem and the two controls of Section 7.

The two examples in numbers

verify/07_controls.py recomputes both examples with exact fractions.

Example A (breaks P2).

Take \(p=1/2\), point-mass classes \(12\) and \(11\) with equal priors, \(\rho=1/4\) and \(c_H=6\). Let \(F_0=\tfrac12U[0,1]+\tfrac12U[8,9]\) and \(F_1=\tfrac12U[0,1]+\tfrac12U[9,10]\). The upper component moves up, so \(F_1\le F_0\). In both economies \(\mu(S)=1/2\) and \(\mathbb{E}[\theta\mid S]=23/2\). The expected payment is \(\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac12+\int_{1/2}^1x\,dx=\tfrac58\) on \(U[0,1]\), \(\tfrac{17}2\) on \(U[8,9]\) and \(\tfrac{19}2\) on \(U[9,10]\).

Economy 0: signal reports the component Economy 1: signal independent of \(R\)
Gross profit after the signal \(\tfrac{23}2-\tfrac58=\tfrac{87}8\) or \(\tfrac{23}2-\tfrac{17}2=3\) \(\tfrac{23}2-\tfrac12\bigl(\tfrac58+\tfrac{19}2\bigr)=\tfrac{103}{16}\) after either signal
High-cost preparation after the low component only after every signal
Entry \(\tfrac14+\tfrac34\cdot\tfrac12=\tfrac58\) \(1\)

Unconditional gross profit falls from \(111/16\) to \(103/16\). In economy 0 the profit after the strong component, \(3\), fails \(c_H\). In economy 1 the average profit \(103/16\) clears \(c_H=6\).

Example B (breaks P1).

Take \(G_L=\tfrac12\delta_1+\tfrac12\delta_3\), \(G_H=\tfrac12\delta_2+\tfrac12\delta_4\), \(p=1/2\), \(\rho=1/4\) and \(c_H=21/10\). Economy 0 has \(R\sim U[0,1/2]\). Economy 1 has \(R\sim U[0,3/5]\). The expected payment is \(\tfrac12\) on \(U[0,\tfrac12]\), and \(\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac56+\int_{1/2}^{3/5}\tfrac{x}{3/5}\,dx=\tfrac5{12}+\tfrac{11}{120}=\tfrac{61}{120}\) on \(U[0,\tfrac35]\).

Economy 0: \(\theta\) independent of \(S\) Economy 1: \(S\) selects the value
\(\mathbb{E}[\theta\mid S]\) \(\tfrac12\cdot2+\tfrac12\cdot3=\tfrac52\) \(\tfrac32\) (lower) or \(\tfrac72\) (upper)
Gross profit after the signal \(\tfrac52-\tfrac12=2<\tfrac{21}{10}\) \(\tfrac32-\tfrac{61}{120}=\tfrac{119}{120}\) or \(\tfrac72-\tfrac{61}{120}=\tfrac{359}{120}\)
Entry \(\tfrac14\) \(\tfrac14+\tfrac34\cdot\tfrac12=\tfrac58\)

The class gross profits move from \((g_L,g_H)=(3/2,5/2)\) to \((179/120,299/120)\). After the upper signal, gross profit \(359/120\) exceeds \(c_H=21/10\).

Control 1 in numbers

At the benchmark slot, \(m=0.268941\) and \(M=0.731059\). The threshold starts at \(\tau(1)=5/8>1/2\), and the entry ceiling is \(r_C\approx3.592659\).

A purchase has probability \(b+\lambda/2=0.363676\). So frozen entry is \(\tfrac14+\tfrac34\times0.363676=0.522757\) for \(1<r\le r_C\), and \(\rho=0.250000\) for \(r>r_C\).

The crossing probabilities:

  • \(r_0\to r_1\): \(B_{r_1}(M)=6.217155\ge c_H\) and \(B_{r_0}(1/2)=4.804167<c_H\). The probability is \(0\).

  • \(r_1\to r_2\): \(B_{r_2}(M)=5.997311<c_H\). The probability is \(0.272757\), exactly the fall in entry.

At \(r_0\), \(B_{r_0}(M)-c_H=0.879842898>0\). The informed orders earn \[\begin{align*} \text{purchase: }&\Delta_T(r_0)(1-M)-k=\tfrac1{60}\times0.268941-\tfrac1{50}=-0.015517643,\\ \text{sale: }&\rho\,\Delta_T(r_0)\,m-k=\tfrac14\times\tfrac1{60}\times0.268941-\tfrac1{50}=-0.018879411. \end{align*}\] Thus frozen entry stays at 0.522757 through \(r_C\), then falls by 0.272757.

Control 2 in numbers

At \(r_0\), \(\Delta_T(r_0)=1/60<k=1/50\). At \(r_1\), \(\rho m\Delta_T(r_1)=\tfrac14\times0.268941\times\tfrac23=0.044824>k\).

The sale margin is \(0.024823570\), as in the equilibrium at \(r_1\). At \(r_1\) the prices are \(0.576074\), \(0.614583\) and \(0.653093\). Entry is \(0.250000\), ownership \(\rho/2=0.125000\), and proceeds \(t_0+\rho\,w(1/2)\): at \(r_1\), \(\tfrac5{12}+\tfrac14\bigl(\tfrac78-\tfrac5{12}+\tfrac13\bigr)=\tfrac{59}{96}=0.614583\).

The outcomes table decoded

Table 6 collects the comparisons of Table 3 in the paper. Section 8.3 gives the auction inputs. Section 6.5 derives equilibrium entry and ownership. The controls use the same aggregation formulas.

Equilibrium outcomes and information controls [paper Table 3].
Regime Strength Entry High-class ownership Target proceeds
Equilibrium \(r_0\) 0.250000 0.125000 0.392708
\(r_1\) 0.522757 0.324401 0.872531
\(r_2\) 0.250000 0.125000 0.664236
Frozen full orders \(r_0\) 0.522757 0.324401 0.503998
\(r_1\) 0.522757 0.324401 0.872531
Hidden price \(r_0\) 0.250000 0.125000 0.392708
\(r_1\) 0.250000 0.125000 0.614583
Expected proceeds.

\(R_T=t_0+\tfrac12e_Hw_H+\tfrac12e_Lw_L\). At \(r_1\): \(\tfrac5{12}+\tfrac12\times0.648803\times\tfrac98+\tfrac12\times0.396711\times\tfrac{11}{24}=0.872531\). At \(r_0\) and \(r_2\), \(e_H=e_L=\rho\), so \(R_T=t_0+\rho w(1/2)\). The values are \(377/960\) and \(1913/2880\). As a check, competitive prices make \(R_T=\mathbb{E}[P(X)]\). With \(e\equiv\rho\), \(w\) is linear and the posteriors average to \(1/2\), so \(R_T\) equals the zero-order price.

The control rows.

The frozen tuple at \(r_0\) has the same \(e_H\) and \(e_L\) as at \(r_1\), because the same purchase recruits; so entry and ownership repeat the \(r_1\) values. Proceeds use the \(r_0\) inputs: \(\tfrac7{24}+\tfrac12\times0.648803\times\tfrac{33}{80}+\tfrac12\times0.396711\times\tfrac{19}{48}=0.503998\). At \(r_1\) the frozen tuple is the equilibrium. At \(r_2\), frozen full orders give the equilibrium row, since \(r_2>r_C\). The hidden-price control at \(r_0\) also gives the equilibrium row, since its price carries no information.

The main result in numbers

Table 7 puts the numbers on Table 1. It compares the three economies of Proposition 2 at the benchmark.

Gross profit at the prior, \(B_r(1/2)\) 4.804167 4.291667 (lower) 4.134722
Target-payoff spread, \(\Delta_T\) 0.016667 0.666667 (\(40\times\)) 0.938889
Preparation threshold, \(\tau(r)\) 0.633117 0.705000 0.731392
Top posterior, \(M(1/2)\) 0.731059 0.731059 0.731059
Investor orders, classes \(H\) and \(L\) \((0,0)\) \((1,-1)\) \((1,-1)\)
Price informative? no yes yes
High-cost preparation none after a purchase none
Entry \(\mathsf{E}\) 0.250000 0.522757 0.250000
High-class ownership \(\mathsf{O}_H\) 0.125000 0.324401 0.125000

From \(r_0\) to \(r_1\), gross profit falls by about \(10.7\) percent, while the spread rises \(40\) times. Entry more than doubles, by a factor of \(2.09\). At \(r_2\) the price stays informative, but \(\tau(r_2)=0.731392\) lies just above \(M(1/2)=0.731059\). So even a purchase cannot recruit a high-cost challenger, and entry falls back to \(\rho\). Entry is non-monotone in incumbent strength.

Why entry doubles while profit falls.

Profit falls at every belief, but the belief that matters changes. At \(r_0\) the price says nothing, so the challenger decides at the prior \(1/2\), and \(B_{r_0}(1/2)=4.804167<c_H=6\). At \(r_1\) a purchase moves the belief to \(0.731059\), and \(B_{r_1}(M)\) exceeds \(6\) by \(0.217155\). A purchase occurs with probability \(b+\lambda/2\) at the prior \(1/2\), so entry is \(\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda/2)=0.522757\).

Previous: Controls: information is the causeNext: Coexistence: the whole strength path

9Coexistence: the whole strength path

Proposition 2 compares three chosen economies. What happens at the strengths between them? Proposition 3 answers completely for the benchmark auction at the prior \(1/2\). It lists every equilibrium at every strength on the path \([r_0,r_2]\), for every slot in the band of Proposition A.1(a), \(\tau(r_1)<M<\tau(r_2)\). This section derives the six marked strengths, the five equilibrium forms and the entry on each form.

Throughout, \(M=M(1/2)=(1+u)/(2+u)\) and \(m=1-M=1/(2+u)\). The default tie rules hold unless a subsection says otherwise: an indifferent investor may mix over its best orders (rule IM), and an indifferent high-cost challenger prepares (rule C1).

A map of the strength axis

Intuition: The path in one paragraph

Against a weak incumbent, no informed order covers its cost. From \(r_A\) on, a purchase that lifts the belief to the threshold \(\tau\) can pay for itself, because it recruits a high-cost challenger. So an informative equilibrium appears next to no trade. At first the high class mixes, and entry rises with the spread. From \(r_F\) on the high class buys with probability one, and entry is flat. Above \(r_N\) no trade fails: even against an uninformative price a unit order pays. Above \(r_C\) even the top posterior cannot recruit, and entry returns to \(\rho\).

Three tools

The threshold.

Section 3.4 derives \(\tau\) and proves \(\tau'>0\); Section 8.2 gives its benchmark form. Its endpoint values give \[\frac12<\tau(r_0)=195/308\le\tau(r)\le\tau(r_2)=4245/5804<\frac34.\] This is part (a) of [paper Lemma A.4].

The root map.

Many thresholds have the form “\(\Delta_T(r)\) equals a number \(d\)”. Solve it once: \[\frac{(r-\ell)^2}{2r}=d\iff r^2-2(\ell+d)\,r+\ell^2=0\iff r=\ell+d\pm\sqrt{d^2+2\ell d}.\] The root above \(\ell\) is \(\mathfrak r(d)=\ell+d+\sqrt{d^2+2\ell d}\). Since \(\Delta_T'(r)=\tfrac12-\ell^2/(2r^2)>0\) for \(r>\ell\), \(\Delta_T(r)\le d\) holds exactly when \(r\le\mathfrak r(d)\).

The recruitment return \(J\).

A purchase that lifts the posterior exactly to \(\tau\) and recruits (\(e(1)=1\)) earns the gross return \(J(r)=\Delta_T(r)[1-\tau(r)]\). With \(1-\tau=(D_\tau-N_\tau)/D_\tau\), \[J(r)=\frac{(r-\ell)^2\,[2r(h-c_H)-r^2-p^2]}{2r\,(2rh-r^2-\ell^2)} \ \overset{\text{benchmark}}{=}\ \frac{(r-1)^2\,(8r-r^2-\tfrac14)}{2r\,(20r-r^2-1)} .\] \(J\) is the largest gross return of a purchase that recruits. It rises strictly on the path [paper Lemma A.4(b)], with \(J(r_0)=113/18480<k\) and \(\rho J(r_2)=263471/4178880>k\) (Section 9.9 explains the proof).

The six marked strengths

The first five strengths equate an order return with \(k\). The sixth is the entry ceiling.

  1. \(r_A\): \(J(r)=k\). A purchase that just reaches \(\tau\) and recruits earns \(k\). \(J\) rises strictly, \(J(r_0)<k\) and \(J(r_2)>\rho J(r_2)>k\), so the root is unique. Exact-sign bisection brackets the root: \(r_A=1.393958\).

  2. \(r_F=\mathfrak r\bigl(k/(1-M)\bigr)\). A full purchase that recruits earns \(\Delta_T(1-M)\), which equals \(k\) when \(\Delta_T=k/(1-M)=k(2+u)=0.074366\). So \(r_F=1.467127\).

  3. \(r_N=\mathfrak r(2k/\rho)\). Against no informed trade, every posterior is \(1/2\) and entry is \(\rho\), so a unit purchase earns \(\rho\Delta_T(1-\tfrac12)\) and a unit sale earns \(\rho\Delta_T\cdot\tfrac12\). Both equal \(k\) when \(\Delta_T=2k/\rho=0.16\): \(r_N=\frac{29+6\sqrt6}{25}\approx1.747878\).

  4. \(r_J\): \(\rho J(r)=k\). A purchase that just reaches \(\tau\) without recruitment earns \(\rho J\). The root is unique for the same reason as \(r_A\): \(r_J=1.966097\).

  5. \(r_S=\mathfrak r\bigl(k/(\rho m)\bigr)\). A full sale never recruits and earns \(\rho\Delta_Tm\). It equals \(k\) when \(\Delta_T=k(2+u)/\rho=0.297463\). So \(r_S=2.124148\).

  6. \(r_C\): \(\tau(r)=M\), or \(B_r(M)=c_H\). Section 8.6.2 derives its quadratic and the root \[r_C=\frac{Mh-c_H+\sqrt{(Mh-c_H)^2+M[(1-M)\ell^2-p^2]}}{M} \approx3.592659.\]

\(r_A\), \(r_N\) and \(r_J\) do not depend on the slot: \(J\), \(\rho\) and \(k\) contain no \(\lambda\) or \(\beta\). The other three depend on it through \(M\) and \(m\).

Result: The order of the thresholds [paper Lemma A.5]

For every slot in the band, \(r_0<r_A<r_F<r_N<r_J<r_S<r_1<r_C<r_2\).

Inequality Reason Slack at the benchmark
\(r_0<r_A<r_1\) \(J(r_0)<k<\rho J(r_1)<J(r_1)\), \(J\) rises \(\rho J(r_1)-k=7/240\)
\(r_A<r_F\) \((1-M)\Delta_T(r_A)<k=(1-\tau(r_A))\Delta_T(r_A)\iff M>\tau(r_A)\) \(M-\tau(r_A)=0.090320\)
\(r_F<r_N\) \(k/(1-M)<2k/\rho\iff M<1-\rho/2\); true as \(M<3/4\) \(1-\rho/2-M=0.143941\)
\(r_N<r_J\) \(\rho J(r_N)=2k[1-\tau(r_N)]<k\iff\tau(r_N)>1/2\) \(\tau(r_N)-\tfrac12=0.154463\)
\(r_J<r_S\) \(\rho(1-M)\Delta_T(r_J)<k=\rho(1-\tau(r_J))\Delta_T(r_J)\iff M>\tau(r_J)\) \(M-\tau(r_J)=0.068100\)
\(r_S<r_1\) \(\rho(1-M)\Delta_T(r_1)>k\iff(1-M)/6>1/50\iff M<22/25\) \(22/25-M=0.148941\)
\(r_1<r_C<r_2\) \(\tau(r_1)<M<\tau(r_2)\): the band 0.026059; 0.000334

For \(r_N<r_J\): at \(r_N\), \(\rho\Delta_T=2k\), so \(\rho J(r_N)=2k[1-\tau(r_N)]\). For \(r_J<r_S\): \(\rho J(r_1)>k\) gives \(r_J<r_1\), so \(M>\tau(r_1)>\tau(r_J)\).

The investor’s two problems

Fix \(r\) and a prior \(\pi<\tau(r)\). No equilibrium puts weight on a wrong-signed order (Section 5). So a candidate has two numbers: the informed purchase probability \(s=\lambda\sigma_H(1)\) and the informed sale probability \(t=\lambda\sigma_L(-1)\). On the orders \((-1,0,1)\), \(f_H=(b,\ b_0+\lambda-s,\ b+s)\) and \(f_L=(b+t,\ b_0+\lambda-t,\ b)\). Bayes’ rule gives \[\mu_+(s)=\frac{\pi(b+s)}{\pi(b+s)+(1-\pi)b}=\frac{\pi(b+s)}{b+\pi s},\qquad \mu_-(t)=\frac{\pi b}{\pi b+(1-\pi)(b+t)}=\frac{\pi b}{b+(1-\pi)t}.\] On \([0,\lambda]\), \(\mu_+\) rises strictly from \(\pi\) to \(M(\pi)\), and \(\mu_-\) falls strictly from \(\pi\) to \(m(\pi)\). The rate that lifts the posterior exactly to \(\tau\) solves \(\pi(b+s)=\tau(b+\pi s)\): \(s^*=b(\tau-\pi)/[\pi(1-\tau)]\), with \(s^*<\lambda\) exactly when \(\tau<M(\pi)\).

Each class compares its gross return with \(k\) (Section 5). Rate zero needs \(K\le k\). An interior rate needs \(K=k\) and rule IM. A full rate needs \(K\ge k\) under IM, or \(K>k\) under I0. Entry after a purchase is \(\rho\) below \(\tau\), \(1\) above \(\tau\), and at \(\tau\) it is \(1\) under C1 (any value in \([\rho,1]\) under CM).

Result: The high class [paper Lemma A.6]

Take a candidate without wrong-signed orders. The pair \((s,e(1))\) satisfies purchase entry and class-\(H\) optimality exactly in these five cases:

  • \(s=0\), \(e(1)=\rho\), with \(\rho\Delta_T(1-\pi)\le k\);

  • \(0<s<\min\{s^*,\lambda\}\), \(e(1)=\rho\), with \(\rho\Delta_T[1-\mu_+(s)]=k\) (rule IM);

  • \(s^*<s<\lambda\), \(e(1)=1\), with \(\Delta_T[1-\mu_+(s)]=k\) (rule IM);

  • \(s=s^*\in(0,\lambda)\), \(e(1)=e^*:=k/J(r)\) (rule IM; C1 needs \(e^*=1\), that is \(J=k\); CM needs \(\rho\le e^*\le1\));

  • \(s=\lambda\). Entry is \(1\) if \(M(\pi)>\tau\), and \(\rho\) if \(M(\pi)<\tau\). If \(M(\pi)=\tau\), C1 gives \(e(1)=1\); CM allows \(e(1)\in[\rho,1]\). In every case, \(e(1)\Delta_T[1-M(\pi)]\ge k\), with strict inequality under I0.

Derivation. At \(s=0\), \(\mu_+=\pi<\tau\), so \(e(1)=\rho\): (N). At an interior \(s\) below \(s^*\), entry is \(\rho\), and indifference gives (Q); above \(s^*\), entry is \(1\), and indifference gives (R). At \(s=s^*\), indifference reads \(e(1)\Delta_T(1-\tau)=e(1)J=k\): (T). At \(s=\lambda\) the posterior is \(M(\pi)\): (F). Since \(1-\mu_+\) falls strictly, (Q) and (R) each have at most one root.

Result: The low class [paper Lemma A.7]

After a sale, \(\mu_-(t)\le\pi<\tau\), so \(e(-1)=\rho\) in every candidate. Under rule IM exactly one sale rate qualifies: \(s_-^*=0\) if \(\rho\Delta_T\pi\le k\); \(s_-^*=\lambda\) if \(\rho\Delta_Tm(\pi)\ge k\); and otherwise \(s_-^*=b\,(\pi\rho\Delta_T/k-1)/(1-\pi)\). Under rule I0 no rate qualifies when \(\rho\Delta_Tm(\pi)\le k<\rho\Delta_T\pi\).

Derivation. \(K_L=\rho\Delta_T\mu_-(t)\) falls strictly from \(\rho\Delta_T\pi\) at \(t=0\) to \(\rho\Delta_Tm(\pi)\) at \(t=\lambda\). In the first case \(K_L<k\) for every \(t>0\); in the second \(K_L>k\) for every \(t<\lambda\). Otherwise the intermediate value theorem gives one interior root: \(\rho\Delta_T\pi b/[b+(1-\pi)t]=k\iff b+(1-\pi)t=\pi b\rho\Delta_T/k\iff t=b(\pi\rho\Delta_T/k-1)/(1-\pi)\). Under I0 in the middle case, \(t=0\) fails because \(K_L>k\) there, \(t=\lambda\) fails because \(K_L\le k\) there, and I0 forbids a mixture.

Result: Product structure [paper Lemma A.8]

If \(s\ge t\), then \(\mu(0)\le\pi<\tau\), so \(e(0)=\rho\). The tuple with rates \((s,t)\) is an equilibrium exactly when \((s,e(1))\) is a high-class pair, \(t\) is a low-class rate, \(e(0)=e(-1)=\rho\), and prices are \(P(x)=t_0+e(x)\,w(\mu(x))\).

Derivation. The zero order has the odds \(\frac{\mu(0)}{1-\mu(0)}=\frac{\pi}{1-\pi}\cdot\frac{b_0+\lambda-s}{b_0+\lambda-t}\le\frac{\pi}{1-\pi}\) when \(s\ge t\). \(K_H\) depends only on \((s,\mu(1),e(1))\) and \(K_L\) only on \((t,\mu(-1),e(-1))\); entry after the zero order enters no payoff. Thus the two order choices satisfy separate payoff conditions. For sufficiency, compute prices from the stated entry. Entry is nondecreasing in the posterior, so the price difference formula in Section 4.7 separates distinct posteriors. Equal posteriors have equal entry and equal prices. The price beliefs therefore equal the order posteriors, which verifies the preparation choices. Bayes’ rule, the price formula, the two payoff conditions, and truthful bids verify the remaining equilibrium conditions.

The equilibrium forms and Proposition 3

At \(\pi=1/2\): \(1-\mu_+(s)=b/(2b+s)\), \(\mu_-(t)=b/(2b+t)\) and \(s^*=b(2\tau-1)/(1-\tau)\). The low-class rate is \(0\) on \([r_0,r_N]\), \(b[\rho\Delta_T/k-2]\) on \((r_N,r_S)\) and \(\lambda\) on \([r_S,r_2]\), because \(\rho\Delta_T\cdot\tfrac12\le k\iff r\le r_N\) and \(\rho\Delta_Tm\ge k\iff r\ge r_S\).

Where each high-class form exists (Step 2 of the proof).

  • N (\(s=0\)) needs \(\rho\Delta_T/2\le k\), that is \(r\le r_N\).

  • R: indifference \(\Delta_T\,b/(2b+s)=k\) gives \(2b+s=b\Delta_T/k\), so \(s_R=b(\Delta_T/k-2)\). The posterior is above \(\tau\) when \(1-\mu_+(s_R)=k/\Delta_T<1-\tau\), that is \(J>k\), that is \(r>r_A\). The rate is below \(\lambda\) when \(b\Delta_T/k<2b+\lambda=b(2+u)\), that is \(\Delta_T(1-M)<k\), that is \(r<r_F\). So R exists exactly on \((r_A,r_F)\).

  • Q: indifference \(\rho\Delta_T\,b/(2b+s)=k\) gives \(s_Q=b(\rho\Delta_T/k-2)\). Then \(s_Q>0\iff r>r_N\); the posterior is below \(\tau\iff k/(\rho\Delta_T)>1-\tau\iff\rho J<k\iff r<r_J\); and \(s_Q<\lambda\iff r<r_S\). Since \(r_J<r_S\), Q exists exactly on \((r_N,r_J)\).

  • T: \(s^*\in(0,\lambda)\iff\tfrac12<\tau<M\iff r<r_C\), and \(\rho\le e^*\le1\iff\rho J\le k\le J\iff r_A\le r\le r_J\), with \(e^*=1\iff r=r_A\). So T exists at \(r_A\) under C1, and on \([r_A,r_J]\) under CM.

  • F (\(s=\lambda\)): for \(r<r_C\), \(e(1)=1\), and \(\Delta_T(1-M)\ge k\iff r\ge r_F\). For \(r>r_C\), \(e(1)=\rho\), and \(\rho\Delta_T(1-M)=\rho\Delta_Tm\ge k\) holds because \(r>r_C>r_S\). At \(r_C\), C1 gives \(e(1)=1\). So F exists on \([r_F,r_2]\).

Step 4: \(s\ge t\).

For \(r\le r_N\), \(t=0\). For \(r>r_N\): F has \(s=\lambda\ge t\); Q has \(s_Q=t\); T has \(s^*\ge t\iff(2\tau-1)/(1-\tau)\ge\rho\Delta_T/k-2\iff1/(1-\tau)\ge\rho\Delta_T/k\iff\rho J\le k\), true for \(r\le r_J\). So Lemma A.8 applies to every combination.

Result: Proposition 3, full statement [paper Prop. 3; App. A.4]

Take the benchmark auction, the prior \(1/2\) and any slot in the band. Then \(r_0<r_A<r_F<r_N<r_J<r_S<r_1<r_C<r_2\), and under rules IM and C1 the equilibrium set is:

Strength Equilibria Entry at the benchmark slot
\(r_0\le r<r_A\) N \(\rho\)
\(r=r_A\) N; T with \(e^*=1\) (the left end of R) \(\rho\); 0.454185
\(r_A<r<r_F\) N; R \(\rho\); rises to 0.522757
\(r_F\le r\le r_N\) N; F \(\rho\); 0.522757
\(r_N<r<r_J\) F; Q 0.522757; \(\rho\)
\(r_J\le r\le r_2\) F 0.522757 up to \(r_C\); \(\rho\) above \(r_C\)

Entry is \(\rho\) in N and Q, \(\mathsf{E}_R(r)=\rho+(1-\rho)\,b\Delta_T(r)/(2k)\) in R, and \(\mathsf{E}_F=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda/2)\) in F up to \(r_C\), with \(\mathsf{E}_F=\rho\) above \(r_C\).

  1. No informed trade and an informative equilibrium coexist exactly on \([r_A,r_N]\).

  2. On the informative branch that coexists with no trade, entry is \[\mathsf{E}(r)=\rho+(1-\rho)\min\{b\Delta_T(r)/(2k),\,b+\lambda/2\}.\] It is continuous, rises strictly on \([r_A,r_F]\), and is constant on \([r_F,r_N]\). It keeps this value along F up to \(r_C\) and equals \(\rho\) above \(r_C\).

  3. The equilibrium set is nonempty at every strength.

Verification and existence. Each listed tuple is checked against (E1)-(E6) by direct computation: orders \(\sigma_H(1)=s/\lambda\), \(\sigma_L(-1)=s_-^*/\lambda\); the three posteriors; beliefs equal to the order posteriors; high-cost preparation \((e(1)-\rho)/(1-\rho)\) after a purchase and zero otherwise; prices \(t_0+e(x)w(\mu(x))\). If two orders share a posterior, one of them is \(0\) or \(-1\), so the common posterior is at most \(1/2<\tau\), and both have entry \(\rho\) and one price. Part (c): N covers \([r_0,r_N]\), F covers \([r_F,r_2]\), and \(r_F<r_N\). verify/08_coexistence.py prints the tuples at eight sample strengths. For example, it gives N and R at \(1.42\), F and Q at \(1.85\), and only F at \(2.50\).

Entry along the informative branch

Entry after the zero order and after a sale is \(\rho\). A purchase has probability \(\tfrac12(b+s)+\tfrac12b=b+s/2\). So \(\mathsf{E}=\rho+[e(1)-\rho](b+s/2)\).

  • R: \(b+s_R/2=b+\tfrac b2(\Delta_T/k-2)=b\Delta_T/(2k)\) and \(e(1)=1\), so \(\mathsf{E}_R=\rho+(1-\rho)\,b\Delta_T/(2k)\). It rises strictly, because \(\Delta_T\) does.

  • F: \(\mathsf{E}_F=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda/2)=0.522757\) below \(r_C\).

  • Continuity at \(r_F\): \(\Delta_T(r_F)=k(2+u)\), so \(b\Delta_T(r_F)/(2k)=b+ub/2=b+\lambda/2\), because \(ub=\lambda\).

  • The start: \(J(r_A)=k\) gives \(\Delta_T(r_A)/k=1/(1-\tau_A)\), \(\tau_A=0.640739\). So \(\mathsf{E}_R(r_A)=\rho+(1-\rho)\,b/[2(1-\tau_A)]=0.454185\).

\(r\) \(r_A=1.393958\) 1.40 1.45 \(r_F=1.467127\) \([r_F,r_C]\)
Purchase rate \(s\) \(s^*\) 0.167670 0.291736 \(\lambda=0.336122\) \(\lambda\)
Entry 0.454185 0.459588 0.506112 0.522757 0.522757

Intuition: Why entry rises while the high class mixes

On R the high class is indifferent: \(\Delta_T[1-\mu(1)]=k\), so \(1-\mu(1)=k/\Delta_T\). A wider spread makes \(k/\Delta_T\) smaller, so the purchase must become more informative. The only way is a higher purchase rate, \(s_R=b(\Delta_T/k-2)\). More purchases mean more recruits, and entry rises. At \(r_F\), \(s=\lambda\), and the investor is still indifferent. Above \(r_F\), the high class strictly prefers a full purchase, and entry stays flat through \(r_C\).

The threshold branch T (CM beyond \(r_A\)).

The purchase lifts the posterior exactly to \(\tau\), and the indifferent challenger prepares with the probability that keeps the high class indifferent. Then \(e^*-\rho=(k-\rho J)/J\) and \(b+s^*/2=b+\frac{b(2\tau-1)}{2(1-\tau)}=\frac{b}{2(1-\tau)}\), so \[\mathsf{E}_T(r)=\rho+\frac{k-\rho J}{J}\cdot\frac{b}{2(1-\tau)}=\rho+\frac b2\cdot\frac{k-\rho J(r)}{\Delta_T(r)\,[1-\tau(r)]^2}.\] At \(r_A\), \(J=k\) gives \(\mathsf{E}_T=\mathsf{E}_R(r_A)\); at \(r_J\), \(\rho J=k\) gives \(\mathsf{E}_T=\rho\). In between, \(k-\rho J\) falls (Lemma A.4(b)) and \(\Delta_T(1-\tau)^2\) rises on \([r_0,2]\) (Lemma A.4(c)); this covers \([r_A,r_J]\) because \(r_J<2\), from \(\rho J(2)-k=11/11200>0\). So \(\mathsf{E}_T\) falls strictly from \(\mathsf{E}_R(r_A)\) to \(\rho\): 0.341462, 0.321311 and 0.305208 at 1.55, 1.60 and 1.65, with \(e^*=0.580287\), 0.506106, 0.447179.

Panel note: What changed from the Laplace version

The Laplace version certified three isolated strengths (1.55, 1.60, 1.65), and the equilibrium correspondence between them was unknown. In the slot version the whole equilibrium set is known along the path, each order rate has an explicit formula, and the high class mixes over orders, not over order sizes.

Other priors

Fix the investor rule IM here. For a prior \(\pi\), let \(r_N(\pi)=\mathfrak r\bigl(k/(\rho\max\{\pi,1-\pi\})\bigr)\) be the end of no trade; \(r_C(\pi)\) the largest strength on the path with \(\tau(r)\le M(\pi)\); and define \(r_\pi\) by \(\tau(r_\pi)=\pi\) when \(\pi\in[\tau(r_0),\tau(r_2)]\). Three priors divide the cases: \(\pi_A^{\rm lo}\) solves \(M(\pi)=\tau_A\); \(\pi_A^{\rm hi}=\tau_A\); and \(\pi_{\max}=\tau(r_*)\), where \(r_*\) solves \(\rho\Delta_T(r)\tau(r)=k\). From the odds of \(M(\pi)\), \[\pi_A^{\rm lo}=\frac{\tau_A}{\tau_A+(1-\tau_A)(1+u)}=0.396175,\qquad \pi_A^{\rm hi}=0.640739,\qquad \pi_{\max}=0.650059\ (r_*=1.634218).\]

Result: Coexistence at every prior [paper Cor. A.2]

The strengths on the path at which no informed trade and an informative equilibrium coexist are \[C(\pi)=\bigl\{r\in[r_0,r_2]:\ r\ge r_A,\ \ \pi<\tau(r)\le M(\pi),\ \ \rho\Delta_T(r)\max\{\pi,1-\pi\}\le k\bigr\}.\] (a) Empty if \(\pi<\pi_A^{\rm lo}\); \(\{r_A\}\) at \(\pi=\pi_A^{\rm lo}\). (b) \([r_A,\min\{r_N(\pi),r_C(\pi)\}]\) if \(\pi_A^{\rm lo}<\pi<\pi_A^{\rm hi}\): the start is \(r_A\) for every such prior and every slot. (c) \((r_\pi,\min\{r_N(\pi),r_C(\pi)\}]\) if \(\pi_A^{\rm hi}\le\pi<\pi_{\max}\). (d) Empty if \(\pi\ge\pi_{\max}\). Entry on the informative branch is \(\rho+(1-\rho)\min\{(1-\pi)\,b\Delta_T(r)/k,\ b+\pi\lambda\}\).

Where each condition comes from.

  • \(\rho\Delta_T\max\{\pi,1-\pi\}\le k\): no trade survives. Against an uninformative price, a purchase earns \(\rho\Delta_T(1-\pi)\) and a sale earns \(\rho\Delta_T\pi\).

  • \(\tau(r)\le M(\pi)\): with no trade alive the low class abstains, so the informative equilibrium has \(s>0\). Q is impossible, because \(\rho\Delta_T[1-\mu_+(s)]<\rho\Delta_T(1-\pi)\le k\). So the purchase must reach \(\tau\).

  • \(r\ge r_A\): a purchase that recruits earns at most \(\Delta_T(1-\tau)=J\), so \(J\ge k\).

  • \(\pi<\tau(r)\): if \(\pi\ge\tau\), no trade gives entry one after every order under C1, and it survives only if \(\Delta_T\max\{\pi,1-\pi\}\le k\). Then a purchase earns \(\Delta_T[1-\mu_+(s)]<\Delta_T(1-\pi)\le k\) and a sale earns less than \(\Delta_T\pi\le k\): no informative equilibrium coexists.

Two facts close the cases. \(r_A<r_N(\pi)\) for every prior, because \(\rho\Delta_T(r_A)\max\{\pi,1-\pi\}<\rho\Delta_T(r_A)=\rho k/(1-\tau_A)<k\) when \(\tau_A<3/4=1-\rho\) (slack 0.109261). For \(\pi>1/2\), \(r_\pi<r_N(\pi)\iff\rho\Delta_T(r_\pi)\tau(r_\pi)<k\iff r_\pi<r_*\iff\pi<\pi_{\max}\). Entry: \(\Pr(X=1)=b+\pi s\); on R, \(\Delta_T(1-\pi)b/(b+\pi s)=k\) gives \(b+\pi s=(1-\pi)b\Delta_T/k\); on F it is \(b+\pi\lambda\).

Examples. At \(\pi=0.45\), coexistence ends near \(1.704\). At \(\pi=3/5\), it ends exactly at \(5/3\). At \(\pi=0.645\), the endpoints are near \(1.504\) and \(1.637\). At priors \(3/10\) and \(0.66\), the set is empty.

Other tie rules

Two alternatives show which conclusions depend on the default rules.

  • Rule I0: an investor indifferent between abstention and a nonzero order abstains.

  • Rule CM: an indifferent high-cost challenger may prepare with any probability.

Result (prior \(1/2\)) IM, C1 IM, CM I0, C1 I0, CM
Proposition 2 holds holds holds holds
No informed trade N \([r_0,r_N]\) \([r_0,r_N]\) \([r_0,r_N]\) \([r_0,r_N]\)
Mixed purchase R \((r_A,r_F)\) \((r_A,r_F)\) none none
Threshold pair T \(r_A\) only \([r_A,r_J]\) none none
Full purchase F \([r_F,r_2]\) \([r_F,r_2]\) \((r_F,r_N]\cup(r_S,r_2]\) \((r_F,r_N]\cup(r_S,r_2]\)
Set on \((r_N,r_J)\) F; Q F; Q; T empty empty
Set on \([r_J,r_S]\) F F (and T at \(r_J\)) empty empty
Entry after a purchase at \(r_C\) \(1\) any in \([\rho,1]\) \(1\) any in \([\rho,1]\)
Coexistence \([r_A,r_N]\) \([r_A,r_N]\) \((r_F,r_N]\) \((r_F,r_N]\)
Why I0 empties \((r_N,r_S]\).

There \(\rho\Delta_Tm\le k<\rho\Delta_T/2\), so no sale rate satisfies I0. At abstention, a sale earns \(\rho\Delta_T/2>k\). A full sale earns at most \(k\), so I0 selects abstention instead. I0 also excludes an interior mixture. At \(r=2\), for example, \(\rho\Delta_T/2=0.03125\) and \(\rho\Delta_Tm=0.016809\).

So IM is the investor rule under which an equilibrium exists along the whole path. The strict rise of entry along R also needs IM, because R is a mixture at indifference.

What CM adds at other priors.

With investor rule IM, CM can add one strength to \(C(\pi)\): the point \(r_\pi\), where the prior equals \(\tau\). CM lets no trade keep entry \(\rho\) when \(\rho\Delta_T\max\{\pi,1-\pi\}\le k\). An informative purchase at that strength needs \(J(r_\pi)>k\), or \(r_A<r_\pi\). Both conditions hold exactly when \(\tau_A<\pi\le\pi_{\max}\). At \(\pi_{\max}\) the point \(r_*=1.634218\) is the whole set: \(M(\pi_{\max})=0.834698\), the zero-order posterior is 0.454140, the full purchase earns \(\Delta_T(r_*)[1-M(\pi_{\max})]-k=0.000343\), and entry is \(\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\pi_{\max}\lambda)=0.560586\).

The tie rules matter only for the intermediate strengths: every comparison in the proof of Proposition 2 is strict (Section 6).

How the proof works

Most of the proof is algebra: the two class lemmas, the product structure and the conditions for each form. Two monotonicity steps use exact Sturm counts.

The path lemma [paper Lemma A.4].

(a) \(\tau\) rises on \([r_0,r_2]\): by the derivative in Section 3.4. (b) \(J\) rises on \([r_0,r_2]\): exact Sturm count. (c) \(\Delta_T(1-\tau)^2\) rises on \([r_0,2]\): exact Sturm count. (d) \(\Delta_T\tau\) rises on \([r_0,r_2]\): by hand, as both positive factors rise.

From a derivative to a polynomial.

Each function in (b) and (c) is a ratio \(N/D\) with \(D>0\) on the path. Its derivative is \((N'D-ND')/D^2\), so its sign is the sign of the polynomial \(N'D-ND'\). For \(J\), \(N=(r-1)^2(8r-r^2-\tfrac14)\) and \(D=2r(20r-r^2-1)\); in lowest integer terms \[Q_J(r)=4r^6-160r^5+743r^4-12r^3-614r^2+40r-1 .\] For \(\Delta_T(1-\tau)^2\), \(N=(r-1)^2(8r-r^2-\tfrac14)^2\) and \(D=2r(20r-r^2-1)^2\), which give the degree-10 polynomial \(16r^{10}-1280r^9+29256r^8-229344r^7+562917r^6-32808r^5-374607r^4+48456r^3-2685r^2+80r-1\).

Exact Sturm counts.

Sturm’s theorem counts the distinct real roots of a polynomial \(P\) in \((a,b]\) with no approximation. Build \(p_0=P\), \(p_1=P'\), \(p_{i+1}=-\operatorname{rem}(p_{i-1},p_i)\) until the remainder is zero. Delete zero values at \(x\). Let \(V(x)\) count the sign changes in the remaining sequence. The number of distinct roots in \((a,b]\) is \(V(a)-V(b)\). With rational coefficients and rational end points, every step is exact rational arithmetic. A count of zero plus a positive value at one point proves that the polynomial is positive on the whole interval.

Rational root brackets.

\(r_A\), \(r_J\) and \(r_*\) are roots of strictly monotone rational functions. Each function has an exact sign at a rational point. Bisection retains endpoints with opposite signs, so the bracket contains the unique root. Thresholds that depend on \(\mathrm{e}\) use rational bounds on \(\mathrm{e}\) from its series. A separate scan rebuilds the set from the flow law at 241 strengths for the four pairs of tie rules; it is a cross-check and enters no proof step.

Panel note: How to say it, and our check

“Proposition 3 is algebra, except two monotonicity facts. Each is the sign of an integer polynomial on a rational interval, and an exact Sturm count settles it.” Our script verify/08_coexistence.py builds \(N'D-ND'\) for both functions from the primitives with exact fractions and reproduces both polynomials coefficient by coefficient. Its Sturm count gives zero roots of \(Q_J\) in \((r_0,r_2]\) and zero roots of the degree-10 polynomial in \((r_0,2]\); both are positive at \(r_0\) (\(Q_J(r_0)=4634399/15625\)).

Previous: The benchmark in numbersNext: Competition creates research

10Competition creates research

So far the investor is present with a fixed probability \(\lambda\). Section 5 of the paper lets the investor choose that probability and pay for it. Over a range of strengths, research, entry and high-class ownership then rise together with the incumbent’s strength. The prior is \(1/2\) throughout. Use rule C1 unless the text states rule CM.

Research as chosen presence

Before it learns the class, the investor chooses a research intensity \(\lambda\in[0,\bar\lambda]\) at the cost \((a/2)\lambda^2\), with \(a>0\) and a cap \(0<\bar\lambda<1\). Research succeeds with probability \(\lambda\), independently of the class, the costs and the incumbent’s value. A success reveals the class, and the investor fills the slot with an order. After a failure a liquidity trader fills the slot. So research is the presence probability of the order slot, chosen at a cost. The cap is the probability that research can reveal the class at all before the challenger decides: some facts about the match appear only in the buyer’s own diligence. The quadratic cost is the simplest cost with a marginal cost that rises.

Intuition: Research removes camouflage

The slot has room for one order. A success assigns it to the informed investor; a failure assigns it to a liquidity trader. More research therefore adds informed orders and removes liquidity orders. Both effects make each informed order easier to read.

Market makers and the challenger do not observe the research choice. They price and decide at a conjectured intensity \(\hat\lambda\), which is correct in equilibrium. The benchmark has \(a=1/40\) and \(\bar\lambda=0.336122\), the benchmark presence, so research at the cap reproduces the benchmark slot.

Research optimality and the unique best response

An equilibrium with research is a tuple \((\hat\lambda,\sigma_H,\sigma_L,\mu,P,\bar\mu,\zeta)\) with truthful bids. (E1)-(E6) hold at \(\hat\lambda\); (E5) holds also when \(\hat\lambda=0\), because a research deviation makes a success possible and the order after it must be optimal. A seventh condition completes it [paper App. A.5]:

  1. Research optimality. Hold \(P\) and \(\zeta\) at their values in the tuple. Then \(\hat\lambda\) is the research choice of a best response among all joint deviations in research and orders.

After a success the orders earn \(U_H(q)=q\,e(q)\Delta_T[1-\mu(q)]-k|q|\) and \(U_L(q)=-q\,e(q)\Delta_T\mu(q)-k|q|\) [paper (A.7)]. The research value is the expected trading payoff after a success, \(V=\tfrac12\max_qU_H(q)+\tfrac12\max_qU_L(q)\ge0\) (abstention earns zero).

Result: Joint deviations [paper Lemma A.9]

For every candidate and every \(z\in[0,\bar\lambda]\), the best payoff of a joint deviation with research \(z\) is \(zV-(a/2)z^2\). The unique best research choice is \(\min\{\bar\lambda,V/a\}\). So (E7) reads \(\hat\lambda=\min\{\bar\lambda,V/a\}\), and research never has a tie.

Derivation. A joint deviation \((z,\sigma'_H,\sigma'_L)\) changes the true order law to \((1-z)\eta+z\sigma'_j\), but not the prices or the preparation rule, which use the candidate’s \(\hat\lambda\) and \(\sigma_j\). The investor trades only after a success, so its payoff is \[\Pi(z,\sigma')=z\Bigl[\tfrac12\textstyle\sum_q\sigma'_H(q)U_H(q)+\tfrac12\sum_q\sigma'_L(q)U_L(q)\Bigr]-\frac a2z^2 .\] The bracket is an average of payoffs, so it is at most \(V\), with equality when each \(\sigma'_j\) uses only maximizers. So \(\sup_{\sigma'}\Pi=zV-(a/2)z^2\). This is strictly concave in \(z\) with derivative \(V-az\): if \(V\ge a\bar\lambda\) the maximum is at \(\bar\lambda\); otherwise it is at \(V/a\). Strict concavity makes it unique and rules out a mixture over intensities.

The recruitment intensity and the research equation

Posterior bounds as functions of research.

At presence \(\lambda\), a directional liquidity order has probability \((1-\lambda)\beta\). At the prior \(1/2\): \[u_\lambda=\frac{\lambda}{(1-\lambda)\beta},\qquad M_\lambda=\frac{1+u_\lambda}{2+u_\lambda},\qquad m_\lambda=\frac1{2+u_\lambda}=\frac{(1-\lambda)\beta}{2(1-\lambda)\beta+\lambda}=\frac{(1-\lambda)\beta}{2\beta+\lambda(1-2\beta)} .\] Differentiate its quotient: \[\frac{dm_\lambda}{d\lambda} =\frac{-\beta[2\beta+\lambda(1-2\beta)]-\beta(1-\lambda)(1-2\beta)}{[2\beta+\lambda(1-2\beta)]^2} =-\frac{\beta}{[2\beta+\lambda(1-2\beta)]^2}<0.\] Thus \(m_\lambda\) falls from \(1/2\) at zero research toward zero. At the cap \(u_{\bar\lambda}=u\), so \(M_{\bar\lambda}=\mathrm{e}/(1+\mathrm{e})=0.731059\) and \(m_{\bar\lambda}=0.268941\).

The recruitment intensity.

For \(1/2<\tau(r)<1\), \(\lambda_E(r)\) is the research level at which a full purchase just reaches the threshold: \(m_\lambda=1-\tau\). Solve: \[\begin{align*} (1-\lambda)\beta=(1-\tau)[2\beta+\lambda(1-2\beta)]&\iff\beta-2\beta(1-\tau)=\lambda[\beta+(1-2\beta)(1-\tau)]\\ &\iff\lambda_E(r)=\frac{\beta\,[2\tau(r)-1]}{\beta+(1-2\beta)[1-\tau(r)]}. \end{align*}\] The quotient rule gives \(d\lambda_E/d\tau=\beta/\{\beta+(1-2\beta)(1-\tau)\}^2>0\). Under C1, a full purchase recruits exactly when research is at least \(\lambda_E(r)\). At equality, CM also permits partial preparation. A consistency check: \(\lambda_E(r_1)=0.290539\) and \(\lambda_E(r_2)=0.336721\) are the ends of the presence band in Table 2 of the paper (\(0.290539\), \(0.336721\)), because both solve \(M=\tau(r_i)\) at the same \(\beta\).

The research equation.

Suppose class \(H\) buys after a success, class \(L\) abstains, and only a purchase recruits. The purchase has posterior \(M_\lambda\) and entry \(1\), so it earns \(\Delta_T(1-M_\lambda)-k=\Delta_Tm_\lambda-k\), and \(V=\tfrac12(\Delta_Tm_\lambda-k)\). Interior research, \(a\lambda=V\), gives \[\begin{equation*} 2a\lambda=\Delta_T(r)\,m_\lambda-k .\tag*{\textsf{\small[paper (9)]}} \end{equation*}\] The left side rises in \(\lambda\); the right side falls, because research removes camouflage. Multiply by \(2\beta+\lambda(1-2\beta)>0\) and use \(m_\lambda[2\beta+\lambda(1-2\beta)]=(1-\lambda)\beta\); then move every term to the left: \[\begin{align*} F_r(\lambda)&=2a(1-2\beta)\lambda^2+\bigl[4a\beta+k(1-2\beta)+\beta\Delta_T\bigr]\lambda+\beta(2k-\Delta_T)\\ &=2\bigl[2\beta+\lambda(1-2\beta)\bigr]\bigl[a\lambda-\tfrac12(\Delta_Tm_\lambda-k)\bigr]. \end{align*}\] All coefficients except the constant are positive, so \(F_r\) rises strictly on \(\lambda\ge0\). Also \(F_r(0)=\beta(2k-\Delta_T)\) and \(F_r(1)=2a(1-2\beta)+4a\beta+k(1-2\beta)+\beta\Delta_T+2k\beta-\beta\Delta_T=2a+k>0\). So if \(\Delta_T(r)>2k\), \(F_r\) has exactly one root \(\lambda_R(r)\in(0,1)\). If \(F_r=A_2\lambda^2+A_1\lambda+A_0\), the quadratic formula gives \[\lambda_R=\frac{-A_1+\sqrt{A_1^2-4A_2A_0}}{2A_2}.\] Here \(A_2>0\), \(A_1>0\), and \(A_0<0\), so the other root is negative. This root defines the interior research branch; equilibrium also needs recruitment and a feasible cap.

The recruitment margin.

Let \(\varphi_a(r)=\Delta_T(1-\tau)-k-2a\lambda_E(r)\): the return of a purchase that just recruits, net of \(k\) and of the marginal research cost at \(\lambda_E\). Let \(e^*(r)=(2a\lambda_E+k)/[\Delta_T(1-\tau)]\). Since \(m_{\lambda_E}=1-\tau\), the factored form gives \(F_r(\lambda_E)=2[2\beta+\lambda_E(1-2\beta)][a\lambda_E-\tfrac12(\Delta_T(1-\tau)-k)]=-[2\beta+\lambda_E(1-2\beta)]\,\varphi_a\). As \(F_r\) rises, \(\lambda_R\ge\lambda_E\) exactly when \(\varphi_a\ge0\); and \(e^*<1\) exactly when \(\varphi_a>0\).

Positive research needs a strict full purchase

Result: [paper Lemma A.11]

Let \(\tau(r)>1/2\), and take an equilibrium with \(\hat\lambda>0\). Then \(U_H(1)>0\) and class \(H\) buys with probability one. Also \(\mu(1)=M_{\hat\lambda}\), \(\mu(0),\mu(-1)\le1/2\), and neither the zero order nor a sale recruits.

Derivation. \(\hat\lambda=\min\{\bar\lambda,V/a\}>0\) gives \(V>0\). Wrong-signed orders earn at most \(-k\) and abstention earns \(0\), so \(U_H(1)>0\) or \(U_L(-1)>0\). Suppose \(U_L(-1)>0\). Then class \(L\) sells with probability one, so \(f_H(-1)=\hat b\), \(f_L(-1)=\hat b+\hat\lambda\), \(\mu(-1)=m_{\hat\lambda}\le1/2<\tau\), entry after a sale is \(\rho\), and \(U_L(-1)=\rho\Delta_Tm_{\hat\lambda}-k>0\). The purchase then earns \(U_H(1)\ge\rho\Delta_T(1-M_{\hat\lambda})-k=\rho\Delta_Tm_{\hat\lambda}-k>0\), since \(e\ge\rho\) and \(\mu(1)\le M_{\hat\lambda}\). So \(U_H(1)>0\) either way, and the purchase is the unique best order of class \(H\). In words: if a sale pays, a purchase pays too, because a purchase has at least the same lower return bound. With a full purchase, \(f_H(0)=\hat b_0\le f_L(0)\), while \(f_H(-1)\le f_L(-1)\). Thus \(\mu(0),\mu(-1)\le1/2< au\), and neither order recruits.

The equilibrium set at intermediate strengths

Result: [paper Lemma A.12]

Let \(1/2<\tau(r)<1\), \(\rho\Delta_T(r)<2k\) (that is, \(r<r_N\)) and \(\lambda_E(r)\ne\bar\lambda\). The equilibria are exactly the tuples below. In each, class \(L\) abstains, the zero order and a sale do not recruit, prices are \(t_0+e(q)w(\mu(q))\), and bids are truthful.

  • No research: \(\hat\lambda=0\), every posterior \(1/2\), no recruitment. It always exists.

  • The research branch: \(\hat\lambda=\lambda_R(r)\), class \(H\) buys, posteriors \((\tfrac12,\ \hat b_0/(2\hat b_0+\hat\lambda),\ M_{\hat\lambda})\) after \((-1,0,1)\), and a purchase recruits. It exists exactly when \(\varphi_a(r)\ge0\) and \(F_r(\bar\lambda)>0\).

  • Research at the cap, with the orders and entry of (R). It exists exactly when \(F_r(\bar\lambda)\le0\) and \(\lambda_E(r)\le\bar\lambda\).

  • Rule CM only: \(\hat\lambda=\lambda_E(r)\), class \(H\) buys, \(\mu(1)=\tau\), and the high-cost challenger prepares with probability \((e^*-\rho)/(1-\rho)\) after a purchase. It exists exactly when \(\lambda_E(r)<\bar\lambda\) and \(\varphi_a(r)>0\).

Entry is \(\rho\) in (Z), \(\rho+(1-\rho)[\beta+(1/2-\beta)\hat\lambda]\) in (R) and (\(\bar{\rm R}\)), and \(\rho+(e^*-\rho)[\beta+(1/2-\beta)\lambda_E]\) in (T). Every trade choice is strict.

  • Posteriors of (R). \(f_H=(\hat b,\hat b_0,\hat b+\hat\lambda)\) and \(f_L=(\hat b,\hat b_0+\hat\lambda,\hat b)\). A sale is uninformative (posterior \(1/2\)), because the low class does not sell.

  • (Z) always exists. With \(\hat\lambda=0\) every posterior is \(1/2<\tau\), so entry is \(\rho\). After a hypothetical success, a purchase or a sale earns \(\rho\Delta_T/2-k<0\), because \(r<r_N\). So \(V=0\): no research confirms itself.

  • (R) needs its two conditions. The purchase must recruit, \(\lambda_R\ge\lambda_E\iff\varphi_a\ge0\), and research must be interior, \(\lambda_R<\bar\lambda\iff F_r(\bar\lambda)>0\). Then the purchase earns \(\Delta_Tm_{\lambda_R}-k=2a\lambda_R>0\), the sale earns \(\rho\Delta_T/2-k<0\), and \(V=a\lambda_R\), so (E7) holds.

  • No other equilibrium. With \(\hat\lambda>0\), Lemma A.11 gives a full purchase and \(\mu(-1)\le1/2\), so the sale earns at most \(\rho\Delta_T/2-k<0\). If \(\hat\lambda<\lambda_E\), the purchase does not recruit and earns \(\rho\Delta_Tm_{\hat\lambda}-k<0\), so \(V=0\) and \(\hat\lambda=0\): a contradiction. If \(\hat\lambda>\lambda_E\), interior research gives \(F_r(\hat\lambda)=0\), which is (R); research at the cap needs \(V\ge a\bar\lambda\iff F_r(\bar\lambda)\le0\), which is (\(\bar{\rm R}\)). If \(\hat\lambda=\lambda_E\), C1 makes the purchase recruit (the previous case); under CM, interior research needs \(a\lambda_E=\tfrac12(e\Delta_T(1-\tau)-k)\), so \(e=e^*\), which is (T).

  • Entry. A purchase has probability \(\tfrac12(\hat b+\hat\lambda)+\tfrac12\hat b=(1-\hat\lambda)\beta+\tfrac{\hat\lambda}2=\beta+(\tfrac12-\beta)\hat\lambda\), and only a purchase can lift entry above \(\rho\). Given class \(H\), a purchase occurs with probability \(\hat b+\hat\lambda=\beta+(1-\beta)\hat\lambda\).

Beliefs and prices in the listed tuples.

Compute \(\mu\) from the stated order laws, then set \(P(q)=t_0+e(q)w(\mu(q))\). Entry is nondecreasing in the posterior in each tuple. For \(\mu_2>\mu_1\), direct subtraction gives \[P_2-P_1=e_2\Delta_T(\mu_2-\mu_1)+(e_2-e_1)w(\mu_1)>0.\] The first term is positive; the second is nonnegative. Thus each distinct posterior has a distinct price, so the price belief equals the stated order posterior. The preparation comparisons above then verify (E3), without assuming it in the price argument. The price formula verifies (E4); the payoff comparisons verify (E5) and (E7). Truthful bids verify (E6).

Strong incumbents: research at the cap

Result: [paper Lemma A.13]

Let \(\tau(r)>1/2\), \(M_{\bar\lambda}\ne\tau(r)\), and \[\begin{equation*} \rho\Delta_T(r)\,m_{\bar\lambda}-k>a\bar\lambda.\tag*{(U)} \end{equation*}\] Under every pair of tie rules the equilibrium is unique: research at the cap, class \(H\) buys, class \(L\) sells, posteriors \((m_{\bar\lambda},\tfrac12,M_{\bar\lambda})\), and a purchase recruits exactly when \(M_{\bar\lambda}>\tau(r)\). Entry is \(\rho+(1-\rho)(\bar b+\bar\lambda/2)\), \(\bar b=(1-\bar\lambda)\beta\), if \(M_{\bar\lambda}>\tau(r)\), and \(\rho\) otherwise. This is the equilibrium of Proposition 2 with presence \(\bar\lambda\).

Derivation. In any candidate every posterior lies in \([m_{\bar\lambda},M_{\bar\lambda}]\) and entry is at least \(\rho\). So the gross returns of the two correctly signed nonzero orders are at least \(\rho\Delta_T(1-M_{\bar\lambda})=\rho\Delta_Tm_{\bar\lambda}\), and by (U) both net payoffs exceed \(a\bar\lambda\ge0\). The purchase and the sale are strict best orders, \(V>a\bar\lambda\), and Lemma A.9 gives \(\hat\lambda=\bar\lambda\). The condition \(M_{\bar\lambda}\ne\tau\) removes the challenger tie. At the benchmark cap, \(m_{\bar\lambda}=1/(1+\mathrm{e})\), so (U) reads \(\Delta_T(r)>(k+a\bar\lambda)(1+\mathrm{e})/\rho\), that is \(r>r_U=\mathfrak r\bigl((k+a\bar\lambda)(1+\mathrm{e})/\rho\bigr)=2.434046\), with the root map \(\mathfrak r\) of Section 9.2.

Proposition 4: competition creates research

Result: Competition creates research [paper Prop. 4]

Take the benchmark auction at the prior \(1/2\), with \(0<\beta\le1/3\) and \(a>0\).

  1. On the strengths with \(\Delta_T(r)>2k\), research \(\lambda_R(r)\) is continuously differentiable and rises strictly in \(r\). Wherever the research branch is an equilibrium, entry \(\rho+(1-\rho)[\beta+(1/2-\beta)\lambda_R(r)]\) and high-class ownership \(\tfrac12[\rho+(1-\rho)(\beta+(1-\beta)\lambda_R(r))]\) rise strictly with \(r\).

  2. At the benchmark, let \(a=1/40\) and \(\bar\lambda=0.336122\).

    1. At \(r_0\), the unique equilibrium has no research or trade, and entry is \(\rho\).

    2. At each \(r\in\{1.55,1.60,1.65\}\), exactly two equilibria exist under C1. One has no research and entry \(\rho\). The other has \(\lambda_E(r)<\lambda_R(r)<\bar\lambda\).

    3. At \(r_1\) and \(r_2\), the unique equilibrium has research at the cap and trade in both directions. Entry is 0.522757 at \(r_1\) and \(\rho\) at \(r_2\).

Part (a): the implicit-function derivative.

\(F_r\) depends on \(r\) only through \(\Delta_T(r)\). Its terms with \(\Delta_T\) are \(\beta\Delta_T\lambda-\beta\Delta_T=-\beta(1-\lambda)\Delta_T\). So \[\partial_\lambda F_r=4a(1-2\beta)\lambda+4a\beta+k(1-2\beta)+\beta\Delta_T>0,\qquad \partial_rF_r=-\beta(1-\lambda)\,\Delta_T'(r)<0,\] because \(\lambda<1\) and \(\Delta_T'(r)=\tfrac12(1-\ell^2/r^2)>0\). On \(\{r:\Delta_T(r)>2k\}\) the implicit function theorem gives \[\lambda_R'(r)=-\frac{\partial_rF_r}{\partial_\lambda F_r}=\frac{\beta\,[1-\lambda_R(r)]\,\Delta_T'(r)}{\partial_\lambda F_r(\lambda_R(r))}>0 .\] On the branch, entry has slope \((1-\rho)(1/2-\beta)\lambda_R'>0\), since \(\beta<1/2\), and ownership has slope \(\tfrac12(1-\rho)(1-\beta)\lambda_R'>0\), since \(W_H=1\). At \(r=1.6\), \(\lambda_R'=0.786586\) (equal to a central difference), the entry slope is \(0.121141\) and the ownership slope is \(0.208055\).

Intuition: Why research rises with strength

A stronger incumbent widens the spread, so a purchase that recruits earns more, \(\Delta_Tm_\lambda-k\). The investor researches more. Research adds informed purchases in class \(H\) and removes liquidity orders in both classes. The first effect dominates: \(\beta+(1/2-\beta)\lambda\) rises in \(\lambda\) because \(\beta<1/2\).

Part (b)(i).

At \(r_0\), any nonzero order after a success has gross return at most \(e\Delta_T\max\{\mu,1-\mu\}\le\Delta_T(r_0)=1/60<k\) (margin 0.003333). So \(V=0\) and research is zero. Every posterior is \(1/2\), and \(c_H-B_{r_0}(1/2)=1.195833>0\), so entry is \(\rho\).

Part (b)(ii).

Table 8 gives the values and the margins that make Lemma A.12 apply, with \(\{\mathrm Z,\mathrm R\}\) as the set under C1 and either investor rule.

Research and entry at the intermediate strengths [paper Table 4], with the margins of Lemma A.12.
\(r=1.55\) \(r=1.60\) \(r=1.65\)
Target-payoff spread \(\Delta_T(r)\) \(\tfrac{121}{1240}=0.097581\) \(\tfrac9{80}=0.112500\) \(\tfrac{169}{1320}=0.128030\)
Preparation threshold \(\tau(r)\) \(\tfrac{1020}{1577}=0.646798\) \(\tfrac{205}{316}=0.648734\) \(\tfrac{7620}{11711}=0.650670\)
Recruitment intensity \(\lambda_E(r)\) 0.196741 0.199698 0.202664
Research \(\lambda_R(r)\) 0.237901 0.279037 0.316683

A worked column at \(r=8/5\).

First, \(\Delta_T=(r-1)^2/(2r)=9/80\) and \(\tau=205/316\), from (3.1). Insert this fraction into \(\lambda_E\) to get 0.199698. The positive quadratic root gives \(\lambda_R\approx0.279037\). Then \(M_{\lambda_R}\approx0.698206>\tau\), so a purchase recruits. The purchase probabilities give \[\mathsf{E}=\frac14+\frac34[\beta+(1/2-\beta)\lambda_R]\approx0.513965,\] \[\mathsf{O}_H=\frac12\left[\frac14+\frac34\{\beta+(1-\beta)\lambda_R\}\right]\approx0.309302.\] The paper brackets \(\lambda_R\) by bisection with exact signs. Our script evaluates the quadratic root at high precision.

Part (b)(iii).

\(\tau(r_1)<M_{\bar\lambda}<\tau(r_2)\), with margins 0.026059 and 0.000334. (U) holds at both strengths, with margins 0.016421 and 0.034723, as both exceed \(r_U\). Lemma A.13 gives entry 0.522757 at \(r_1\) and 0.250000 at \(r_2\). Every trade choice at the six strengths is strict and research never has a tie, so the lists do not depend on the investor’s tie rule.

The research branch along the path

At the benchmark, the research branch is an equilibrium exactly on \([r_R,\bar r_R)\), with endpoints \(1.501023\) and \(1.677633\) to six places.

  • The start \(r_R\) is the root of \(\varphi_a\): \(\Delta_T(1-\tau)=k+2a\lambda_E(r)\).
