The margin rises on \([r_0,7/4]\): with \(\tau'>0\) from (3.2), \[\varphi_a'=\Delta_T'(1-\tau)-\Delta_T\tau'-\frac{2a\beta\,\tau'}{\{\beta+(1-2\beta)(1-\tau)\}^2},\] and on \([r_0,7/4]\) the bounds \(\Delta_T'\ge11/72\), \(1-\tau\ge19/55\), \(\Delta_T\le9/56\), \(\tau'\le16875/211288\) (largest numerator over smallest denominator) give \((\Delta_T(1-\tau))'\ge0.039942\) and \(\varphi_a'\ge0.033767>0\) at \(a=1/40\); the bound stays positive for \(a<a_{\rm mono}=0.161713\). Since \(\varphi_a(r_0)<0<\varphi_a(7/4)\), the root is unique; exact-sign bisection gives \(r_R=1.501023\), with \(\lambda_E(r_R)=0.193853\) and entry 0.500846.

  • The end \(\bar r_R\): \(F_r(\bar\lambda)=0\) is \(\Delta_T=(2a\bar\lambda+k)(1+\mathrm{e})\), so \(\bar r_R=\mathfrak r\bigl((2a\bar\lambda+k)(1+\mathrm{e})\bigr) =1.677633\), with entry 0.522757. Above it, an interior intensity at which the purchase recruits would need \(\hat\lambda\ge\lambda_R(r)\ge\bar\lambda\): research sits at the cap, and (\(\bar{\rm R}\)) exists on \([\bar r_R,r_N)\) because \(\lambda_E(r)\le\lambda_E(7/4)=0.208635<\bar\lambda\).

  • Zero research is an equilibrium exactly when \(\rho\Delta_T\le2k\), that is \(r\le r_N=1.747878\). Since \(\bar r_R<r_N\) (margin 0.070245), it coexists with the whole branch.

  • Above \(r_U=2.434046\), (U) forces full orders and research at the cap. On \((r_N,r_U)\) the paper does not characterize the equilibrium set with research.

Panel note: Why the research branch starts later than \(r_A\)

With a fixed presence, the informative branch starts at \(r_A=1.393958\), where a purchase that recruits just covers \(k\). With research, it must also cover the marginal research cost \(2a\lambda_E\), so the branch starts at \(r_R=1.501023\). The adjustment also differs: with a fixed presence the high class mixes; with research the intensity adjusts and the high class buys with probability one after a success.

The admissible cap and cost, and the range of \(\beta\)

Result: The admissible cap and cost [paper Prop. A.2]

Fix \(\beta=0.294655\). Require the patterns of Proposition 4(b), with \(\lambda_E(r)<\lambda_R(r)<\bar\lambda\) at each intermediate strength. These patterns have strict margins exactly when \[\lambda_E(r_1)<\bar\lambda<\lambda_E(r_2),\qquad a_{\rm lo}(\bar\lambda)<a<a_{\rm up}.\] The cost bounds are \[a_{\rm lo}(\bar\lambda)=\frac{\Delta_T(1.65)\,m_{\bar\lambda}-k}{2\bar\lambda},\qquad a_{\rm up}=\frac{\Delta_T(1.55)\,[1-\tau(1.55)]-k}{2\lambda_E(1.55)}.\] The cap strip is \((0.290539,0.336721)\). At \(\bar\lambda=0.336122\) the cost window is \((0.021469,0.036763)\); at \(a=1/40\) the cap range is \((0.316683,0.336721)\).

  • \(\bar\lambda>\lambda_E(r_1)\): research at the cap recruits strictly at \(r_1\); otherwise every posterior there is at most \(\tau(r_1)\). \(\bar\lambda<\lambda_E(r_2)\): research at the cap does not recruit at \(r_2\); otherwise C1 lifts entry above \(\rho\).

  • \(a<a_{\rm up}\) is \(\varphi_a(1.55)>0\): the purchase recruits strictly at 1.55, and, as \(a_{\rm up}<a_{\rm mono}\) makes \(\varphi_a\) rise, at 1.60 and 1.65 too.

  • \(a>a_{\rm lo}(\bar\lambda)\) is \(F_{1.65}(\bar\lambda)>0\): research stays below the cap at 1.65, and so at 1.55 and 1.60, as \(\lambda_R\) rises. \(a_{\rm lo}(\bar\lambda)=a\) exactly when \(\bar\lambda=\lambda_R(1.65)\); so the lower end of the cap range at \(a=1/40\) is \(\lambda_R(1.65)=0.316683\).

  • (U) at \(r_1\) and \(r_2\) adds nothing on the strip: both bounds \(a_U(r;\bar\lambda)=[\rho\Delta_T(r)m_{\bar\lambda}-k]/\bar\lambda\) fall in \(\bar\lambda\), and at \(\bar\lambda=\lambda_E(r_2)\) they are 0.073556 and 0.127845, both above \(a_{\rm up}\). The window is nonempty on the whole strip, since \(a_{\rm lo}\) falls in \(\bar\lambda\) and \(a_{\rm lo}(\lambda_E(r_1))=0.030579<a_{\rm up}\).

The benchmark cap lies 0.045583 and 0.000599 inside the strip; the cost lies 0.003531 and 0.011763 inside its window. The upper cap margin is the posterior margin \(\tau(r_2)-M_{\bar\lambda}=0.000334\) in another coordinate.

The range of \(\beta\).

Set the cap to the benchmark presence at each \(\beta\), \(\bar\lambda=\lambda_B(\beta)=u\beta/(1+u\beta)\) with \(1+u=\mathrm{e}\), and keep \(a=1/40\).

  1. \(u_{\bar\lambda}=u\) for every \(\beta\), so \(M_{\bar\lambda}=\mathrm{e}/(1+\mathrm{e})\) and \(m_{\bar\lambda}=1/(1+\mathrm{e})\) do not move, and the strip condition holds for every \(\beta\).

  2. (U) at \(r_1\) holds for every \(\beta\le1/3\), because \(\lambda_B\) rises in \(\beta\) and \(\rho\Delta_T(r_1)/(1+\mathrm{e})-k-a\lambda_B(1/3)=0.015719>0\).

  3. \(a_{\rm lo}=[\Delta_T(1.65)/(1+\mathrm{e})-k]/[2\lambda_B(\beta)]\) falls in \(\beta\). So \(a_{\rm lo}<a\) exactly when \(\lambda_B(\beta)>L=[\Delta_T(1.65)/(1+\mathrm{e})-k]/(2a)=0.288653\), that is \(\beta>\beta_{\rm lo}=L/[u(1-L)]=0.236157\).

  4. \(a_{\rm up}(\beta)\) falls in \(\beta\), because \(\partial\lambda_E/\partial\beta=(2\tau-1)(1-\tau)/\{\beta+(1-2\beta)(1-\tau)\}^2>0\), and it exceeds \(a\) for \(\beta<0.4897\). \(a_{\rm mono}(\beta)\) falls in \(\beta\), with \(a_{\rm mono}(1/3)=0.150885>a\).

So the patterns hold for every \(\beta\in(0.236157,1/3]\), with entry \(\rho+(1-\rho)[\beta+(1/2-\beta)\bar\lambda]\) at \(r_1\).

Tie rules with research

Result: Tie rules with research [paper Remark A.1]

Research has a unique best response, and Proposition 4(b) needs no investor tie rule. Under the challenger rule CM, each intermediate strength has one more equilibrium, the threshold equilibrium (T). Above \(r_U\) the tuple is unique under C1, and under CM at every strength except the entry ceiling \(r_C=3.592659\).

In (T), research is \(\lambda_E(r)\), so the purchase posterior equals \(\tau\). The indifferent challenger prepares so that entry after a purchase is \(e^*=(2a\lambda_E+k)/[\Delta_T(1-\tau)]\). The purchase then earns \(e^*\Delta_T(1-\tau)-k=2a\lambda_E\), so \(V=a\lambda_E\) and (E7) holds. The preparation probability lies in \((0,1)\): \(e^*<1\) is \(\varphi_a>0\), and \(e^*>\rho\) because \(\rho\Delta_T(1-\tau)<\rho\Delta_T/2<k<2a\lambda_E+k\). Entry is \(\rho+(e^*-\rho)[\beta+(1/2-\beta)\lambda_E]\):

\(r=1.55\) \(r=1.60\) \(r=1.65\)
\(e^*\) 0.865703 0.758777 0.673748
Entry in (T) 0.456294 0.420777 0.392494

Entry in (T) falls across the three strengths, while entry on the research branch rises. At \(r_C\), \(M_{\bar\lambda}=\tau(r_C)\): CM admits every preparation probability after a purchase, each with its own competitive price, and entry fills \([\rho,0.522757]\) while research stays at the cap.

Caution: Two threshold objects

The threshold pair T of Section 9 has a fixed presence and a mixed purchase rate \(s^*\). The threshold equilibrium (T) here has a pure purchase after a success and research \(\lambda_E\). Both put the purchase posterior exactly at \(\tau\).

Access to prices with research, and its effect on proceeds and surplus at \(r_1\), belongs to the welfare analysis (Section 12.1).

The old guide had no research stage. Here presence responds to its expected return.

Previous: Coexistence: the whole strength pathNext: Robustness

11Robustness

The mechanism rests on three facts: the entry floor, the trading bounds of Section 5.4, and recruitment after a purchase. Each extension below changes one primitive and asks which facts survive. [paper Sec. 6; Appendix A.6] The bounds \(\rho\kappa\Delta_T\le K_H,K_L\le\Delta_T\) still concern only the gross returns of correctly signed nonzero orders.

Preparation cost laws: keep the floor first

[paper Sec. 6.1; Prop. A.4]

Intuition: The atoms do not carry the result

The zero-cost type prepares after every price, so entry never falls below \(\rho\). The high-cost type prepares only after good news. Any cost law with a low and a high component can do both jobs, if strict gaps separate the costs from the gross profits.

Let the cost be independent of the class, the values and the slot, with law \(H_C=\rho H^0+(1-\rho)H^1\), \(\operatorname{supp}H^0\subset[0,L]\) and \(\operatorname{supp}H^1\subset[c_-,c_+]\). Either component may have atoms.

Result: Preparation cost laws [paper Prop. A.4]

Let \(F_1\) strengthen \(F_0\), and suppose \[0<L<B_1(m),\qquad B_0(\pi)<c_-<c_+<B_1(M),\qquad \Delta_0<k<\rho\kappa\Delta_1.\] At \(F_0\), orders are uniquely zero, and entry is \(\rho\). At \(F_1\), orders are uniquely full, only a purchase recruits the high component, and entry is \(\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\pi\lambda)\). Ownership rises under the exact condition of Proposition 2.

The proof of Proposition 2 goes through with three changes.

  1. The floor. In any candidate at \(F_0\) or \(F_1\), every price belief lies in \([m,M]\), also at a pooled price (Section 4.5; a pool with the zero order uses \(\beta\le1/3\)). Since \(g_H\ge g_L\) and \(B_0\ge B_1\), \(B_i(\bar\mu)\ge B_i(m)\ge B_1(m)>L\). The whole low component prepares after every price, so \(e\ge\rho\).

  2. The orders. The trading bounds need only this floor. At \(F_0\) a correct order earns at most \(\Delta_0-k<0\); at \(F_1\) at least \(\rho\kappa\Delta_1-k>0\). So orders are zero at \(F_0\), where \(B_0(\pi)<c_-\) keeps the high component out, and full at \(F_1\).

  3. Recruitment. \(B_1(M)>c_+>c_->B_0(\pi)\ge B_1(\pi)\) and \(M>\pi\) give \(g_{H,1}>g_{L,1}\). So \(B_1(m)<B_1(\pi)<c_-\) keeps the high component out after a sale or zero, and \(B_1(M)>c_+\) admits all of it after a purchase.

Every cost comparison is strict, so no tie rule matters. Entry is at least \(\rho\) and nondecreasing in the posterior, so the price algebra of Section 4.7 applies unchanged, and entry and ownership follow as in Section 6.5.

The third economy needs another gap.

At \(F_2\), the conditions \(L<B_2(m)\), \(B_2(M)<c_-\) and \(k<\rho\kappa\Delta_2\) keep full orders and return entry to \(\rho\). Without \(B_2(M)<c_-\), part of the high component still prepares after a purchase. With full orders and \(B_r(\pi)<c_-\), entry is \(\mathsf{E}(r)=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\pi\lambda)\,H^1\bigl(B_r(M)\bigr)\). Only here does atomlessness matter: if \(H^1\) has no atom, entry is continuous and nonincreasing in \(r\), and the drop at the entry ceiling becomes a ramp.

Benchmark check.

Take \(L=1/5\) and \(H^1\) uniform on \([6-\varepsilon,6+\varepsilon]\). At \(\varepsilon=1/10\), the support margins are \(B_1(m)-L=2.166178511\), \(c_-B_0(\pi)=1.095833333\) and \(B_1(M)-c_+=0.117154822\), and entry at \(r_1\) is the two-point value \(0.522757\). At \(r_2\), \(B_2(M)=5.997311\) lies inside the band, and \(H^1(B_2(M))=(B_2(M)-6+\varepsilon)/(2\varepsilon)\) gives \(\mathsf{E}_2=0.382711\) and \(\mathsf{O}_{H,2}=0.222020\). Entry returns to \(\rho\) at \(r_2\) for every half-width below \(6-B_2(M)=0.002688902\), such as \(1/1000\).

Caution: Keep the three parts separate

The half-width \(1/10\) keeps the rise from \(r_0\) to \(r_1\), but not entry \(\rho\) at \(r_2\). Atomlessness smooths the drop at the ceiling; it does not remove it.

General value classes and any prior

[paper Sec. 6.2; Prop. A.5; Table A.4]

Intuition: Put both strengths just above the point where the classes split

Near the lowest value at which the classes differ, the target-payoff spread is close to zero, so informed trade against the weaker incumbent cannot cover \(k\). The gross-profit gap there is close to the whole class gap, so a high cost that only good news justifies fits inside it.

Let the classes satisfy the class ranking with \(\mathbb{E}_H[\theta]>\mathbb{E}_L[\theta]\). With \(D=G_L-G_H\ge0\), define \(\Gamma(x)=\int_p^xD(y)\,dy\), \(\bar\Gamma=\mathbb{E}_H[\theta]-\mathbb{E}_L[\theta]>0\) and \(v_*=\inf\{x\ge p:\Gamma(x)>0\}\). \(\Gamma\) rises from \(0\) to \(\bar\Gamma\), and \(v_*\) is the first value above the reserve where the classes differ. For point masses, \(v_*=\ell\).

Step 1: the spread vanishes at \(v_*\). For \(R\sim U[0,r]\), the spread formula [paper (8)] has \(D(p^-)=0\) and \(1-F(y)=1-y/r\). Integrate by parts with \(\Gamma'=D\) and \(\Gamma(p)=0\); the boundary term is zero at both ends: \[\Delta_T(r)=\int_p^r\Bigl(1-\frac yr\Bigr)D(y)\,dy =\Bigl[\Bigl(1-\frac yr\Bigr)\Gamma(y)\Bigr]_p^r+\frac1r\int_p^r\Gamma(y)\,dy =\frac1r\int_p^r\Gamma(y)\,dy .\] Since \(\Gamma\) rises, \(\int_p^r\Gamma\le(r-p)\Gamma(r)\), so \(\Delta_T'(r)=[r\Gamma(r)-\int_p^r\Gamma]/r^2\ge p\Gamma(r)/r^2>0\) for \(r>v_*\). Since \(\Gamma=0\) on \([p,v_*]\), \(\Delta_T(r)\to0\) as \(r\downarrow v_*\).

Step 2: the profit gap stays large. The surplus split of Section 3.7 gives \(\Delta_T+g_H-g_L=\bar\Gamma\) at every strength. With \(\Delta_T(r)\le(r-p)\bar\Gamma/r\) from Step 1, \(g_H(r)-g_L(r)=\bar\Gamma-\Delta_T(r)\ge p\bar\Gamma/r>0\). At \(r=v_*\) the gap is the whole \(\bar\Gamma\), so \(B_{v_*}(M)-B_{v_*}(\pi)=(M-\pi)\bar\Gamma>0\). Also \(W_H(r)\ge p/r>0\), since every value is at least \(p\), and \(g_j\) and \(W_H\) are continuous in \(r\).

Result: Construct the strengths and the costs in this order [paper Prop. A.5]

Let \(e_H=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda)>\rho\).

  1. Choose \(r_1>v_*\) close to \(v_*\), with \(B_{r_1}(M)>B_{v_*}(\pi)\) and \(e_HW_H(r_1)>\rho W_H(v_*)\). Both hold at \(r_1=v_*\), so continuity permits the choice.

  2. Choose \(r_0\in(v_*,r_1)\) close to \(v_*\), with \(\Delta_T(r_0)<\rho\kappa\Delta_T(r_1)\). Gross profit and \(W_H\) fall with strength, so \(B_{r_0}(\pi)<B_{r_1}(M)\) and \(e_HW_H(r_1)>\rho W_H(r_0)\).

  3. Choose \(c_H\in\bigl(B_{r_0}(\pi),B_{r_1}(M)\bigr)\) and \(k\in\bigl(\Delta_T(r_0),\rho\kappa\Delta_T(r_1)\bigr)\).

  4. Since \(g_j(r)\to0\) as \(r\to\infty\), choose \(r_2>r_1\) with \(B_{r_2}(M)<c_H\).

Every condition is a strict inequality between continuous functions. So, for every prior, reserve, \(\rho\) and slot, a nonempty open set of \((r_0,r_1,r_2,c_H,k)\) satisfies (H1)-(H3) and the ownership condition. For point masses this holds for every \(h>\ell\).

Six economies.

Each row keeps the benchmark slot, \(p=1/2\) and \(\rho=1/4\), with its own costs and strengths. A single value is a point mass; a bracket is a uniform class. In every row, entry at \(r_0\) is \(\rho\), and so is entry at \(r_2\) when the row has a third strength.

Economy Low class High class \(\pi\) \((c_H,k)\) \((r_0,r_1,r_2)\) \(\mathsf{E}_1\) \(\mathsf{O}_{H,1}\)
Moderate gap \(1\) \(2\) \(1/2\) \((89/100,1/500)\) \((21/20,3/2,\text{-})\) 0.522757 0.324401
Narrow bands \([19/20,21/20]\) \([199/20,201/20]\) \(1/2\) \((6,1/50)\) \((6/5,3,18/5)\) 0.522757 0.324401
Low prior \(1\) \(10\) \(3/10\) \((9/2,1/100)\) \((11/10,3,18/5)\) 0.472339 0.194641
High prior \(1\) \(10\) \(7/10\) \((36/5,1/100)\) \((11/10,3,18/5)\) 0.573175 0.454162
Wide classes \([3/5,7/5]\) \([1,19]\) \(7/10\) \((67/10,1/50)\) \((1,9/2,11/2)\) 0.573175 0.419819
Same means \(1\) \(10\) \(7/10\) \((67/10,1/50)\) \((1,9/2,11/2)\) 0.250000 0.175000

The first five rows show the rise; the moderate-gap row has no third strength, so it checks (H1) and (H2) only. The last row puts point masses at the means of the wide classes: \(B_1(M)-c_H=-0.018122<0\), so a purchase does not recruit. The prior rows need their own costs: at the benchmark costs and strengths, the prior \(3/10\) gives entry \(0.250000\) at every strength, and the prior \(7/10\) gives entry \(1.000000\) at \(r_0\), because the prior itself recruits.

How to compute a row. For a point value \(v\), the payoff terms of Section 3.3 apply with \(v\) in place of \(\ell\) when \(p\le v\le r\) (\(W_v=v/r\)), and with \(v\) in place of \(h\) when \(v\ge r\) (\(W_v=1\)). A uniform class averages them over its band, with a cut at \(v=r\). Inside \([p,r]\), \(g_v\) is linear in \(v^2\), so a band of half-width \(\varepsilon\) around \(\ell\) replaces \(\ell^2\) by \(\mathbb{E}[v^2]=\ell^2+\varepsilon^2/3\). Above \(r\), \(t_v\) does not depend on \(v\). For the wide high class \(U[1,19]\) at \(r_1=9/2\), \(W_H=\frac1{18}\bigl[\int_1^{9/2}\frac{v}{9/2}\,dv+(19-\frac92)\bigr]=\frac1{18}\bigl[\frac{77}{36}+\frac{29}2\bigr] =\frac{599}{648}<1\), while \(W_H(r_0)=1\). Ownership still rises, because the rise of \(e_H\) from \(\rho\) outweighs the fall of \(W_H\). The paper evaluates these integrals in exact rational arithmetic, with outward intervals for every quantity that depends on \(\mathrm{e}\).

Intuition: Why dispersion helps gross profit

For each \(R\), \(v\mapsto(v-\max\{p,R\})_+\) is convex. A mean-preserving spread within a class therefore weakly raises its gross profit, and \(B_r(\mu)\) at every belief. So the wide classes recruit, and point masses at the same means do not. The argument ranks gross profit, not entry in every equilibrium.

Many classes: separation and a price collision

[paper Prop. A.6; Appendix A.6]

With \(J\) classes the price is one number, but the posterior is a vector; the price reveals only the posterior mean of class proceeds, \(\nu\cdot t\). Each class reaches each order through a liquidity order, so with \(\beta\le1/3\) every ratio \(f_j(q)/f_l(q)\) lies in \([1/(1+u),1+u]\) (Section 4.3). Bayes’ rule, \(\mu_j(q)/\pi_j=f_j(q)/\sum_l\pi_lf_l(q)\), then puts every order posterior in the convex set \[\mathcal M_\pi=\Bigl\{\nu\ge0:\ \sum_j\nu_j=1,\ \ \frac{\nu_j}{\pi_j}\le(1+u)\frac{\nu_l}{\pi_l}\ \text{for all }j,l\Bigr\},\] and a pooled price belief too. As in Section 5.2, against a fixed price and entry schedule, \(U_j(q)=q\,e(q)\,[t_j-\mu(q)\cdot t]-k|q|\) with \(e(q)\in[\rho,1]\); this needs no price sufficiency.

Result: Many classes under separation [paper Prop. A.6]

Split the classes into a low group \(\mathcal L\) and a high group \(\mathcal H\), with high-group prior mass \(\bar\pi\) and prior-weighted group means \(\bar g_{\mathcal L},\bar g_{\mathcal H}\) of \(g_{j,1}\). Suppose \(\max_jt_{j,0}-\min_jt_{j,0}<k\), \(\sum_j\pi_jg_{j,0}<c_H\), \[\min_{j\in\mathcal H}t_{j,1}-\max_{\nu\in\mathcal M_\pi}\nu\cdot t_1>\frac k\rho,\qquad \min_{\nu\in\mathcal M_\pi}\nu\cdot t_1-\max_{j\in\mathcal L}t_{j,1}>\frac k\rho,\] \(\bar g_{\mathcal H}>\bar g_{\mathcal L}\) and \(\bar g_{\mathcal L}+M(\bar\pi)(\bar g_{\mathcal H}-\bar g_{\mathcal L})>c_H\). At \(F_0\), every class abstains, and entry is \(\rho\). At \(F_1\), the high group buys, the low group sells, only a purchase recruits, and entry is \(\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\bar\pi\lambda)\).

Proof. At \(F_0\), every residual is at most \(\max_lt_{l,0}-\min_lt_{l,0}<k\) in absolute value, so all classes abstain, posteriors stay at the prior, and \(\sum_j\pi_jg_{j,0}<c_H\) gives entry \(\rho\). At \(F_1\), under every candidate, a high-group purchase earns at least \(\rho[\min_{\mathcal H}t_{l,1}-\max_\nu\nu\cdot t_1]-k>0\), and its sale at most \(-k\); the low group is symmetric. With these orders, the class proportions within each group stay at the prior proportions: with \(z\) the posterior mass of \(\mathcal H\), \(\mu_j=z\pi_j/\bar\pi\) on \(\mathcal H\) and \(\mu_j=(1-z)\pi_j/(1-\bar\pi)\) on \(\mathcal L\), and \(z=(m(\bar\pi),\bar\pi,M(\bar\pi))\) by the two-class formulas. Gross profit is \(\bar g_{\mathcal L}+z(\bar g_{\mathcal H}-\bar g_{\mathcal L})\). At \(z=\bar\pi\) it equals \(\sum_j\pi_jg_{j,1}\le\sum_j\pi_jg_{j,0}<c_H\) (Proposition 1(a)); at \(M(\bar\pi)\) it exceeds \(c_H\). Separation gives \(\bar t_{\mathcal H}>\bar t_{\mathcal L}\), so the three prices differ, and each reveals \(z\), hence the vector.

Four classes. Take the benchmark slot, costs, reserve, \(r_0\) and \(r_1\), equal priors \(1/4\), and point values \(1\), \(1001/1000\) (low group) and \(9999/1000\), \(10\) (high group). Every vector in \(\mathcal M_\pi\) is \(\nu_j=\pi_jw_j/\sum_l\pi_lw_l\) with \(1\le w_j\le1+u\). The mean \(\nu\cdot t\) is linear-fractional, hence monotone, in each weight, so its extremes lie at the 16 corners of the weight box. They give the separation distance \(\gamma=0.178871\) and the strong trading margin \(\rho\gamma-k=0.024718\).

Why separation matters: a price collision.

Take priors \((1/4,1/2,1/4)\), \(R\sim U[0,8]\), \(p=1\), \(\rho=1/4\), \(c_H=10\), \(k=1/8\), the benchmark slot, and \(G_1=\delta_1\), \(G_2=(1-w)\delta_1+w\delta_{10}\), \(G_3=\delta_{10}\) with \(w=(1+2b_0+\lambda)/2=0.940709\). Since \(\beta>1/4\), \(1-w=(1-\lambda)(4\beta-1)/2>0\). Class 1 sells, class 2 abstains and class 3 buys. Every class gross profit is at most \(95/16<c_H\), so entry is \(\rho\) at every price. The auction terms give \(t=(1,\,1+49w/16,\,65/16)\); normalize to \((0,w,1)\). After zero the posterior weights are proportional to \((b_0/4,(b_0+\lambda)/2,b_0/4)\), and after a purchase to \((b/4,b/2,(b+\lambda)/4)\), so the normalized means are \[z_0=\frac{b_0(w/2+1/4)+\lambda w/2}{b_0+\lambda/2},\qquad z_1=\frac{b(w/2+1/4)+\lambda/4}{b+\lambda/4}.\] Cross-multiply, cancel the common term \(bb_0(w/2+1/4)\), divide by \(\lambda\), and use \(2b+b_0+\lambda=1\): \[z_0=z_1\iff w\,\frac{2b+b_0+\lambda}8=\frac{3b_0}{16}+\frac b8+\frac\lambda8 \iff w=\frac{2b+3b_0+2\lambda}{2(2b+b_0+\lambda)}=\frac{1+2b_0+\lambda}2 .\] Both orders have the price \(1.522107\), but the posterior vectors differ: \((0.154664,0.690671,0.154664)\) after zero and \((0.174878,0.349755,0.475367)\) after a purchase. The chosen orders beat the best alternatives by \(0.260795\), \(0.020627\) and \(0.024768\), so this is an equilibrium with strict choices. The collision leaves entry at \(\rho\); it refutes only the claim that the price always reveals the whole vector. It is a knife-edge: the paper checks that a change of \(1/1000\) in \(w\), in either direction, separates the two prices and keeps every choice strict.

Complementary private signals

[paper Sec. 6.3; Prop. A.7]

Intuition: Accuracy does not measure redundancy

The investor can know the target’s customers; the challenger can know its own integration plans. Two signals about different facts each add news, even when the challenger’s signal is the more accurate one.

Set-up. Prior \(1/2\), values \(h\) and \(\ell\). The investor sees \(T\in\{+,-\}\) with accuracy \(a_T\); the challenger sees \(Y\in\{+,-\}\) with accuracy \(d\); \(1/2<a_T,d<1\). The signals are independent given the class and independent of the slot, the cost and \(R\). The investor orders on \(T\). Write \(\nu(x)=\Pr(T=+\mid X=x)\).

Step 1: the public belief. The order depends on the class only through \(T\), so \(\mu(x)=a_T\nu(x)+(1-a_T)[1-\nu(x)]=(1-a_T)+(2a_T-1)\nu(x)\). The slot bounds of Section 4.2, applied to \(T\), give \(m\le\nu\le M\). So \(\mu_-\le\mu(x)\le\mu_+\), where \(\mu_-\) and \(\mu_+\) are the values of \(\mu\) at \(\nu=m\) and \(\nu=M\).

Step 2: the challenger adds its signal. Bayes’ rule with prior \(\mu\) gives \(\phi_+(\mu)=d\mu/[d\mu+(1-d)(1-\mu)]\) and \(\phi_-(\mu)=(1-d)\mu/[(1-d)\mu+d(1-\mu)]<\phi_+(\mu)\). Let \(I_y\) be high-cost preparation after \(Y=y\); since \(B\) rises, \(I_+\ge I_-\). A class-\(H\) challenger draws \(Y=+\) with probability \(d\), so \[e_H=\rho+(1-\rho)[dI_++(1-d)I_-],\qquad e_L=\rho+(1-\rho)[(1-d)I_++dI_-],\qquad 0\le e_H-e_L\le(1-\rho)(2d-1).\]

Step 3: the price still reveals \(\mu\). Competitive prices give \(P=t_0+\mu e_Hw_H+(1-\mu)e_Lw_L=t_0+e_Lw_L+\mu\Delta_P\), with \(\Delta_P=e_Hw_H-e_Lw_L=e_L\Delta_T+(e_H-e_L)w_H\). Since \(\rho\le e_L\le1\), \[\rho\Delta_T\le\Delta_P\le\Delta_T+(1-\rho)(2d-1)w_H=:D^+(r).\] Entry depends on the price, the signal and the cost, so two orders with one price share \(e_H\) and \(e_L\), and \(\Delta_P\ge\rho\Delta_T>0\) forces equal public posteriors.

Step 4: residuals. After \(T=+\), the investor’s class belief is \(a_T\), so the purchase residual is \((a_T-\mu)\Delta_P=(2a_T-1)[1-\nu(1)]\Delta_P\). After \(T=-\), the sale residual is \([\mu-(1-a_T)]\Delta_P=(2a_T-1)\nu(-1)\Delta_P\). With \(m\le\nu\le M\) and \(1-M=m\), every correct gross return lies in \([(2a_T-1)\rho m\Delta_T,\ (2a_T-1)D^+(r)]\) under every profile. A wrong-signed order earns at most \(-k\).

Result: Complementary signals [paper Prop. A.7]

Suppose \[B_{r_0}(d)<c_H<B_{r_1}\bigl(\phi_+(\mu_+)\bigr),\qquad (2a_T-1)D^+(r_0)<k<(2a_T-1)\rho m\Delta_T(r_1).\] At \(r_0\), orders are zero after both signals, and entry is \(\rho\). At \(r_1\), the investor buys after \(T=+\) and sells after \(T=-\), and entry exceeds \(\rho\). If also \(B_{r_1}(\phi_-(\mu_+))<c_H\), only \((X,Y)=(1,+)\) recruits, and \[\bar e_H=\rho+(1-\rho)d(b+a_T\lambda),\qquad \bar e_L=\rho+(1-\rho)(1-d)[b+(1-a_T)\lambda],\qquad \mathsf{E}_1=\tfrac12(\bar e_H+\bar e_L).\] The conditions allow \(d>a_T\).

The trading bounds fix the orders. At \(r_0\), zero orders give \(\mu\equiv1/2\), so the challenger’s belief is \(d\) or \(1-d\), and \(B_{r_0}(d)<c_H\) keeps the high cost out. At \(r_1\), the purchase posterior is \(\mu_+\), and \(\phi_+(\mu_+)\) recruits. In the one-pair case, a sale or zero has public posterior at most \(1/2\), and \(B_{r_1}(d)\le B_{r_0}(d)<c_H\); a purchase has probability \(b+a_T\lambda\) in class \(H\) and \(b+(1-a_T)\lambda\) in class \(L\).

The vector \(S(B)\) and an exact witness.

Keep the benchmark slot, values, reserve and prior, and set \((\rho,c_H,k,r_0,r_1)=(17/20,357/50,3/200,11/10,23/10)\) and \((a_T,d)=(7/10,3/4)\). Entry rises from \(0.850000\) to \(0.879797\). Without \(Y\), the top public posterior cannot justify the high cost at either strength, and entry stays at \(\rho\): both sources are needed. For a witness in exact fractions, set \((\lambda,\beta)=(1/3,3/10)\), so \(b=1/5\), \(u=5/3\), \(m=3/11\) and \(M=8/11\). The five margins (private exclusion, joint recruitment, weak orders, strong orders, the other pair) are \[\frac{21}{400},\qquad\frac{1363}{36800},\qquad\frac{2}{1375},\qquad\frac{603}{31625},\qquad\frac{76753}{18400}.\] All are positive, with \(d>a_T\).

The benchmark with the accuracies of \(S(B)\).

Restore the benchmark \(\rho\), costs and strengths, and keep \((a_T,d)=(7/10,3/4)\). Three of the four conditions fail, and each equilibrium has a mixed order. Write \(s=\lambda\sigma_+(1)\) and \(t=\lambda\sigma_-(-1)\); then \(1-\nu(1)=b/(2b+s)\) and \(\nu(-1)=b/(2b+t)\).

  • At \(r_0\). Under every profile, \(B_{r_0}(\phi_+(\mu_-))>c_H>B_{r_0}(\phi_-(\mu_+))\): \(Y=+\) recruits at every price and \(Y=-\) never does. So \((e_H,e_L)=(13/16,7/16)\) and \(\Delta_P=\tfrac7{16}\cdot\tfrac1{60}+\tfrac6{16}\cdot\tfrac{33}{80}=311/1920\). The purchase gross return \(\tfrac25\cdot\tfrac{b}{2b+s}\cdot\tfrac{311}{1920}\) falls strictly in \(s\), exceeds \(k\) at \(s=0\), and falls short of \(k\) at \(s=\lambda\). Its unique zero-profit rate solves \[\frac{311b}{4800(2b+s)}=\frac1{50}\iff 2b+s=\frac{311b}{96}\iff s=\frac{119b}{96}.\] The sale gives the same \(t\). Each signal type trades with probability \(119/(96u)=0.721409\), and entry is \(0.625000\).

  • At \(r_1\). \(Y=-\) never recruits. A purchase has public posterior at least \(1/2\), and \(B_{r_1}(d)>c_H\), so \(Y=+\) recruits after it, with \(\Delta_P=\tfrac7{16}\cdot\tfrac23+\tfrac6{16}\cdot\tfrac98=137/192\). The purchase earns at least \(\tfrac25m\tfrac{137}{192}-k>0\), so \(s=\lambda\). After a sale, \(Y=+\) recruits exactly when \(\phi_+(\mu(-1))\ge\tau(r_1)=141/200\), that is \(\mu(-1)\ge47/106\), \(\nu(-1)\ge19/53\), \(t\le15b/19\). On that range the sale earns at least \(\tfrac25\cdot\tfrac{19}{53}\cdot\rho\Delta_T(r_1)=19/795>k\), so the investor would sell with probability one; since \(\lambda>15b/19\), no equilibrium has \(t\le15b/19\). Above that range, entry after a sale is \(\rho\), and the net sale payoff \(\tfrac25\cdot\tfrac{b}{2b+t}\cdot\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac23-\tfrac1{50} =\tfrac{b}{15(2b+t)}-\tfrac1{50}\) falls strictly in \(t\). Its unique zero is \(t=4b/3\in(15b/19,\lambda)\): the sale probability is \(4/(3u)=0.775969\). After zero, \(Y=+\) also recruits, so only a sale blocks recruitment after \(Y=+\). A sale has probability \(b+(1-a_T)t\) in class \(H\) and \(b+a_Tt\) in class \(L\), so \[\mathsf{E}_1=\rho+\frac{1-\rho}2\bigl\{d[1-b-(1-a_T)t]+(1-d)(1-b-a_Tt)\bigr\}=\frac58-\frac{23b}{40}=0.512521.\]

Entry falls in this comparison. Rule IM lets the investor mix at zero profit, and the strict monotonicity of each return makes these tuples unique. Under rule I0, which forces abstention at zero profit, neither strength has an equilibrium. Without \(Y\), entry is \(\rho\) at both strengths, with zero orders at \(r_0\) and rates \(s=t=4b/3\) at \(r_1\), again under rule IM.

The benchmark itself.

At the benchmark the conditions hold with \((a_T,d)=(0.94,0.505)\). Entry rises to \(0.386933\), and it stays at \(\rho\) without \(Y\). The conditions cannot hold there with \(d\ge a_T\). Write \(z=2a_T-1\). The strong bound needs \(z>z_*=k/(m\rho\Delta_T(r_1))\). If \(d\ge a_T\), then \(2d-1\ge z\), and the weak gross bound is \(zD^+(r_0)\ge z_*[\Delta_T(r_0)+(1-\rho)z_*w_H(r_0)]>k\), which violates the weak bound. This limits these sufficient conditions at the benchmark only: \(S(B)\) has \(d>a_T\).

Order sizes and the Laplace comparison

[paper Sec. 2.3, fn. 6]

A liquidity trader places each of the orders \(\{-1,0,1\}\) with positive probability, so every order is on path, and Bayes’ rule defines every price. A menu of continuous sizes needs a convention for the sizes that no class uses, unless liquidity flow covers every size. The Laplace version took the second route. With additive Laplace noise of scale \(s\) (density proportional to \(\mathrm{e}^{-|z|/s}\)) and full orders \(\pm1\), the likelihood ratio of the flow \(x\) is \[\frac{f(x-1)}{f(x+1)}=\exp\Bigl(\frac{|x+1|-|x-1|}{s}\Bigr)\le\mathrm{e}^{2/s},\] since \(|x+1|-|x-1|\le2\). So the two set-ups have the same posterior bounds when \(1+u=\mathrm{e}^{2/s}\), that is \(s=2/\log(1+u)\). The benchmark \(1+u=\mathrm{e}\) corresponds to \(s=2\). The match concerns \(m\) and \(M\), not the experiment or the equilibrium. In the slot, class \(H\) reaches the top posterior with probability \(b+\lambda\) (a purchase); in the Laplace model, it reaches the top plateau \(x\ge1\) with probability \(1/2\). These masses enter entry even when the bounds agree.

Panel note: What changed from the Laplace version

The slot removes the derivative argument over order sizes: you compare three payoffs directly. The old logistic-noise extension has no counterpart. Cost atoms, the moderate value gap and complementary signals still support the reversal, and many classes add a new point: one price need not reveal a whole posterior vector.

Previous: Competition creates researchNext: Welfare and sale terms

12Welfare and sale terms

This chapter holds the incumbent fixed and asks who gains when the challenger sees the price. It then changes the sale terms: the payment rule and the reserve. Write \(\mathcal R\) for expected target proceeds and \(\mathcal W\) for expected acquisition surplus net of preparation costs. Acquisition surplus excludes transfers between bidders, shareholders and traders. [paper Sec. 7; Appendix A.7]

Access to prices: two equilibria with the same orders

[paper Sec. 7.1; Prop. A.8]

Intuition: Same orders, better preparation decisions

At the strong incumbent the investor trades fully even when the challenger cannot see the price. So access leaves the orders and the trading costs unchanged. It changes one decision: a high-cost challenger now prepares after a purchase, where its expected gross profit exceeds its cost.

Hold the incumbent at \(F_1\) under the hypotheses of Proposition 2, and solve two economies. With access, the equilibrium is the strong tuple \(\mathcal E_1\) of Section 6.4: full orders, posteriors \((m,\pi,M)\) and entry \((\rho,\rho,1)\). With a hidden price, the challenger’s belief stays at \(\pi\), and \(B_1(\pi)\le B_0(\pi)<c_H\), so entry is \(\rho\) after every order. The trading bound holds for every entry in \([\rho,1]\) (Section 5.4), so full orders are again unique; Section 7.3 solves this economy. The orders, and hence the trading costs, coincide at every realization.

Result: Access to prices [paper Prop. A.8]

Under the hypotheses of Proposition 2, at \(F_1\), with superscript \(1\) for access, \(0\) for the hidden price and \(w_j=t_j-t_0\): \[\begin{align*} \mathcal R^1-\mathcal R^0&=(1-\rho)\bigl\{\pi(b+\lambda)w_H+(1-\pi)\,b\,w_L\bigr\}>0,\\ \mathcal W^1-\mathcal W^0&=(1-\rho)(b+\pi\lambda)\bigl[B_1(M)-c_H+pF_1(p)\bigr]>0. \end{align*}\]

The proceeds gain.

Couple the two economies on the class, the value, the cost, the slot identity and the liquidity order. Only high-cost preparation after a purchase differs. A purchase has probability \(b+\lambda\) in class \(H\) and \(b\) in class \(L\) (Section 6.5). The cost is high with probability \(1-\rho\). Each added preparation raises expected proceeds from \(t_0\) to \(t_j\), that is by \(w_j\). Multiply the three factors and weight the classes by \(\pi\) and \(1-\pi\). Both terms are nonnegative, and \(w_H=w_L+\Delta_1\ge\Delta_1>0\), so the gain is positive.

The surplus gain.

Without a challenger the allocation value is \(\mathcal V_0(R)=R\,\mathbf{1}\{R\ge p\}\). Truthful bids give the target to the highest admissible value, so an admissible challenger with value \(\theta\) adds \[\max\{\mathcal V_0(R),\theta\}-\mathcal V_0(R)=(\theta-\max\{p,R\})_++p\,\mathbf{1}\{R<p\}.\] If \(R<p\), both sides equal \(\theta\), since \((\theta-p)+p=\theta\). If \(R\ge p\), both sides equal \((\theta-R)_+\). Take the expectation over \(R\) and over the class at the purchase posterior \(M\). The first term gives \(B_1(M)\) and the second gives \(pF_1(p)\), the value of the sales that the reserve would otherwise block. Subtract \(c_H\), and multiply by the probability of an added preparation, \((1-\rho)\Pr(X=1)=(1-\rho)(b+\pi\lambda)\). The bracket is positive because \(B_1(M)>c_H\) by (H1). The gain is an expectation at the purchase posterior, not a gain at each realized value.

Benchmark check.

At \(r_1\), \(w_H=9/8\), \(w_L=11/24\) (Section 3.3) and \(pF_1(p)=\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac{1/2}{3}=\tfrac1{12}\). With \(\pi=1/2\) and \(\rho=1/4\), \[\mathcal R^1-\mathcal R^0=\frac38\Bigl[\frac98(b+\lambda)+\frac{11}{24}b\Bigr]=0.257948,\qquad \mathcal W^1-\mathcal W^0=\frac34\Bigl(b+\frac\lambda2\Bigr)\Bigl[B_1(M)-6+\frac1{12}\Bigr]=0.081960.\] In levels, proceeds rise from \(0.614583\) to \(0.872531\), and net surplus from \(2.552083\) to \(2.634044\). The surplus level is \(\mathbb{E}[\mathcal V_0(R)]=(r^2-p^2)/(2r)\), plus \(g_j+p^2/r\) for each prepared class-\(j\) challenger, minus \(c_H\) for each high-cost preparation; the low cost is zero. If the execution cost is a resource cost, both economies lose \(\lambda k\), and the gains do not change. With research at the cap (Section 10.6), research and trading costs also coincide, and the gains are the same, \(0.257948\) and \(0.081960\).

Transfers, trade scale and the limits of the gain

[paper Sec. 7.1; Appendix A.7]

A shareholder who holds through the sale receives \(V_T\), not the interim price, so target shareholders receive the proceeds gain. The investor’s profit is a transfer. In the access economy class \(H\) buys and meets entry one, so \(K_H=\Delta_1(1-M)\); class \(L\) sells and meets entry \(\rho\), so \(K_L=\rho\Delta_1m\) (Section 5.2). Weight by the class probabilities and by the presence probability: \[G=\lambda\Delta_1\{\pi(1-M)+(1-\pi)\rho m\}=0.037665,\qquad G/\lambda=0.112059.\] Competitive prices give \(\mathbb{E}[V_T-P(X)\mid X]=0\), so \(\mathbb{E}[X(V_T-P(X))]=0\). Split this expectation by who filled the slot: the informed part is \(G\), so liquidity traders lose \(G\) in expected gross terms. Market makers earn zero, and the execution cost is separate.

The scale of trade.

Let one order unit be \(z\) shares per share of float, with the same signal experiment. Without the investor every posterior is \(\pi\) and entry is \(\rho\), so proceeds are \(\mathcal R^0\). Even if initial shareholders bear the whole liquidity loss, they gain over that economy exactly when \[(\mathcal R^1-\mathcal R^0)-zG>0\iff z<\frac{\mathcal R^1-\mathcal R^0}{G}=6.848394.\] This holds the signal experiment fixed; it does not solve a game in which the investor chooses its size.

Information, not the price level.

Add a deterministic external dividend of \(0.257948\) to the traded claim of the hidden-price economy. \(V_T\) and every price rise by that amount, so mean prices agree across the economies, while every order payoff, the challenger’s information and entry stay the same.

Access outside the recruitment region.

Suppose instead \(B_1(m)<c_H<B_1(\pi)\), with \(k<\rho\kappa\Delta_1\). Without the price every challenger prepares, so entry is one after every order. With the price, entry is \((\rho,1,1)\), and orders are full in both economies. Access now removes high-cost preparation after a sale, which has probability \(b\) in class \(H\) and \(b+\lambda\) in class \(L\). The same steps give \[\begin{align*} \mathcal R^1-\mathcal R^0&=-(1-\rho)\{\pi b\,w_H+(1-\pi)(b+\lambda)w_L\}<0,\\ \mathcal W^1-\mathcal W^0&=-(1-\rho)[b+(1-\pi)\lambda]\,[B_1(m)-c_H+pF_1(p)]. \end{align*}\] At the strong benchmark with \(c_H=12/5\), \(B_1(m)-c_H=-0.033821\), but the bracket is positive because \(pF_1(p)=1/12\). Access changes proceeds by \(-0.173917\) and surplus by \(-0.013505\).

Caution: State the welfare domain

The positive result holds where the high cost does not pay at the prior. Do not say that price access raises proceeds or surplus for every cost.

The payment rule: why the sign can change

[paper Sec. 7.2; Prop. A.9]

Proposition 1 belongs to the cash second-price rule (Section 3.5). Now change the payment rule. The institution has zero reserve and verifiable values. After diligence the buyer with the highest value acquires the target, and a sale to the next buyer at its value is an enforceable fallback. The seller and the winner split the surplus above the fallback by Nash bargaining, with seller weight \(\omega\in[0,1)\). Keep the point-mass classes, and let the incumbent’s support be \([0,\bar r]\) with \(\ell<\bar r<h\).

Result: The payment rule [paper Prop. A.9]

\[\begin{gather*} T_\omega(R,\theta)=(1-\omega)\min\{R,\theta\}+\omega\max\{R,\theta\},\qquad g_{\theta,\omega}=(1-\omega)\,\mathbb{E}[(\theta-R)_+],\\ \Delta_\omega=\omega(h-\ell)+(1-2\omega)\,\mathbb{E}[(R-\ell)_+]. \end{gather*}\] A stronger incumbent weakly lowers \(g_{\theta,\omega}\), and it moves the spread with the weak sign of \(1-2\omega\); the move is strict when \(\mathbb{E}[(R-\ell)_+]\) changes strictly and \(\omega\ne1/2\). At \(\omega=1/2\), \(\Delta_\omega=(h-\ell)/2\) for every incumbent distribution.

The payment.

Let the winner’s value be \(V\) and the fallback \(z\le V\). For \(0<\omega<1\) and \(V>z\), the payment \(P\in[z,V]\) maximizes \((P-z)^\omega(V-P)^{1-\omega}\). Its logarithm has the derivative \(\omega/(P-z)-(1-\omega)/(V-P)\), which falls strictly in \(P\). Set it to zero: \[\omega(V-P)=(1-\omega)(P-z)\iff P=(1-\omega)z+\omega V.\] At \(\omega=0\) or \(V=z\) the payment is \(z\), as the formula gives. With a challenger, \(V=\max\{R,\theta\}\) and \(z=\min\{R,\theta\}\), which is \(T_\omega\). Without one, the fallback is zero and the seller receives \(\omega R\).

Profit and spread.

A challenger wins when \(\theta>R\) and keeps \(\theta-T_\omega=(1-\omega)(\theta-R)\); this gives \(g_{\theta,\omega}\). For the spread, compare the classes at each \(R<h\). If \(R\le\ell\), both classes win and pay \((1-\omega)R+\omega\theta\), so the difference is \(\omega(h-\ell)\). If \(\ell<R<h\), the high class wins, and against the low class the incumbent wins and pays \((1-\omega)\ell+\omega R\): \[T_\omega(R,h)-T_\omega(R,\ell)=[(1-\omega)R+\omega h]-[(1-\omega)\ell+\omega R]=\omega(h-\ell)+(1-2\omega)(R-\ell).\] Both cases equal \(\omega(h-\ell)+(1-2\omega)(R-\ell)_+\), and the expectation over \(R\) gives \(\Delta_\omega\). The condition \(\bar r<h\) matters: for \(R\ge h\) both classes lose, and the difference would be \((1-\omega)(h-\ell)\). Finally, \((\theta-R)_+\) falls and \((R-\ell)_+\) rises in \(R\), so a first-order strengthening weakly lowers \(g_{\theta,\omega}\) and moves \(\Delta_\omega\) with the sign of \(1-2\omega\).

Intuition: Sensitivities

Take \(R>\ell\). The high-class payment \((1-\omega)R+\omega h\) moves with \(R\) at rate \(1-\omega\). Against the low class the incumbent wins and pays \((1-\omega)\ell+\omega R\), which moves at rate \(\omega\). Competition widens the gap only if \(1-\omega>\omega\). The zero-reserve second-price auction is \(\omega=0\): all weight on the runner-up’s value.

For a uniform incumbent, \(\mathbb{E}[(R-\ell)_+]=(r-\ell)^2/(2r)=\Delta_T(r)\) (Section 3.3), so \(\Delta_\omega(r_1)-\Delta_\omega(r_0)=(1-2\omega)(\tfrac23-\tfrac1{60})=13/20\,(1-2\omega)\). State the scope before the panel does: the result covers the payment stage only, not the trading and preparation equilibrium under Nash bargaining.

Reserves: preparation, sale and admissible bids

[paper Sec. 7.3; Prop. A.10; Table 5]

Now let the reserve take any value, so a class value can lie below it. For a point value \(v\) and \(R\sim U[0,r]\), with \(t_0=p(1-p/r)_+\), \[(t_v,g_v,W_v)=\begin{cases} (t_0,0,0),&v<p,\\ (p,\,v-p,\,1),&r\le p\le v,\\ \bigl(v-\tfrac{v^2-p^2}{2r},\,\tfrac{v^2-p^2}{2r},\,\tfrac vr\bigr),&p\le v\le r,\\[3pt] \bigl(\tfrac r2+\tfrac{p^2}{2r},\,v-\tfrac r2-\tfrac{p^2}{2r},\,1\bigr),&p\le r\le v. \end{cases}\] Below the reserve the challenger cannot bid, so the outcome is that of no entry. If \(r\le p\le v\), the incumbent never meets the reserve, and the challenger buys alone at \(p\). The last two rows are the integrals of Section 3.3 with \(\ell\) or \(h\) replaced by \(v\). The pieces agree at \(v=r\) and at \(p=r\), and a class law averages the kernel.

Exclusion can widen the spread.

For point values with \(\ell<p<r<h\), the low class adds nothing: \(t_L=t_0=p-p^2/r\) and \(g_L=0\), while \(t_H=r/2+p^2/(2r)\). So \(\Delta_T=r/2-p+3p^2/(2r)\), the spread of the worked example in Section 3.6. For \(r<p<h\), \(t_0=t_L=g_L=0\), \(t_H=p\), \(g_H=h-p\) and \(\Delta_T=p\). A larger spread can make full orders pay even against the weak incumbent.

Preparation, sale and competition differ.

Let \(I\) indicate preparation and \(a=\Pr(I=1,\theta\ge p)\). Entry is \(\Pr(I=1)\). Two admissible bidders meet with probability \((1-p/r)_+a\), because \(R\) is independent of \((I,\theta)\), and a sale occurs with probability \((1-p/r)_++a-(1-p/r)_+a\). The zero-cost challenger prepares by the model’s convention, so preparation can occur without an admissible bid.

Result: Reserves [paper Prop. A.10]

Fix a reserve. Keep the class order, the costs, the slot and a strengthening \(F_1\le F_0\), and compute each class object with the reserve. Suppose \(k<\rho\kappa\min\{\Delta_0,\Delta_1\}\).

  1. If \(B_0(\pi)<c_H<B_1(M)\), entry is flat: \(\mathsf{E}_0=\mathsf{E}_1=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\pi\lambda)\).

  2. If \(B_0(\pi)<c_H<B_0(M)\) and \(B_1(M)<c_H\), entry falls strictly: \(\mathsf{E}_0=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\pi\lambda)>\rho=\mathsf{E}_1\).

Each economy has a unique outcome under every pair of tie rules.

Derivation. The trading bound of Section 5.4 needs only \(\Delta_T\ge0\) and entry in \([\rho,1]\), so it holds with the reserve kernel. Full orders are forced at both strengths, and the posteriors are \((m,\pi,M)\). Gross profit falls with strength at every belief, because \((\theta-\max\{p,R\})_+\) is nonincreasing in \(R\). In part (a), \(B_1(\pi)\le B_0(\pi)<c_H<B_1(M)\le B_0(M)\), so only a purchase recruits, at both strengths. In part (b), the weak strength has the same pattern, and at the strong strength \(B_1(\mu)\le B_1(M)<c_H\) at every posterior. A purchase has probability \(b+\pi\lambda\) (Section 6.5). Every comparison is strict, so no tie rule matters.

Reserve Strength Preparation Sale Two admissible Proceeds
\(1/2\) \(r_0\) 0.250000 0.687500 0.145833 0.392708
\(r_1\) 0.522757 0.920459 0.435631 0.872531
\(101/100\) \(r_0\) 0.522757 0.431371 0.051364 0.440564
\(r_1\) 0.522757 0.772548 0.215186 0.994384
\(31/20\) \(r_0\) 0.522757 0.324401 0 0.502822
\(r_1\) 0.250000 0.547917 0.060417 0.893073

The rows at \(p=1/2\) repeat Proposition 2, with no trade at \(r_0\). The reserve \(101/100\) excludes the low class, and part (a) gives flat entry. Entry is exactly flat because the probability of a purchase, \(b+\pi\lambda\), does not depend on strength. The reserve \(31/20\) gives strict deterrence by part (b); it exceeds \(r_0\), so it also excludes the weak incumbent.

Where strict deterrence holds.

With the low class excluded, \(g_L=0\), so gross profit at the top posterior is \(Mg_H\). At \(r_1\) it equals the cost when \[M\Bigl(h-\frac{r_1}{2}-\frac{p^2}{2r_1}\Bigr)=c_H\iff p=p_H:=\sqrt{2r_1(h-c_H/M)-r_1^2},\] and \(B_1(M)<c_H\) above \(p_H\). At \(r_0<p\), \(g_H=h-p\), so \(B_0(M)>c_H\) exactly when \(p<h-c_H/M\). Strict deterrence therefore holds for \(p_H\approx1.325270<p<h-c_H/M\approx1.792723\). The other hypotheses of part (b) hold there: \(B_0(1/2)=(h-p)/2<c_H\), and both spreads rise with \(p\), so the margin of the trading bound, \(\rho\kappa\min\{\Delta_0,\Delta_1\}-k\), is at least its value \(0.050792\) at \(p_H\). Both endpoints exceed \(r_0\). For \(\ell<p\le r_0\), the top posterior still recruits at both strengths, and entry is flat. At either endpoint the challenger’s tie rule matters. None of this identifies the seller’s optimal reserve.

Previous: RobustnessNext: Empirical implications

13Empirical implications

The empirical results are predictions of the model, not estimates from takeover data. They use the benchmark auction, the prior \(1/2\) and a slot in the band of Proposition A.1, and they concern execution prices, preparation decisions and winners. [paper Sec. 8; Appendix A.8]

The domain and the observable order law

Full two-sided trade on \((r_S,h)\).

Let \(r_S\) be the strength at which a full sale earns the trading cost, \(\rho\Delta_T(r_S)m=k\); Section 9.3 solves it with the root map. For \(r_S<r<h\), the bounds of Section 5.4 give, under every candidate, \[K_H\ge\rho\Delta_T(r)(1-M)=\rho\Delta_T(r)m>k,\qquad K_L\ge\rho\Delta_T(r)m>k,\] because \(1-M=m\) at the prior \(1/2\) and \(\Delta_T\) rises in \(r\). So full orders are unique under both investor rules. The interval contains \(r_1\), \(r_C\) and \(r_2\). Use the exact root for strict claims: the display \(r_S\approx2.124148\) lies below \(r_S\), and at \(r=2.1241481\), above the display, the full sale earns \(\rho\Delta_Tm-k=-8.59\times10^{-10}<0\).

The order law.

Under full orders class \(H\) buys with probability \(b+\lambda\) and class \(L\) with probability \(b\). At the prior \(1/2\), write \(\bar f=b+\lambda/2\). Then \(\Pr(X=\pm1)=\bar f\), \(\Pr(X=0)=1-2\bar f\) and \(\mathbb{E}[X]=0\). The volume \(v=\Pr(X\ne0)=2b+\lambda\) counts nonzero orders per order opportunity. The informed share of nonzero orders is \[\vartheta=\frac{\lambda}{2b+\lambda}=\frac{u}{2+u}=M-m=2M-1,\] where the last two forms use \(M=(1+u)/(2+u)\) and \(m=1/(2+u)\) at the prior \(1/2\). The share differs from the presence probability: at the benchmark, \(v=0.727352\), \(\vartheta=0.462117\) and \(\lambda=0.336122\).

Entry.

Write \(A_r(\mu)=t_L-t_0+\Delta_T(r)\mu\); this is the average entry gain \(w(\mu)\) of Section 4.6 at strength \(r\). Let \(\chi_r=\mathbf{1}\{B_r(M)\ge c_H\}\). Only a purchase can recruit, and \(B_r(M)\) falls strictly in \(r\) (Section 6.6), so under rule C1 \(\chi_r=1\) exactly when \(r\le r_C\). Entry after \((-1,0,1)\) is \((\rho,\rho,e_+)\) with \(e_+=\rho+(1-\rho)\chi_r\), so \(\mathsf{E}=\rho+(1-\rho)\chi_r\bar f\) (Section 6.5). From \(r_0\) to \(r_1\) entry rises by \((1-\rho)\bar f=0.272757\). With the challenger’s information held fixed, a stronger incumbent cannot raise entry (Section 7.1); the rise needs the change in information.

The execution spread and its entry component

[paper Prop. A.11]

Intuition: A purchase moves two things

A sale and a purchase move the class belief by the same amount in opposite directions. A purchase can also recruit a high-cost challenger. That second effect enters the purchase price alone.

By Section 4.6, \(P(-1)=t_0+\rho A_r(m)\), \(P(0)=t_0+\rho A_r(1/2)\) and \(P(1)=t_0+e_+A_r(M)\). Define the spread \(S=P(1)-P(-1)\), the purchase impact \(I_+=P(1)-P(0)\) and the sale impact \(I_-=P(0)-P(-1)\).

Result: The spread and its entry component [paper Prop. A.11]

Let \(r_S<r<h\).

  1. \(S=\rho\Delta_T\vartheta+(1-\rho)\chi_rA_r(M)\): a class component and an entry component.

  2. \(I_-=\rho\Delta_T\vartheta/2\) and \(I_+=I_-+(1-\rho)\chi_rA_r(M)\). So \(I_+>I_-\) exactly when a purchase recruits.

  3. With a hidden price, full orders remain unique, and \(S=\rho\Delta_T\vartheta\).

  4. On \((r_S,r_C]\), both components, both impacts and \(S\) rise strictly with \(r\). On \((r_C,h)\), the entry component is zero, and the class component, both impacts and \(S\) rise strictly.

  5. At \(r_C\), \(S\), \(P(1)\) and \(I_+\) fall by \((1-\rho)A_{r_C}(M)>0\); \(P(-1)\), \(P(0)\) and \(I_-\) are continuous.

Derivation. Since \(e_+=\rho+(1-\rho)\chi_r\), \(S=\rho[A_r(M)-A_r(m)]+(1-\rho)\chi_rA_r(M)\). The function \(A_r\) has slope \(\Delta_T\), so \(A_r(M)-A_r(m)=\Delta_T\vartheta\): this is (i). Since \(m+M=1\), \(\tfrac12-m=M-\tfrac12=\vartheta/2\), which gives both impacts; and \(A_r(M)>0\), which gives the test in (ii). With a hidden price entry is \(\rho\) after every order (Section 7.3), so \(\chi_r\) drops out: (iii). At the benchmark \(t_L-t_0=\tfrac12-\tfrac1{8r}\) and \(\Delta_T\) rise in \(r\), so \(A_r(M)\) rises, while \(\rho\) and \(\vartheta\) do not move: (iv). Rule C1 gives \(\chi_{r_C}=1\), and \(\chi_r=0\) above \(r_C\); every other term is continuous: (v). The test in (ii) needs no auction values.

Benchmark check.

At \(r_1\), \(S=0.077020+0.709279=0.786299\), so the entry component is \(90.2\%\) of the spread. The impacts are \(I_-=0.038510\) and \(I_+=0.747789\). With a hidden price the spread would be \(0.077020\). At \(r_2\) it is \(0.108469\), the class component alone, and at \(r_C\) it falls by \(0.861835\). Under rule CM the value at \(r_C\) can lie anywhere between the two one-sided limits.

Caution: This is not a quoted bid-ask spread

The model has no quote stage. \(S\) is the difference between the execution prices of a unit purchase and a unit sale, each conditional on its order. The sale impact uses the price after a zero order as its reference.

Identify the informed share from prices and counts

[paper Prop. A.12]

Counts alone.

Under full orders the order law \((v/2,1-v,v/2)\) and entry \(\rho+(1-\rho)\chi_rv/2\) depend on \((\lambda,\beta)\) only through \(v\). Fix a ratio \(u'>0\) and solve \(v=2b'+\lambda'\), \(\lambda'=u'b'\): \[b'=\frac{v}{2+u'},\qquad \lambda'=\frac{vu'}{2+u'},\qquad \beta'=\frac{b'}{1-\lambda'}=\frac{v}{2+u'(1-v)}.\] This pair is unique, and it is a slot of the model exactly when \(\beta'\le1/3\), that is \(3v\le2+u'(1-v)\). In the share \(\vartheta'=u'/(2+u')\), so that \(u'=2\vartheta'/(1-\vartheta')\), the right side is \((2-2v\vartheta')/(1-\vartheta')\), and \[3v(1-\vartheta')\le2-2v\vartheta'\iff v\vartheta'\ge3v-2\iff\vartheta'\ge3-\frac2v.\] So every share from \(3-2/v\) up gives one slot with the same counts. The counts do not choose among them.

Incentives.

A slot with share \(\vartheta'\) has \(m'=(1-\vartheta')/2\). A sale never recruits, so under full orders the low-class sale earns \(\rho\Delta_T(r)m'-k\), which is nonnegative only if \(\vartheta'\le\bar\vartheta(r)=1-2k/(\rho\Delta_T(r))\). At \(r_1\), \(\bar\vartheta=1-\frac{1/25}{1/6}=0.760000\). So under rule IM the counts and the incentives at \(r_1\) allow \(\vartheta'\in[3-2/v,\ \bar\vartheta(r_1)]\approx[0.250299,\ 0.760000]\). At the upper end the full sale earns exactly zero. A lower sale rate would raise \(\mu(-1)\) and make the sale strictly profitable, so no mixture replaces the full sale. Rule I0 excludes the full sale there, and that slot then has no equilibrium at \(r_1\).

The band.

Strict recruitment at \(r_1\) and strict exclusion at \(r_2\) are \(\tau(r_1)<M'<\tau(r_2)\), that is, with \(\vartheta'=2M'-1\), \[2\tau(r_1)-1=\frac{41}{100}<\vartheta'<\frac{1343}{2902}=2\tau(r_2)-1.\] Every share in this band exceeds \(3-2/v\), so it has a slot with the benchmark counts and the benchmark entry at \(r_1\) and \(r_2\). Full orders are unique at both strengths, because the weakest correct-order bound at \(r_1\), \(\rho\Delta_T(r_1)[1-\tau(r_2)]-k=0.024768\), is positive, and \(\Delta_T(r_2)>\Delta_T(r_1)\). For example, \(u'=8/5\) gives \(\vartheta'=4/9=0.444444\) and the same entry, but a spread of \(0.778935\) at \(r_1\), against \(0.786299\). Prices separate slots that counts cannot.

Result: Recover the slot from prices [paper Prop. A.12(d)]

Let \(r_S<r<h\). Given \(\rho=\Pr(\text{preparation}\mid X\ne1)\) and the spread \(\Delta_T(r)\), the sale impact and the volume identify the slot: \[\vartheta=\frac{2I_-}{\rho\Delta_T(r)},\qquad \lambda=v\vartheta,\qquad \beta=\frac{v(1-\vartheta)}{2(1-v\vartheta)}.\]

The first formula inverts \(I_-=\rho\Delta_T\vartheta/2\); the second uses \(\vartheta=\lambda/v\); the third uses \(b=v(1-\vartheta)/2\) and \(\beta=b/(1-\lambda)\). The floor is observable because entry after a sale and after zero is \(\rho\). The target-payoff spread \(\Delta_T(r)\) remains a maintained input of the auction model.

Counts under purchases only.

On \([r_F,r_N]\), form F of Section 9.5 has class \(H\) buying with probability one and class \(L\) abstaining. Then \(\Pr(X=1)=b+\lambda/2\), \(\Pr(X=-1)=b\), \(\mathbb{E}[X]=\lambda/2\) and \(v=2b+\lambda/2\), so \(\lambda=2\mathbb{E}[X]\) and \(b=(v-\mathbb{E}[X])/2\): the counts identify the slot. On branch R class \(H\) buys with probability \(\sigma_H(1)<1\), and the same algebra identifies only the purchase rate \(s_+=\lambda\sigma_H(1)=2\mathbb{E}[X]\) and \(b\), not \(\lambda\).

Orders predict the winner after entry becomes flat

[paper Prop. A.13]

Let \(A\) be the event that the challenger prepares and \(W\) the event that it acquires the target. The cost is independent of the class and of \(R\), so preparation leaves the class posterior unchanged: \(\Pr(H\mid X=x,A)=\mu(x)\). A prepared high-class challenger always wins, because \(h>r\); a prepared low-class challenger wins when \(R<\ell\), with probability \(\ell/r\). Thus \[w_x:=\Pr(W\mid X=x,A)=\mu(x)+[1-\mu(x)]\,\ell/r,\qquad \Pr(W\mid X=x)=e(x)\,w_x,\] and under full orders \(w_1-w_{-1}=(M-m)(1-\ell/r)=\vartheta(1-\ell/r)\). The gap rises with \(r\), with derivative \(\vartheta\ell/r^2>0\). It is \(0.308078\) at \(r_1\) and \(0.333751\) at \(r_2\).

Since \(\mathbb{E}[X]=0\), an indicator \(Y\) with conditional means \(y_x\) has \(\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)=\mathbb{E}[XY]=\bar f(y_1-y_{-1})\). For preparation \(y_1-y_{-1}=e_+-\rho\), so \(\operatorname{Cov}(X,\mathbf{1}_A)=(1-\rho)\chi_r\bar f\): \(0.272757\) at \(r_1\) and zero above \(r_C\). Above \(r_C\), \(e\equiv\rho\), so \(\operatorname{Cov}(X,\mathbf{1}_W)=\bar f\rho\vartheta(1-\ell/r)>0\), which is \(0.030344\) at \(r_2\). Zero covariance between orders and entry does not mean that the price carries no class information.

Announcement returns and the zero order

[paper Prop. A.14]

Add a public event after the price that reveals only whether the challenger prepared. With preparation the price becomes \(t_0+A_r(\mu(x))\); without it, \(t_0\). Subtract \(P(x)=t_0+e(x)A_r(\mu(x))\): \[\Delta_x^1=[1-e(x)]A_r(\mu(x)),\qquad \Delta_x^0=-e(x)A_r(\mu(x)).\] The report of no preparation has probability \(1-e(x)\). When \(e(x)<1\), the mean change over both reports is \(e(x)[1-e(x)]A_r-[1-e(x)]e(x)A_r=0\). When \(e(x)=1\), a report of preparation changes nothing.

A sample of preparation reports alone weights order \(x\) by \(\Pr(X=x\mid A)=\Pr(X=x)e(x)/\mathsf{E}\), so its mean change is \(\sum_x\Pr(X=x\mid A)[1-e(x)]A_r(\mu(x))\). Every term is nonnegative. The posteriors average to the prior, so some order has \(\mu(x)\le1/2\). At the benchmark \(B_r(1/2)<c_H\) for \(1<r<h\), so \(\tau(r)>1/2\ge\mu(x)\), and entry at that order is \(\rho<1\). The order has positive probability, so the mean is positive. At \(r_1\) the changes after a sale, zero and a purchase are \((0.478221,\ 0.593750,\ 0)\), with mean \(0.160592\) in the sample of preparation reports. These are price changes; a percentage return divides by the price before the event.

No order is bad news about entry.

The expected price before the order opportunity is \(\bar P=\sum_x\Pr(X=x)P(x)\). Since \(A_r\) is affine and \(m+M=1\), \(\bar fA_r(m)+\bar fA_r(M)=2\bar fA_r(1/2)\), so \[\begin{align*} \bar P&=t_0+\rho\bigl[\bar fA_r(m)+(1-2\bar f)A_r(1/2)+\bar fA_r(M)\bigr]+(1-\rho)\chi_r\bar fA_r(M)\\ &=t_0+\rho A_r(1/2)+(1-\rho)\chi_r\bar fA_r(M)=P(0)+(1-\rho)\chi_r\bar fA_r(M). \end{align*}\] At \(r_1\), \(\bar P=0.872531\), \(P(0)=0.614583\), and \(P(0)-\bar P=-0.257948\). The zero order leaves the class posterior at \(1/2\) but removes the chance of recruitment after a purchase. This difference is minus the access gain of Section 12.1: \(\bar P\) equals expected proceeds with access, and \(P(0)\) equals expected proceeds with a hidden price. Above \(r_C\) the difference is zero.

Two clusters at one intermediate strength

[paper Sec. 8; Prop. 3; Appendix A.8]

For \(r_F<r<r_N\), Proposition 3 gives exactly two equilibria under rules IM and C1 (Section 9.5). In N both classes abstain, the three prices coincide and entry is \(\rho\). In F class \(H\) buys, class \(L\) abstains, the mean order is \(\lambda/2=0.168061\), and every challenger prepares after a purchase.

F has a price pattern that a test can use. After a sale the posterior is \(1/2\), because \(f_H(-1)=f_L(-1)=b\). After zero, \(f_H(0)=b_0\) and \(f_L(0)=b_0+\lambda\), with \(b_0=1-2b-\lambda\). With \(v_U=2b+\lambda/2\), \[\mu_0=\frac{b_0}{2b_0+\lambda}=\frac{1-2b-\lambda}{2-4b-\lambda},\qquad \frac12-\mu_0=\frac{\lambda}{2(2-4b-\lambda)}=\frac{\lambda}{4(1-v_U)}.\] Both orders have entry \(\rho\), so \(P(-1)-P(0)=\rho\Delta_T\lambda/[4(1-v_U)]>0\): a sale executes above the price after zero. At \(r=1.60\) the difference is \(0.005363\). Rule CM adds the threshold form T as a third tuple, so the two-cluster statement needs rule C1. The model does not say which equilibrium a process selects.

Measurement: date the decision and retain zero orders

[paper Sec. 8]

  1. Preparation is the start of substantive diligence, or a proposal that needs costly preparation; a final public offer is a narrower, selected event.

  2. Incumbent strength and the price must both be public before the further buyer’s decision; a later price can reflect anticipation of the bid.

  3. The order is the first prespecified public order opportunity after the sale becomes public, such as a scheduled call auction. An opportunity with no order, or an imbalance below a size fixed in advance, is a zero, and it stays in the denominator of \(v\).

  4. The announcement formulas need events that reveal preparation alone.

Caution: The omitted zero changes the sample

A sample of first executed trades discards the inactive opportunities. It cannot identify the volume, the price effect of a zero order or the informed share. The share formula also needs \(\rho\) and \(\Delta_T(r)\), so it is conditional on the maintained auction model.

Previous: Welfare and sale termsNext: Panel preparation
Part III

Whiteboard derivations and questions

14Panel preparation

This chapter collects what you need in the room: seven derivations to do from memory, the likely questions with answers, the points that a referee will press, the terms to use exactly, and a plan for a 15-minute talk. Each answer points to the chapter that derives it in full.

Seven derivations to do on a whiteboard

Practise these until you can do each one in about two minutes, without notes.

W1. The spread.

If \(R\le\ell\), both classes pay \(\max\{p,R\}\). If \(R>\ell\), class \(H\) wins and pays \(R\), and against class \(L\) the incumbent wins and pays \(\ell\). So the gap is \((R-\ell)_+\). With \(R\sim U[0,r]\), \[\Delta_T=\frac1r\int_\ell^r(y-\ell)\,dy=\frac{(r-\ell)^2}{2r},\qquad \Delta_T'(r)=\frac{r^2-\ell^2}{2r^2}>0 .\] For general classes, the class ranking gives \(\Delta_T(F)=\int_p^\infty[1-F(y)]D(y)\,dy\): a stronger incumbent moves slices \(D(y)\,dy\) of the value gap to the target (Sections 3.3 and 3.5).

W2. Posterior bounds and \(\beta\le1/3\).

For \(x=\pm1\), every profile gives \(b\le f_j(x)\le b+\lambda\). So \(1/(1+u)\le f_H(x)/f_L(x)\le1+u\). The odds form of Bayes’ rule, \[\frac{\mu(x)}{1-\mu(x)}=\frac{\pi}{1-\pi}\cdot\frac{f_H(x)}{f_L(x)},\] puts \(\mu(\pm1)\) in \([m(\pi),M(\pi)]\). At zero the liquidity mass is \(b_0=(1-\lambda)(1-2\beta)\), and \(b_0\ge b\iff1-2\beta\ge\beta\iff\beta\le1/3\). A price belief averages the posteriors of the orders with that price, so it obeys the same bounds (Sections 4.2, 4.3 and 4.5).

W3. The residual advantage and \(\kappa\).

With \(\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid j,X=q]=t_0+e(q)w_j\) and \(P(q)=t_0+e(q)[w_L+\Delta_T\mu(q)]\), subtract: \[\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid H,q]-P(q)=e(q)\Delta_T[1-\mu(q)],\qquad P(q)-\mathbb{E}[V_T\mid L,q]=e(q)\Delta_T\mu(q).\] Since \(1-M(\pi)=m(1-\pi)\) and \(m\) rises, \(\kappa(\pi)=\min\{m(\pi),1-M(\pi)\}=m(\min\{\pi,1-\pi\})\). Its maximum is \(\kappa(1/2)=1/(2+u)\) (Sections 5.2 and 5.5).

W4. The global trading bounds.

Use the floor \(e\ge\rho\) and the bounds of W2: \[K_H=e(1)\Delta_T[1-\mu(1)]\ge\rho\Delta_T[1-M]\ge\rho\kappa\Delta_T,\qquad K_L=e(-1)\Delta_T\mu(-1)\ge\rho\Delta_Tm\ge\rho\kappa\Delta_T,\] and \(K_H,K_L\le\Delta_T\). A wrong-signed order earns at most \(-k\). So \(\Delta_T<k\) forces zero orders, and \(k<\rho\kappa\Delta_T\) forces full orders, under every candidate (Sections 5.4 and 5.6).

W5. The threshold \(\tau\).

\(B(\mu)=g_L+\mu(g_H-g_L)\ge c_H\) exactly when \(\mu\ge\tau=(c_H-g_L)/(g_H-g_L)\). In the benchmark (Sections 3.4 and 8.2), \(c_H-g_L=(2rc_H-\ell^2+p^2)/(2r)\) and \(g_H-g_L=(2rh-r^2-\ell^2)/(2r)\), so \[\tau(r)=\frac{48r-3}{4(20r-r^2-1)},\qquad \tau(r_1)=\frac{141}{200}.\]

W6. Entry under full orders.

Only a purchase recruits. A purchase has probability \(\lambda+(1-\lambda)\beta=b+\lambda\) in class \(H\) and \(b\) in class \(L\). So \[e_H=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\lambda),\qquad e_L=\rho+(1-\rho)b,\qquad \mathsf{E}=\rho+(1-\rho)(b+\pi\lambda),\] which is 0.522757 at the benchmark (Section 8.6.1).

W7. The exact band of \(u\).

At \(\pi=1/2\), \(M=(1+u)/(2+u)\) and \(\kappa=1/(2+u)\).

  • (H1), right side: \(M>141/200\iff200(1+u)>141(2+u)\iff59u>82\).

  • (H3): \(M<4245/5804\iff5804(1+u)<4245(2+u)\iff1559u<2686\).

  • (H2), right side: \(1/50<1/[6(2+u)]\iff u<19/3\), which the band already implies.

So (H1)-(H3) hold exactly when \(82/59<u<2686/1559\) (Section 8.6.3).

Panel note: if you have time for one

Do W4. It is the step that fixes the orders before any candidate is known, so it carries the uniqueness of Proposition 2.

Likely questions, with suggested answers

A. Modelling choices

Q1. Why one order slot?

“It is the simplest market in which no order reveals the class. The investor fills the slot with probability \(\lambda\); otherwise a liquidity trader fills it, in either direction, as in Dow and Gorton (1997). So every order has positive probability under every profile, and no off-path belief enters. And no order has a likelihood ratio above \(1+u\), so every posterior lies in \([m,M]\) under every strategy, mixed or wrong-signed. The uniqueness proofs rest on these two facts.” (Sections 4.1 and 4.2.)

Q2. What replaces Laplace noise, and what is lost?

“The liquidity trader replaces additive noise. The bound on the likelihood ratio now comes from the liquidity mass in each direction, not from a noise density. With \(1+u=\mathrm{e}^{2/s}\) the two set-ups have the same \(m\) and \(M\); the benchmark \(1+u=\mathrm{e}\) is the Laplace scale \(2\).” The slot gains exact algebra: three payoffs, no derivative in the order size, and the whole equilibrium set along the path. It loses four things. The investor does not choose a size. Full orders give only three posteriors, so frozen entry is flat up to \(r_C\) and then drops; the Laplace continuum of posteriors let frozen entry fall as the threshold rose (Section 7.2). Class \(H\) reaches the top posterior with probability \(b+\lambda\), not \(1/2\), so equal bounds do not give equal entry. The logistic-noise extension has no counterpart. (Section 11.5.)

Q3. Why \(\beta\le1/3\)?

“It is the condition \(b_0\ge b\): a liquidity abstention is at least as likely as a liquidity order in each direction. Then the zero order obeys the same bounds as a trade, and a price that pools the zero order keeps its belief in \([m,M]\). Without it, a one-sided profile makes zero the most informative order: with \(\lambda=6/17\), \(\beta=2/5\), and a profile in which class \(H\) abstains and class \(L\) sells, \(\mu(0)-M=165/1924>0\). The restriction serves the statements that cover every profile, such as the universal ceiling. Propositions 2 and 3 force full orders first, so they hold for every \(\beta<1/2\).” The benchmark lies 0.038678 inside it. (Sections 4.3 and 4.5.)

Q4. Why a trading cost, and why a zero preparation cost with probability \(\rho\)?

“Without \(k\), any positive residual advantage makes a trade pay, so no informed trade is never an equilibrium. The cost is execution, carry or the cost to borrow for a short sale, not price impact. The zero-cost challenger gives the entry floor, which does three jobs: with \(\Delta_T>0\) it lets the price reveal the posterior, it gives every correctly signed order at least \(\rho\kappa\Delta_T\), and it means that information can only add entry. The atoms are not needed: Proposition A.4 allows any cost law with a low component below \(B_1(m)\) and a high component strictly inside the high-cost window.” (Sections 5.6, 4.6 and 11.1.)

Q5. Why would a buyer learn about its own value from a stock price?

“Value depends on facts about the target: its customers, its technology, its demand. A specialist investor can know these before diligence; the buyer knows its own integration plans. These are different facts about one match. In the example \(S(B)\) of Proposition A.7, the buyer’s signal is the more accurate one, \(d=0.75\) against \(a_T=0.70\); entry rises from 0.850000 to 0.879797, and the rise needs both signals.” (Section 11.4.)

Q6. Why a cash second-price auction, and why do the bidders not trade?

“Truthful bids are weakly dominant, so no payoff depends on a conjecture about bid shading. The payment rule matters: under Nash bargaining with seller weight \(\omega\), competition moves the spread with the sign of \(1-2\omega\), and the second-price auction is \(\omega=0\). The bidders do not trade, and the sale binds all shares; this keeps toeholds and free riding out and isolates the information channel.” (Sections 3.1 and 12.3.)

B. The mechanism

Q7. In one sentence, why does a stronger incumbent raise the spread?

“A high-class challenger must outbid the incumbent and pays \(R\), but against a low-class challenger the incumbent wins and pays only \(\ell\); so the gap is \((R-\ell)_+\), and it grows when \(R\) tends to be larger.” (Section 3.2.)

Q8. A stronger incumbent also lowers the challenger’s profit. Where is that in the result?

“It is the deterrence effect. It raises the preparation threshold as the incumbent strengthens, and it ends recruitment above \(r_C\). With the information fixed, it is the only effect. The reversal comes from the change of the price from uninformative to informative between \(r_0\) and \(r_1\).” (Sections 3.4 and 7.)

Q9. Market makers anticipate entry. Why is anything left for the investor?

“Rational prices remove the no-entry proceeds \(t_0\) and the common entry gain \(e(q)w_L\). They cannot remove the class component, because a liquidity order in the same direction is always possible. The residual is \(e(q)\Delta_T[1-\mu(q)]\), and \(1-\mu(1)\ge1-M>0\).” (Section 5.2.)

Q10. Why does the price reveal the posterior, when entry depends on the price?

“Two orders with one price face the same challenger, so they have the same entry \(\bar e\ge\rho\). The price formula gives \(0=\bar e\Delta_T[\mu(x)-\mu(x')]\), and \(\bar e\Delta_T>0\) forces equal posteriors. You never solve the circle. Recovery needs \(\Delta_T>0\); prices that rise strictly with the posterior also need \(g_H>g_L\).” (Section 4.7.)

Q11. Why is entry non-monotone in incumbent strength?

“At \(r_0\) the spread is too small to pay for a trade, so the price carries no information and entry is \(\rho\). At \(r_1\) trade is informative, and the top posterior 0.731059 clears \(\tau(r_1)=0.705000\). At \(r_2\) the price stays informative, but \(\tau(r_2)=0.731392\) lies above the top posterior, so entry returns to \(\rho\).” (Section 6.6.)

Q12. Why does research comove with entry?

“A wider spread raises the return to a purchase that recruits, \(\Delta_Tm_\lambda-k\). Interior research solves \(2a\lambda=\Delta_Tm_\lambda-k\), and the implicit-function theorem gives \(\lambda_R'>0\). A purchase has probability \(\beta+(1/2-\beta)\lambda\): research adds informed purchases in class \(H\) and removes liquidity orders in both classes, and the first effect is larger because \(\beta<1/2\).” From 1.55 to 1.65, research rises from 0.237901 to 0.316683 and entry from 0.507630 to 0.519763. (Section 10.7.)

Q13. Is the effect large, or a knife-edge?

“The set of primitives is open and nonempty, and Proposition A.5 constructs it for every ranked pair of classes with different means, at every prior. At the benchmark, entry rises by 0.272757, a factor of about 2.09, and high-class ownership rises from 0.125000 to 0.324401.” The smallest margin belongs to the ceiling at \(r_2\); Section 14.3 explains why it says nothing about the rise.

C. Proof technique

Q14. What exactly is unique, and what is not?

“In each economy of Proposition 2, the orders, posteriors, prices, beliefs and on-path entry are unique under truthful bids. The argument allows mixed and wrong-signed orders, and every comparison is strict, so no tie rule matters. Uniqueness fails in three places: no trade and an informative equilibrium coexist on \([r_A,r_N]\); zero research and the research branch coexist at the intermediate strengths; and at boundary cases, such as \(u=82/59\) or \(r=r_C\), rule CM allows a range of entry.” (Sections 6.9, 9.5 and 10.7.)

Q15. What does coexistence mean for comparative statics?

“Proposition 2 compares three economies with unique outcomes; it is not a derivative and not a path. On \([r_A,r_N]\) two equilibria exist at one strength, and the model does not select one, so a statement along the path must name its branch. On the no-trade branch entry is \(\rho\); on the informative branch it rises strictly on \([r_A,r_F]\) and then stays at 0.522757 up to \(r_C\). The headline needs no selection: \(r_0\) lies below \(r_A\), and \(r_1\) lies above \(r_J\), where only full orders remain. In data, coexistence predicts two clusters of processes at one public strength.” (Sections 9.5 and 13.6.)

Q16. How is Proposition 3 proved, and why is there no derivative of a deviation?

“By algebra, except two monotonicity facts: \(J\) rises on the path, and \(\Delta_T(1-\tau)^2\) rises on \([r_0,2]\). Each is the sign of an integer polynomial on a rational interval, and an exact Sturm count settles it. Bisection with exact signs at rational points brackets \(r_A\), \(r_J\) and \(r_*\), and every quantity that depends on \(\mathrm{e}\) uses rational bounds on \(\mathrm{e}\). A deviation by the investor compares three numbers at the candidate’s prices and entry: zero, \(K_j-k\), and at most \(-k\). Nothing is differentiated in the order size.” (Sections 9.9 and 5.3.)

Q17. What does the fixed-information theorem require, and what do the examples and controls show?

“Fix the joint law of the class and the challenger’s signal \(S\), the class value laws and the cost law. In each economy the law of the two values given \((j,S)\) must be \(G_j\otimes F_i\), and the cost must be independent of the class, both values and \(S\). Then a stronger incumbent cannot raise entry. Each part of the product law matters. In Example A the signal reports the incumbent’s component in one economy only, and entry rises from \(5/8\) to \(1\) although the class posterior stays at \(1/2\). In Example B the signal tells the challenger its value within the class, and entry rises from \(1/4\) to \(5/8\). The controls apply the theorem: frozen full orders keep entry at 0.522757 at \(r_0\) and \(r_1\), and a hidden price keeps it at 0.250000. The frozen profile at \(r_0\) is a control, not an equilibrium: both orders lose there.” (Sections 7.1, 7.2 and 7.3.)

D. Welfare and sale terms

Q18. Who gains from price access?

“At \(r_1\) the orders are full with and without access, so trading costs are the same. Access adds high-cost preparation after a purchase. Expected proceeds rise by 0.257948 and net surplus by 0.081960. A shareholder who holds through the sale receives \(V_T\), so shareholders receive the proceeds gain; the investor’s gross profit, 0.037665, is a transfer from liquidity traders.” Name the domain: where the high cost pays at the prior, as at \(c_H=12/5\), access removes preparation after a sale, and proceeds change by -0.173917. (Sections 12.1 and 12.2.)

Q19. Should the seller set a reserve to make the price informative?

“A reserve that excludes the low class widens the spread, so trade is informative against both incumbents. At \(p=101/100\) the top posterior recruits at both strengths, and entry is flat at 0.522757. At \(p=31/20\) strict deterrence returns. The seller’s optimal terms with the whole trading continuation are the next theoretical step.” (Section 12.4.)

E. Empirics

Q20. How would you test this, when incumbent strength is endogenous?

“Take publicly visible sale processes with one prepared bidder. The outcome is the start of substantive diligence by a further buyer. Measure strength only from information that is public before that decision, and date the price before it, because target returns before a bid partly reflect anticipation (Schwert 1996). Where the price cannot reach the buyer in time, a stronger incumbent can only deter. The order is the first prespecified public order opportunity, such as the opening call auction, and an opportunity with no order stays in the sample as a zero. A second test needs no auction values: the purchase impact exceeds the sale impact exactly when a purchase recruits. At \(r_1\) the entry component is 90.2% of the spread.” (Sections 13.2 and 13.7.)

Where a panel will push

A referee will press six points. Use one formula for each: “This is a deliberate choice. Here is what it buys. Here is where the paper relaxes it, or why the result does not depend on it.”

P1. “The investor knows the class exactly, and the buyer knows nothing.”

It buys one public belief, so the mechanism is a short chain of inequalities. Relaxed: Proposition A.7 gives both sides private signals about different facts; in \(S(B)\) the buyer’s signal is the more accurate one, and the rise needs both signals. At the benchmark, with \((a_T,d)=(0.94,0.505)\), entry rises from 0.250000 to 0.386933. Say what fails: at the benchmark costs and strengths the sufficient conditions cannot hold with \(d\ge a_T\), so \(S(B)\) has its own costs and strengths. Section 5 of the paper makes the investor’s information the result of costly research (Sections 11.4 and 10).

P2. “The windows of (H1)-(H3) are narrow at the benchmark.”

The band is \(82/59<u<2686/1559\); the benchmark \(u=1.718282\) lies 0.004617 below its top, and the smallest margin is \(c_H-B_{r_2}(M)=0.002689\). Answer in four steps.

  1. The small margins belong to (H3) and measure where \(r_2\) sits: \(r_2-r_C=0.007341\). The ceiling holds on all of \((r_C,h)\), and its margin is 0.149164 at \(r=4\).

  2. The rise uses only (H1) and (H2): \(82/59<u<19/3\), that is \(141/200<M<22/25\), or presence between 0.290539 and 0.651100 at \(\beta=0.294655\).

  3. The trading-cost window runs from 0.016667 to \(1/[6(1+\mathrm{e})]=0.044824\), so its upper end is about 2.69 times its lower end.

  4. Proposition A.5 constructs an open set for every ranked pair of classes with different means, at every prior.

So the rise is robust; the return to \(\rho\) is close to its boundary because \(r_2\) sits just above \(r_C\) (Sections 6.7 and 6.9).

P3. “What happens between the economies, and which equilibrium is selected?”

It buys a headline in which each economy has one outcome, so no selection drives it. Relaxed: Proposition 3 lists the whole equilibrium set at every strength on the path, for every slot in the band; Table A.3 shows what changes under the other tie rules; and Corollary A.2 covers every prior. The model does not select on \([r_A,r_N]\); the empirical chapter turns this into two clusters. Say plainly what remains open: with research, the set on \((r_N,r_U)\) (Sections 9.5, 9.8 and 10.8).

P4. “The payment-rule result covers only the payment stage.”

It buys the one object that the mechanism takes from the sale rule: how competition moves the spread. Scope: Proposition A.9 is a payment-stage result; the trading and preparation equilibrium under bargaining is not solved. What it shows: for \(\omega<1/2\) the spread still rises with strength, at \(\omega=1/2\) it is \((h-\ell)/2\) for every incumbent, and above \(1/2\) competition narrows it (Section 12.3).

P5. “The seller would choose its terms.”

It buys a clean separation of the information channel from the design problem. Relaxed: Proposition A.10 changes the reserve: exclusion makes the price informative at both strengths and can flatten entry, and strict deterrence holds for reserves between about 1.325270 and 1.792723. Open: the seller-optimal reserve, and whether the seller commits before trade or revises after the price; the conclusion of the paper names this the next step (Section 12.4).

P6. “What is not modelled?”

Toeholds, free riding and manipulation: the bidders do not trade, and the sale binds all shares, so the incumbent cannot move the price and the investor earns a pure trading residual. Order sizes and repeated trade: one unit opportunity keeps every order on path and gives exact bounds (Section 11.5). The investor’s position size: the scale of a unit holds the experiment fixed (Section 12.2). The zero-cost challenger prepares by convention, even at zero rent. Payment is in cash. The empirical results are predictions with a measurement design, not estimates (Section 13).

Terms to use exactly

Term Precise meaning
Entry \(\mathsf{E}\) The probability that the challenger prepares. Not the sale probability, and not the probability that two admissible bidders meet; these differ once a reserve can exclude.
Preparation The payment of the cost (diligence, financing, approvals) that reveals the value and permits a bid.
Stronger incumbent A first-order stochastic strengthening of the public law of the prepared bidder’s value; a larger \(r\) in the benchmark. Not more bidders.
Target proceeds What the auction pays per target share, \(V_T\), with means \(t_0,t_H,t_L\). Not the interim price.
Gross profit \(B(\mu)=g_L+\mu(g_H-g_L)\): the expected auction rent before the preparation cost.
Target-payoff spread \(\Delta_T=t_H-t_L\). Not the merger value, not the investor’s profit, and not the price spread \(S\) of Section 13.2.
Top, bottom posterior \(M(\pi)\), \(m(\pi)\): bounds on the posterior after every order under every profile; full orders reach them.
Residual advantage What prices leave to the investor: \(e(q)\Delta_T[1-\mu(q)]\) in class \(H\), \(e(q)\Delta_T\mu(q)\) in class \(L\).
The order slot One public order opportunity, filled by the investor with probability \(\lambda\) and by a liquidity trader otherwise. Not additive flow.
